# Large Language Models Interview Primer — 328 Questions

How large language models actually work, built up from scratch — the transformer/LLM internals, not the API. A Machine Learning companion and the mechanics counterpart to the AI Engineering primer (which is the applied product layer): this one explains what computes inside the model. Karpathy "let's build GPT" / Illustrated-Transformer level, aimed at an engineer who can use an LLM but wants to understand tokenization → embeddings → attention → transformer blocks → the forward pass → sampling → autoregressive generation → training → post-training.

Covers LLM foundations & the big picture, tokenization, embeddings, positional encodings, attention (Q/K/V), self/cross attention & causal masking, multi-head attention, the transformer block, feed-forward networks, normalization & residuals, the full stacked architecture, the forward pass, sampling & decoding, autoregressive generation & the KV cache, pretraining, training mechanics (backprop & optimization), scaling laws, post-training (SFT, then RLHF/DPO & alignment), modern efficiency architecture (FlashAttention, GQA/MQA, MoE), and an interview/reasoning capstone.

Every answer is intuition-first with the real mechanism, in plain ASCII maths (the reader renders no LaTeX — e.g. `softmax(Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k)) @ V`) and nanoGPT-style Python/pseudocode. Warm-up ("what is a token", "what is next-token prediction") to senior ("derive scaled dot-product attention and why sqrt(d_k)", "walk a token from text to logits with shapes", "explain RLHF vs DPO", "why is attention O(n^2) and what fixes it"), and the classic flavour questions — why "strawberry" trips up letter counting, why the same prompt varies, why a token can't see the future, what actually gets updated during training.

## LLM Foundations & the Big Picture

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

This topic sets up the single idea the rest of the primer decodes: a large language model is, at its core, a **next-token predictor**. Given a sequence of previous tokens, it outputs a probability distribution over what the next token should be — `p(next | previous)` — and text is generated by sampling that distribution over and over. Everything else (tokenization, embeddings, attention, transformer blocks, the forward pass, sampling, training, alignment) is machinery in service of computing that one conditional distribution well. The 16 questions here cover: what an LLM fundamentally *is* and *is not*; the transformer revolution (Vaswani et al., 2017) and why it replaced RNNs; the three architecture families — **encoder-only (BERT)**, **encoder-decoder (T5)**, and **decoder-only (GPT)** — and why modern generative LLMs are decoder-only; the "everything is matrices + attention + next-token prediction" mental model; the GPT lineage; and how this *internals* view differs from the *applied* API/product view covered in the sister AI Engineering primer.

**Mental model**

Picture a function with billions of numbers (weights) inside it. You feed in a sequence of tokens (integers) and it emits a vector of scores, one per vocabulary entry, saying how likely each possible next token is. That is the whole model. To generate text you run this function, pick a next token from its output distribution, append that token to the input, and run again — an **autoregressive loop**. Training is nothing more exotic than adjusting those billions of weights so the predicted distribution puts high probability on the token that *actually* came next in real text. Compress "predict the next token over trillions of tokens of the internet" hard enough and the model is forced to learn grammar, facts, translation, arithmetic, code, and reasoning as *side effects* — because all of those help it predict better. There is no separate "reasoning module." It is one differentiable function trained on one objective, sampled in a loop.

**Key terms**

- **Next-token prediction** — the core task: output `p(next token | all previous tokens)` over the vocabulary.
- **Autoregressive** — generates one token at a time, feeding each output back as input for the next step.
- **Transformer** — the 2017 architecture built on attention; the backbone of all modern LLMs.
- **Decoder-only** — the GPT family; causal (left-to-right) attention, built for generation.
- **Encoder-only** — BERT-style; bidirectional attention, built for understanding/classification, not generation.
- **Encoder-decoder** — T5/original-transformer style; an encoder reads the input, a decoder generates output. Good for seq2seq (translation, summarization).
- **Logits** — the raw unnormalized scores the model outputs, one per vocab token, before softmax.
- **Parameters / weights** — the learned numbers (mostly in matrices) that define the function.
- **Base model** — a pretrained next-token predictor; a text completer, not yet a helpful assistant.
- **Context window** — the maximum number of tokens the model can attend over at once.

**Why interviewers ask this**

This is the framing question — get it wrong and every later answer is built on sand. A junior answer says "it's an AI that understands language" or reaches for the chatbot/product view. A senior answer says "it's a conditional probability model over token sequences, `p(next | previous)`, sampled autoregressively" and can immediately explain what that *rules out*: the model has no memory beyond its context, no ground-truth database, no separate planning stage — hallucination and the "strawberry" letter-counting failure fall directly out of the objective and the tokenization. Interviewers also probe whether you know *why* decoder-only won: a candidate who can contrast BERT/T5/GPT and explain that generation only needs a causal decoder signals they understand the design space, not just the one model they've used.

**Common confusions**

- "The model looks things up in a database." No — the knowledge is baked into the weights during training; there is no retrieval unless you bolt on RAG (an applied-layer choice, see AI Engineering).
- "GPT uses the encoder-decoder transformer from the 2017 paper." No — GPT is **decoder-only**; it dropped the encoder and cross-attention entirely.
- "It predicts words." It predicts **tokens** (subword units), which is why letter- and character-level tasks trip it up (see the Tokenization topic).
- "Bigger context = more memory across sessions." The context window is per-request working memory; nothing persists between calls unless you re-feed it.
- "It reasons, then writes." It emits one token at a time; any apparent reasoning is produced token-by-token in the same forward loop (this is why chain-of-thought prompting helps — it gives the model tokens to 'think' in).

**What follows from this topic**

Everything. **Tokenization** turns the text into the integer sequence this predictor consumes. **Embeddings** turn those integers into vectors. Attention and the transformer block (later topics) are how `p(next | previous)` is actually computed. Sampling and autoregressive generation are how the predictor becomes a text generator. Pretraining is how the weights get set. Keep returning to the one-line definition — `p(next | previous)`, sampled in a loop — and the rest of the primer is just filling in *how each piece is computed*. For the applied counterpart (prompting, RAG, tools, cost), see the AI Engineering primer; this primer owns the internals.

### Q1. In one sentence, what fundamentally is a large language model?

A large language model is a **next-token predictor**: a big parameterized function that, given a sequence of previous tokens, outputs a probability distribution over the vocabulary for the next token — `p(next token | all previous tokens)` — and generates text by sampling that distribution repeatedly, feeding each new token back in.

That is the entire object. It is trained on huge amounts of text by adjusting its weights so that the probability it assigns to the token that *actually* followed is as high as possible. There is no separate knowledge base, planner, or reasoning engine — grammar, facts, and skills are all emergent consequences of getting good at that one prediction task.

### Q2. Walk me through what happens, at a high level, when I type a prompt and get a response.

```text
prompt text
  -> tokenizer:      "the cat sat" -> [464, 3797, 3332]   (integer token IDs)
  -> embeddings:     each ID -> a d_model vector           (batch, seq, d_model)
  -> N transformer blocks:  attention + FFN, shape preserved
  -> final norm + LM head:  -> logits (seq, vocab)
  -> take LAST position's logits, softmax -> p(next token)
  -> sample a token, append it to the input
  -> repeat the whole loop until an end token or length limit
```

The key points: text becomes integers, integers become vectors, the stack of transformer blocks mixes information across positions, and the final layer produces one score per vocabulary token. For generation you only care about the **last position's** logits — that is the prediction for "what comes next." Sampling one token and feeding it back is the autoregressive loop. Every later topic zooms into one of these arrows.

### Q3. Why did the transformer (2017) replace RNNs and LSTMs?

RNNs process a sequence **one step at a time**, carrying a hidden state forward. Two fatal problems: (1) they are inherently **sequential** — you can't compute step t until step t-1 is done, so they don't parallelize across the sequence on GPUs; and (2) **long-range dependencies** degrade — information from far back has to survive many recurrent steps, and gradients vanish/explode over long distances even with LSTM gating.

The transformer's attention mechanism lets **every position look directly at every other position in one operation**, so (1) the whole sequence is processed in parallel during training, and (2) the path length between any two tokens is O(1) — no information has to be shuffled through dozens of intermediate steps. That combination — parallelizable training plus direct long-range access — is what let models scale to billions of parameters and trillions of tokens. The title of the paper, "Attention Is All You Need," is the thesis: drop recurrence entirely, keep only attention.

### Q4. Contrast encoder-only, encoder-decoder, and decoder-only architectures.

| | Encoder-only | Encoder-decoder | Decoder-only |
|---|---|---|---|
| Example | BERT | T5, original 2017 transformer | GPT, Llama, Claude |
| Attention | Bidirectional | Bidir encoder + causal decoder + cross-attention | Causal (left-to-right) only |
| Sees | Both directions | Encoder: both; decoder: past only | Past tokens only |
| Trained for | Masked-token / understanding | Sequence-to-sequence (input -> output) | Next-token prediction |
| Best at | Classification, embeddings, NER | Translation, summarization | Open-ended generation, chat |
| Can generate? | No (not autoregressive) | Yes | Yes |

**Encoder-only (BERT)** sees the whole input at once (bidirectional) and is trained to fill in masked tokens — great for producing representations for classification, but it can't generate text autoregressively. **Encoder-decoder (T5)** has an encoder that reads the full input and a decoder that generates output while cross-attending to the encoder — natural for tasks with a distinct input and output (translate this sentence). **Decoder-only (GPT)** has just the causal decoder stack; input and output are one continuous token stream.

### Q5. Why are modern generative LLMs decoder-only rather than encoder-decoder?

Because for open-ended generation you don't need the encoder. In a decoder-only model, the "input" (prompt) and "output" (completion) are just one token stream; the prompt tokens are simply the first tokens the model conditions on. Everything reduces to one uniform objective — next-token prediction over the whole sequence — with one simple attention pattern (causal masking).

The encoder-decoder split makes sense when input and output are genuinely different modalities or languages with a clean boundary (translate French -> English). But it adds cross-attention, two separate stacks, and a rigid input/output boundary. Decoder-only is **simpler, more uniform, and scales better**: one stack, one objective, one attention type. It also unifies every task — Q&A, summarization, translation, code — as "continue this text," so a single model does everything with in-context prompting. That simplicity-plus-generality is why GPT-style decoder-only architectures dominate.

### Q6. What does "the model is just matrices and attention" actually mean?

It means there is nothing else. A transformer is a stack of the same operations repeated: matrix multiplications (linear projections), the attention operation (itself dot products = matrix multiplies plus a softmax), elementwise nonlinearities, normalization, and residual additions. The learned "knowledge" lives entirely in the **weight matrices** — the embedding matrix, the query/key/value/output projections in each attention layer, and the feed-forward weight matrices.

```python
# the whole forward pass, schematically
x = embedding[token_ids] + positional        # (batch, seq, d_model)
for block in blocks:
    x = x + attention(norm(x))               # mix across positions
    x = x + feed_forward(norm(x))            # process each position
logits = x @ unembedding                     # (batch, seq, vocab)
```

No if-statements about grammar, no symbolic rules, no lookup tables. When people say a model "knows" that Paris is the capital of France, that fact is encoded as particular values inside these matrices, learned by gradient descent. Understanding the model deeply means understanding what each matrix does — which is exactly what the attention and transformer-block topics build up.

### Q7. What is the GPT lineage, and what changed across it?

GPT (Generative Pretrained Transformer) is a family of decoder-only models. The through-line is unchanged: a decoder-only transformer trained on next-token prediction. What changed is mostly **scale plus post-training**:

- **GPT-1 (2018)** — showed generative pretraining + task fine-tuning works.
- **GPT-2 (2019)** — much bigger, demonstrated surprisingly good zero-shot generation from scale alone.
- **GPT-3 (2020)** — ~175B params; **in-context learning** emerged — you could 'program' it with examples in the prompt, no weight updates.
- **InstructGPT / ChatGPT (2022)** — added **post-training** (SFT then RLHF) to turn a base completer into a helpful, instruction-following assistant. This, not raw scale, is what made LLMs usable products.
- **GPT-4 and beyond** — larger, multimodal, longer context, better reasoning; architectural refinements (e.g. Mixture-of-Experts) plus much heavier post-training.

The senior insight: the *architecture* barely changed — it's still a decoder-only transformer predicting the next token. The leaps came from **scale (params + data + compute)** and, critically, **post-training alignment**, which the later topics in this primer cover.

### Q8. How is this "internals" view different from the API/product view of an LLM?

The **product/API view** (the AI Engineering primer's domain) treats the model as a black box you call over HTTP: you send a prompt, set `temperature` and `max_tokens`, maybe attach tools or retrieved documents (RAG), and get text back. It's about *using* the model well — prompting, context management, cost, latency, evals.

The **internals view** (this primer) is about what computes *inside* that box: how text becomes tokens, tokens become vectors, attention mixes them, blocks stack, logits come out, and how sampling and training work. Both matter, but they answer different questions. "Why did lowering temperature make output more deterministic?" is applied; "what does temperature do to the softmax over logits?" is internal — and knowing the internal reason makes you better at the applied choice. Throughout this primer, when a topic touches the applied layer (sampling params, context window, tokens as cost), we explain the *internal mechanism* and defer the *applied usage* to the AI Engineering primer.

### Q9. Where does "knowledge" live in an LLM, and what does that imply about hallucination?

Knowledge lives in the **weights** — the numeric values of the matrices, set during pretraining. There is no database, no index, no source-of-truth the model consults at inference time. When it "recalls" a fact, it is really computing a next-token distribution that, thanks to training, happens to favor the correct continuation.

This directly explains **hallucination**: the model is optimized to produce *plausible* next tokens, not *true* ones. It has no built-in notion of "I don't know" — it always outputs a distribution, and will confidently continue even when the true answer wasn't well-represented in training. Facts that were rare or absent in the training data are exactly where it fabricates. This is why the applied layer bolts on **retrieval (RAG)** to inject ground-truth text into the context — an external fix for an intrinsic property of a next-token predictor. The mechanism is internal; the mitigation is applied.

### Q10. If an LLM is "just predicting the next token," how can it possibly reason or do math?

Because next-token prediction over a large, diverse corpus is an extraordinarily demanding objective. To predict the token after "The proof follows by induction; the base case is" well, the model must have internalized structure about proofs. To continue "17 * 24 =" correctly, it needs to have learned arithmetic patterns. Compressing the internet into a next-token predictor forces the model to build reusable internal circuits — for syntax, coreference, arithmetic, translation — because they all reduce prediction error.

But it's genuinely bounded by the token-by-token, single-forward-pass nature: the model does a fixed amount of computation per token. That's why **chain-of-thought** helps — letting the model emit intermediate "thinking" tokens gives it more forward passes and a scratchpad to condition on, rather than forcing the whole computation into one step. And it's why arithmetic on long numbers is unreliable: partly the fixed compute budget, partly tokenization (numbers split awkwardly, see the Tokenization topic). Reasoning is real but emergent and constrained — not a separate symbolic engine.

### Q11. What is a base model and why isn't it the same as ChatGPT?

A **base model** is the raw output of pretraining: a pure next-token predictor trained on trillions of tokens of web text, code, and books. It is astonishingly good at *continuing* text but has no notion of being a helpful assistant. Prompt a base model with a question and it might continue with *more questions* (because that's how FAQ pages look in training data) rather than answering.

Turning a base model into an assistant like ChatGPT requires **post-training**: **supervised fine-tuning (SFT)** on curated (instruction, response) demonstrations, then **preference optimization (RLHF or DPO)** to make it helpful, honest, and harmless. That's what teaches it the chat format, to answer directly, to refuse harmful requests, and to follow instructions. The architecture and objective are identical — still a decoder-only transformer doing next-token prediction — but the *data* it was tuned on changes its behavior completely. This base-vs-chat distinction is developed fully in the post-training topics; flag it early because it's a favorite interview trap.

### Q12. What is the context window, and what is the internal reason it's limited?

The **context window** is the maximum number of tokens the model can attend over in a single forward pass — the prompt plus the generated-so-far. Internally it's limited for two reasons. First, **attention is O(n^2)** in sequence length: every token attends to every other, so doubling the context quadruples the attention compute and the size of the attention matrix. Second, **positional encoding** and training only cover positions the model was trained on; asking it to attend over far more positions than it saw in training degrades quality (models extrapolate poorly beyond their trained length without special techniques like RoPE scaling).

So the window isn't an arbitrary product cap — it's set by the quadratic cost of attention and the range of positions the model was trained to handle. Much of modern LLM efficiency work (FlashAttention, sliding-window attention, RoPE interpolation) is precisely about pushing this limit out. The applied consequence — "fit your prompt in the window, it costs per token" — is the AI Engineering primer's concern; the internal *why* is attention's cost and positional range.

### Q13. Why is "in-context learning" surprising, and is the model actually learning?

**In-context learning** is the ability to give a model a few examples in the prompt ("apple -> fruit, carrot -> vegetable, banana -> ?") and have it infer the pattern and continue correctly — with **no weight updates**. It emerged at scale (notably GPT-3) and is surprising because "learning" normally means changing parameters via gradient descent, yet here the model adapts purely from what's in its context.

Is it really learning? Not in the weight-update sense — the parameters are frozen. What's happening is that the pretrained model has internalized enough patterns that the forward pass itself can pick up on the regularity in the prompt and condition its next-token distribution on it. One view is that the attention mechanism implements a kind of implicit, on-the-fly pattern-matching over the examples in context. The practical upshot (few-shot prompting) is an applied-layer technique; the internal fact is that everything happens inside one frozen forward pass over the context — no training occurs at inference.

### Q14. Is an LLM deterministic? Why might the same prompt give different answers?

The *model* — the forward pass from tokens to logits — is deterministic: same inputs, same weights, same logits. The **variability comes from sampling**. Generation doesn't always pick the single highest-scoring token; it *samples* from the probability distribution (controlled by temperature, top-k, top-p). Different random draws produce different tokens, and one different token early on changes everything that follows. That's the primary reason the same prompt yields different answers.

Even with sampling disabled (temperature 0, greedy argmax), you can see small variation in practice due to **floating-point non-associativity**, GPU batching, and, in Mixture-of-Experts models, routing that depends on the batch. So "deterministic" has two layers: the math is deterministic given fixed inputs and execution, but the sampling step is deliberately stochastic, and hardware/implementation details add tiny non-determinism. The sampling topic covers the mechanism; the takeaway here is that randomness is injected *after* the model, at the sampling step, not inside the prediction itself.

### Q15. What are the biggest misconceptions engineers have about how LLMs work?

- **"It understands like a human / has a world model it queries."** It computes a next-token distribution; any 'understanding' is emergent structure in the weights, not symbolic reasoning over facts.
- **"It looks up answers."** No retrieval unless you add RAG. Knowledge is compressed into weights, which is why it hallucinates on the long tail.
- **"It predicts words."** It predicts **tokens** — subword units — hence character-level failures like counting letters.
- **"Bigger context means it remembers me between chats."** The window is per-request; nothing persists unless re-fed.
- **"Temperature 0 makes it fully deterministic."** Mostly, but floating-point and batching break perfect determinism.
- **"GPT is the 2017 encoder-decoder transformer."** It's decoder-only; the encoder was dropped.
- **"It reasons first, then writes."** It emits token by token in one loop; chain-of-thought works precisely because thinking *is* the emitted tokens.

Naming these crisply signals you understand the mechanism, not the marketing.

### Q16. If you had to implement a toy GPT, what are the pieces and in what order?

```text
1. Tokenizer          text <-> integer token IDs (train BPE, or reuse one)
2. Embedding matrix   (vocab, d_model): token ID -> vector
3. Positional info    add position (learned or RoPE) so order matters
4. Transformer block  (repeat N times):
      a. causal multi-head self-attention (with -inf mask)
      b. position-wise feed-forward network (MLP)
      c. residual connections + layer norm around each
5. Final layer norm
6. LM head            (d_model, vocab), often tied to the embedding -> logits
7. Loss / training    cross-entropy on next-token, backprop, AdamW
8. Sampling           softmax(logits/temp), top-k/top-p, autoregressive loop
```

Each item is a later topic in this primer. The mental checklist an interviewer wants: you know the model is embeddings + a stack of identical attention-and-FFN blocks + an unembedding head, trained by cross-entropy next-token prediction and run in an autoregressive sampling loop. If you can name these pieces and say what shape each produces, you've shown you could actually build a nanoGPT — which is the bar this primer is aiming you at.

## Tokenization

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

Before a language model can do anything, text has to become numbers. **Tokenization** is the step that maps a string to a sequence of integer **token IDs** using a fixed, learned **subword vocabulary** — and maps them back on the way out. This topic covers how that vocabulary is built (**Byte-Pair Encoding**, **WordPiece**, **SentencePiece**, **byte-level BPE**), why subword tokenization beats both word-level and character-level schemes, typical **vocabulary sizes** (~30k to 130k+), the crucial fact that **tokens are not words** (" apple", "apple", and "App" are three different tokens), special tokens, and the practical pitfalls — the famous **"how many r's in strawberry"** letter-counting failure, plus whitespace, numbers, code, and multilingual quirks. It also covers why tokens are the **unit of cost and context**. The 16 questions here range from "what is a token" to "trace exactly why the model can't count letters" and "compare BPE vs WordPiece vs SentencePiece."

**Mental model**

Think of tokenization as a fixed dictionary of subword chunks, learned once before training and then frozen. Common words become a single token; rarer words get split into a few subword pieces; truly novel strings fall back to bytes. The model **never sees characters or raw text** — it only ever sees the integer IDs. That single fact is the source of a whole class of behaviors: because the token for "strawberry" is one opaque integer (or a couple of chunks), the letters s-t-r-a-w-b-e-r-r-y are simply not present as separate inputs, so counting them is like being asked to count the letters in a word you can only hear as a single sound. Tokenization sits *outside* the neural network — it's a deterministic preprocessing/postprocessing step — but it shapes everything: what the model can perceive, how long a prompt is, and how much it costs.

**Key terms**

- **Token** — the atomic unit the model consumes; usually a subword chunk, mapped to an integer ID.
- **Vocabulary** — the fixed set of all tokens (e.g. 32k–128k entries); an integer <-> token-string table.
- **BPE (Byte-Pair Encoding)** — builds the vocab by repeatedly merging the most frequent adjacent pair of symbols.
- **WordPiece** — BERT's scheme; merges by likelihood gain rather than raw frequency; marks continuations with `##`.
- **SentencePiece** — treats text as a raw byte/character stream (spaces included), language-agnostic; no pre-tokenization needed.
- **Byte-level BPE** — BPE over raw bytes; **no out-of-vocabulary (OOV)** ever, since any string decomposes into bytes.
- **OOV (out-of-vocabulary)** — a token the model can't represent; subword/byte schemes essentially eliminate it.
- **Special tokens** — reserved IDs like end-of-text, padding, or chat-role markers, not derived from normal text.
- **Detokenization** — mapping generated IDs back to a string (handling the space-marking scheme correctly).
- **Tokens-per-word** — English averages ~1.3 tokens/word; code and non-English text are far higher.

**Why interviewers ask this**

Tokenization is where the elegant "it's just next-token prediction" story meets messy reality, and it separates people who've actually looked inside from people who've only used the API. A senior candidate can explain *why* a model fails at spelling and counting (it sees IDs, not letters), *why* the same word costs differently in different positions (leading-space tokens), and *why* non-English prompts are more expensive (worse token efficiency). It also underpins cost and context reasoning — everything is billed and bounded in tokens. Interviewers love the "strawberry" question specifically because a shallow answer says "the model is bad at math"; a deep answer traces the failure to the tokenizer and shows you understand the model's actual input. Getting this right demonstrates you know the boundary between the neural network and its preprocessing.

**Common confusions**

- "One token = one word." No — a word can be several tokens, and a token can be a fragment, a space+word, or punctuation.
- "The model can spell / see letters." It sees integer IDs; individual letters aren't separate inputs, so spelling and letter-counting are unreliable.
- "OOV words break the model." Modern byte-level BPE has **no OOV** — anything decomposes to bytes.
- "Tokenization is part of the neural net." It's a deterministic pre/post-processing step *outside* the network.
- "Bigger vocab is strictly better." Bigger vocab = shorter sequences but a much larger embedding/unembedding matrix and rarer tokens seen less often — it's a trade-off.
- "Numbers are tokenized digit-by-digit." Depends on the tokenizer; many split numbers inconsistently, which is a real source of arithmetic errors.

**What follows from this topic**

The integer IDs produced here are the direct input to the **Embeddings** topic — each ID indexes a row of the embedding matrix. Vocabulary size sets the height of both the embedding matrix (`vocab x d_model`) and the final unembedding/LM head (`d_model x vocab`), and thus the shape of the output **logits** (`seq x vocab`). Tokens are also the unit in which **context window** and **cost** are measured (an applied concern developed in the AI Engineering primer). And the "model sees IDs, not letters" fact recurs whenever we discuss what the model can and can't perceive. Tokenization is step zero — get it clear and the shapes in every later topic line up.

### Q1. What is a token, and why don't LLMs operate on raw characters or whole words?

A **token** is the atomic unit an LLM reads and writes — almost always a **subword** chunk (a whole common word, a word fragment, punctuation, or a space+word), mapped to an integer ID via a fixed vocabulary. The model consumes and produces sequences of these integers, never raw text.

Why subwords rather than the two obvious extremes?

- **Whole words** — the vocabulary would be enormous and still hit **out-of-vocabulary** words (typos, new terms, rare names). You can't have a slot for every possible string.
- **Characters** — no OOV problem, but sequences become very long (one token per letter), which blows up the O(n^2) attention cost and forces the model to relearn spelling from scratch for every word.
- **Subwords** — the sweet spot: common words are one token (short sequences), rare words split into reusable pieces ("tokenization" -> "token" + "ization"), and byte-level fallback guarantees no OOV. You get compact sequences *and* full coverage.

That's why every modern LLM uses subword tokenization.

### Q2. Explain Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE). How is the vocabulary actually built?

BPE builds the vocabulary **bottom-up by merging**. You start with a base alphabet (characters or bytes), then repeatedly find the **most frequent adjacent pair** of symbols in the corpus and merge it into a new single symbol, recording the merge rule. Repeat until you hit the target vocab size.

```text
start (characters):   l o w   l o w e r   n e w e s t   w i d e s t
count adjacent pairs; "e s" is most frequent -> merge into "es"
  ->  l o w   l o w e r   n e w es t   w i d es t
next most frequent "es t" -> merge into "est"
  ->  l o w   l o w e r   n e w est   w i d est
next "l o" -> "lo", then "lo w" -> "low", ...
final vocab = base chars + all merged symbols
merge rules (ordered) = the tokenizer
```

At inference, you apply the learned merge rules greedily to any new text: split into base symbols, then apply merges in order. Frequent words end up as a single token; rare words as a handful of subword pieces. The elegance is that the vocabulary is **learned from data** — the corpus's statistics decide which chunks are worth a token.

### Q3. Compare BPE, WordPiece, and SentencePiece.

| | BPE | WordPiece | SentencePiece |
|---|---|---|---|
| Used by | GPT, Llama (byte-level BPE) | BERT | T5, Llama, many multilingual |
| Merge criterion | Most **frequent** adjacent pair | Pair that most increases **likelihood** | Wraps BPE or unigram over raw stream |
| Pre-tokenization | Usually split on whitespace first | Splits on whitespace first | **None** — treats text (incl. spaces) as raw |
| Continuation mark | Leading space encodes word start | `##` prefix marks continuation | `_` (underscore) marks a space |
| OOV | None (byte-level) | Falls back to `[UNK]`/chars | None |

**BPE** merges the *most frequent* pair. **WordPiece** (BERT) is very similar but picks the merge that maximizes the *likelihood* of the training data rather than raw frequency, and marks subword continuations with `##` (e.g. "playing" -> "play", "##ing"). **SentencePiece** is more of a framework: it operates directly on the **raw character/byte stream including spaces**, so it needs no language-specific pre-tokenization (great for languages without spaces, like Japanese) and treats whitespace as a real symbol (the `_` marker). Byte-level BPE (GPT-2 onward) runs BPE over raw bytes, guaranteeing zero OOV. In practice they're variations on the same "learn subword merges from data" theme.

### Q4. What does "byte-level BPE" buy you, and why does it mean no out-of-vocabulary tokens?

Byte-level BPE runs the BPE merge process over **raw bytes** (0–255) instead of Unicode characters. The base alphabet is just the 256 possible byte values, and merges build up common byte sequences into tokens.

The payoff is **no OOV, ever**. Any conceivable string — a typo, an emoji, a Klingon word, a hash, a byte sequence you've never seen — is ultimately a sequence of bytes, and every byte is in the base vocabulary. So the tokenizer can always represent *anything*; worst case it falls back to encoding a weird string byte-by-byte (many tokens, but no failure). Contrast a word-level or even character-level Unicode tokenizer, which can hit characters or words outside its fixed set and must emit an `[UNK]` token, destroying information. Byte-level fallback is why you can paste arbitrary garbage into a modern LLM and it still tokenizes cleanly — it just may cost a lot of tokens.

### Q5. Trace exactly why an LLM struggles with "how many r's are in strawberry."

Because the model **never sees the letters**. Here's the chain:

```text
"strawberry"  --tokenizer-->  e.g. ["str", "aw", "berry"]  or even one token
                              -> integer IDs, e.g. [2536, 707, 8396]
model input:  just those integers. The characters s-t-r-a-w-b-e-r-r-y
              are NOT separate inputs anywhere.
```

Each token is an opaque integer that indexes a row of the embedding matrix. The model has no direct access to the character composition of a token — "berry" is a single vector, not the sequence b-e-r-r-y. To answer "how many r's," it would have to have *memorized*, per token, the letter counts — which it sometimes approximately learns but can't reliably compute, because letter-level structure was never in its input.

It's like being asked to count the letters in a word you've only ever heard spoken as one syllable. The failure is **not** a reasoning or math weakness per se — it's a **perception** limit imposed by tokenization. This is the canonical demonstration that the model operates on token IDs, not text. (Aside: this is also why some models do better if you space out the letters — "s t r a w b e r r y" tokenizes closer to per-character.)

### Q6. Why are " apple", "apple", and "App" three different tokens?

Because the tokenizer distinguishes them by exact byte content, including **leading whitespace and case**:

- **" apple"** (with a leading space) is typically a single token — the common "word at a word boundary" form. Most words inside running text have a preceding space, so tokenizers learn the space-prefixed version as the frequent token.
- **"apple"** (no leading space) is a *different* token or splits differently — it's the form that appears at the very start of text or right after punctuation with no space.
- **"App"** is a different token again — different letters, different case; "App" (capital A) is not "app".

Each of these maps to a **distinct integer ID** and therefore a **distinct embedding row**. This is why prompt formatting subtly matters, why leading/trailing spaces can change token counts and even outputs, and why " the" and "the" can behave differently. The model learns the meaning of each token independently, though it typically learns that the space-prefixed and non-prefixed versions of a word are related.

### Q7. What are special tokens and why do they exist?

**Special tokens** are reserved vocabulary entries that don't correspond to normal text — they carry structural meaning. Common ones:

- **End-of-text / EOS** — marks the end of a document (in pretraining) or the end of the model's turn (in chat); generation stops when it's sampled.
- **BOS / start** — marks the beginning of a sequence.
- **Padding** — fills batches to equal length (masked out so it doesn't affect the result).
- **Chat-role markers** — like `<|system|>`, `<|user|>`, `<|assistant|>`, used by the chat template to delimit turns.
- **Unknown ([UNK])** — in schemes that can hit OOV (not needed in byte-level BPE).

They exist because the model needs to perceive structure that isn't in the raw words — where a document ends, whose turn it is, where to stop. They're given their own IDs and embeddings, learned like any other token. The **chat template** (an SFT/post-training concept) is built entirely from special tokens: they're how a decoder-only model knows "this part is the user, now generate the assistant part." A subtle security note: because they're real tokens, you generally don't let raw user text inject them.

### Q8. Roughly how big is an LLM's vocabulary, and what's the trade-off in choosing it?

Typical vocabularies run from about **30k (BERT) to 100k–130k+** for recent large models (some multilingual/multimodal ones go higher). The choice is a genuine trade-off:

**Bigger vocabulary:**
- Shorter token sequences (more text per token) -> less O(n^2) attention cost, longer effective context, cheaper per unit of text.
- Better handling of many languages (more room for non-English subwords).
- But: a much larger **embedding matrix** (`vocab x d_model`) and **LM head** (`d_model x vocab`) — millions more parameters — and each rare token is seen less often in training, so it's learned less well.

**Smaller vocabulary:**
- Fewer embedding/output parameters, each token well-trained.
- But: longer sequences (more tokens per word), higher attention cost, worse multilingual coverage.

So vocab size balances **sequence length and multilingual coverage** against **parameter count and per-token training signal**. Modern models trend larger (~100k+) because context length and multilingual quality became priorities and the extra embedding params are affordable relative to the depth of the network.

### Q9. How do tokenizers handle numbers, and why does this hurt arithmetic?

Number tokenization is often **inconsistent**, and that inconsistency is a real source of arithmetic errors. Depending on the tokenizer, "12345" might become one token, or "123"+"45", or "12"+"345", and the split can depend on surrounding context and whitespace. Some newer tokenizers deliberately split numbers into individual digits (or fixed 3-digit chunks) to make arithmetic more regular.

The problem: if "27" is one token and "28" is another *unrelated* token, the model doesn't get the digit-level structure that makes addition systematic — it has to memorize relationships between whole-number tokens rather than compute digit by digit with carrying. Irregular splits ("1000" vs "1", "000") make it worse. This is *on top of* the fixed-compute-per-token limit. Combined, they explain why LLMs are unreliable on multi-digit arithmetic even though they nail the concept. The fix in practice is either digit-level tokenization, tool use (call a calculator), or chain-of-thought that forces per-digit steps — the first is a tokenizer choice, the latter two are applied-layer patterns.

### Q10. Why is code, or non-English text, less token-efficient?

**Token efficiency** = how much text one token carries, and it depends on how well the training corpus's statistics match your input.

- **English prose** — tokenizers are usually trained heavily on English, so common English words are single tokens; efficiency is high (~1.3 tokens/word).
- **Code** — lots of whitespace/indentation, punctuation, `camelCase`/`snake_case` identifiers that split into pieces, and symbols. Indentation alone can eat tokens. So code is denser in tokens per character.
- **Non-English / non-Latin scripts** — if the language was underrepresented when the vocab was built, its words fragment into many subword or even byte tokens. A sentence in a low-resource language can cost several times more tokens than its English translation.

The consequences are practical: non-English users pay more (tokens = cost) and hit context limits faster for the same content, and heavily formatted code consumes context quickly. It's a fairness and cost issue rooted purely in whose text dominated the tokenizer's training corpus. The efficiency numbers are an applied concern (AI Engineering), but the *cause* — vocab trained on a skewed corpus — is internal to tokenization.

### Q11. Is tokenization part of the neural network? Where does it sit in the pipeline?

No — tokenization is a **deterministic pre/post-processing step that sits outside the neural network**. It has no learned weights that change during model training in the usual sense (the tokenizer's merge rules are learned once, *before* model training, and then frozen).

```text
[ text ] --tokenizer (outside the net)--> [ token IDs ]
        --> [  NEURAL NETWORK: embeddings + transformer blocks + LM head  ]
        --> [ logits -> sample token ID ] --detokenizer (outside the net)--> [ text ]
```

The network only ever ingests integer IDs and only ever outputs a distribution over IDs. Encoding (text -> IDs) and decoding (IDs -> text) are lookup-and-merge algorithms bolted on the front and back. This boundary is exactly why the model "can't see letters": the character-level information is discarded by the tokenizer before the network ever runs. Understanding that the tokenizer is a separate, frozen, deterministic component — not something the model reasons through — is the key mental separation this topic is teaching.

### Q12. Why are tokens the unit of cost and context, not words or characters?

Because tokens are literally what the model processes, so everything the model does is measured per token:

- **Context window** is a token count — the attention mechanism operates over a sequence of token positions, and its O(n^2) cost and the trained positional range are both in terms of *token positions*, not words.
- **Cost / pricing** is per token — each token is one forward-pass position (input) or one autoregressive step (output), so compute scales with token count.
- **Latency** — output latency scales with the number of tokens generated (one forward step each).

Words and characters are the wrong unit because the mapping to tokens is nonuniform: an English word averages ~1.3 tokens, a rare word or non-English word many more, a character usually less than one token. So "500 words" could be 600 or 2000 tokens depending on content. That's why every limit and price is quoted in tokens. The *usage* implications (budgeting, truncation, cost estimation) belong to the AI Engineering primer; the *reason* — tokens are the model's actual processing unit — is internal.

### Q13. Given a tokenizer, how would you tokenize "unhappiness" and what would you expect?

You'd apply the tokenizer's learned merge rules greedily. For a subword BPE/WordPiece vocabulary, "unhappiness" is unlikely to be a single token (it's not that frequent), so you'd expect it to split into reusable morphological-ish pieces:

```text
"unhappiness"  ->  ["un", "happ", "iness"]     # illustrative BPE-style
             or ->  ["un", "happiness"]         # if "happiness" made the vocab
WordPiece style ->  ["un", "##happ", "##iness"] # ## marks continuation
```

The exact split depends entirely on which merges that specific tokenizer learned from its corpus, which is why you should *actually run the tokenizer* rather than guess. The important interview points: (1) common prefixes/suffixes like "un" and "-ness" tend to become their own tokens because they recur across many words, giving the model reusable pieces; (2) the split is **not** guaranteed to align with real morphemes — it follows *frequency*, not linguistics; and (3) a leading space would change the first token (" un..." vs "un..."). Knowing to reach for the actual tokenizer, and knowing splits are statistical not linguistic, is the senior signal.

### Q14. Two prompts differ only by a trailing space or newline. Can that change the output? Why?

Yes, it can — and the reason is entirely tokenization. A trailing space or newline changes the **token sequence**, and often the boundary tokens. For example, ending your prompt with "The answer is" versus "The answer is " (trailing space) can tokenize differently: with the trailing space, the model may be "expecting" a space-prefixed continuation token, but you've already consumed the space, so the next token it should produce is a *non*-space-prefixed one — a different distribution.

```text
"The answer is"    -> next expected token often " 42"  (space-prefixed)
"The answer is "   -> the space is used up; next token "42" (no space)
```

Because the model's whole computation is a function of the exact token IDs, any change to those IDs — including invisible whitespace — can shift the output distribution. This is why careful prompt formatting matters and why "trailing whitespace" is a classic gotcha: it's not superstition, it's the model conditioning on a genuinely different input sequence. It also underlies why some tokenizers warn against adding a trailing space before generation.

### Q15. What's the relationship between the vocabulary size and the model's parameters and output shape?

The vocabulary size `V` directly sets three things:

- **Embedding matrix**: shape `(V, d_model)` — one learned row per token. With `V = 100k` and `d_model = 4096`, that's ~410M parameters just for embeddings.
- **Unembedding / LM head**: shape `(d_model, V)` — projects the final hidden state to one score per vocab token. Often **tied** to the embedding matrix (same weights reused) to save `V*d_model` parameters.
- **Output logits shape**: `(batch, seq, V)` — the model emits a score for *every* vocab token at *every* position. Softmax over the `V` dimension gives the next-token distribution.

So a larger vocabulary makes the two biggest single matrices in the model bigger and widens the logits/softmax the model computes at every step. That's the parameter-count side of the vocab trade-off from Q8, and it's why the softmax over the vocabulary can be a nontrivial cost. It also ties this topic directly into Embeddings (the `(V, d_model)` matrix) and the forward pass (logits of shape `(seq, V)`).

### Q16. What are the biggest tokenization pitfalls an engineer should know?

- **Letter-level tasks** — spelling, counting characters, reversing strings, rhyming: unreliable because the model sees token IDs, not letters (the strawberry problem).
- **Arithmetic** — inconsistent number tokenization breaks digit-level structure; prefer tools or digit-level tokenization.
- **Whitespace sensitivity** — leading/trailing spaces and newlines change tokens and can change outputs; " word" != "word".
- **Non-English cost** — underrepresented languages fragment into many tokens; higher cost, faster context exhaustion.
- **Code density** — indentation and punctuation eat tokens; formatted code fills context fast.
- **Token-boundary surprises** — a word can split unexpectedly; token count != word count; always measure with the real tokenizer.
- **Special-token injection** — raw user text should not be able to inject role/control tokens (a security and correctness concern).
- **Detokenization edge cases** — reassembling spaces/`##`/`_` markers correctly matters when streaming partial tokens.

The unifying theme: the tokenizer defines what the model can *perceive* and how much everything *costs*. Most "the model is dumb at X" complaints, when X is character- or number-level, trace straight back here.

## Embeddings & the Embedding Matrix

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

Tokenization gives us integer token IDs; those integers carry no meaning a neural network can use (ID 5000 isn't "bigger" or "closer" to anything). This topic covers the step that fixes that: the **embedding matrix**, a learned table of shape `(vocab, d_model)` that maps each token ID to a dense vector (its **embedding**). We cover how the lookup works (it's literally selecting a row), why representing **meaning as geometry** (similar tokens land near each other, measured by cosine similarity) is the whole point, what **d_model** is, how embeddings are *learned* (they're ordinary parameters updated by gradient descent, not hand-designed), the idea of **tied** input and output (unembedding) weights, and the critical distinction between **static** token embeddings (what the lookup gives you) and **contextual** representations (what the transformer blocks produce). The 15 questions here range from "what is an embedding" to "why tie the embedding and unembedding weights" and "static vs contextual embeddings."

**Mental model**

Imagine a giant spreadsheet with one row per token in the vocabulary and `d_model` columns (say 4096). Row 5000 is a list of 4096 numbers — the "coordinates" of that token in a high-dimensional space. Embedding a token is just **looking up its row**. Initially these numbers are random; during training, gradient descent nudges them so that tokens used in similar ways drift close together and the geometry of the space comes to encode meaning — directions and distances become semantically meaningful. This is the model's first act of "understanding": turning a meaningless integer into a rich vector it can do math on. Crucially, the vector you get from the lookup is **static** — the same for "bank" whether you mean a riverbank or a financial one. It's the transformer blocks *above* the embedding that mix in context and produce a context-dependent representation. The embedding matrix is where discrete symbols become continuous geometry.

**Key terms**

- **Embedding** — the dense vector a token ID maps to; a point in `d_model`-dimensional space.
- **Embedding matrix** — the learned lookup table of shape `(vocab, d_model)`; row `i` is token `i`'s embedding.
- **d_model** — the model's hidden/embedding dimension (e.g. 768, 4096); the width carried through every layer.
- **Lookup / gather** — indexing the embedding matrix by token ID to fetch its row (equivalent to a one-hot matmul).
- **Meaning as geometry** — semantically similar tokens end up as nearby vectors; relationships become directions.
- **Cosine similarity** — measures the angle between two vectors; the usual metric for "how similar" two embeddings are.
- **Unembedding / LM head** — the matrix `(d_model, vocab)` mapping the final hidden vector back to per-token logits.
- **Weight tying** — reusing the embedding matrix (transposed) as the unembedding matrix to save parameters.
- **Static embedding** — the context-free vector from the lookup (same for every occurrence of a token).
- **Contextual representation** — the context-dependent vector a transformer block outputs for a position.

**Why interviewers ask this**

Embeddings are the bridge from "discrete symbols" to "differentiable geometry," and understanding them cleanly signals you grasp *why* neural nets can work with language at all. A junior answer treats "embedding" as a buzzword or confuses the model's internal token embeddings with the API "embeddings endpoint" (sentence embeddings for search). A senior answer explains the exact shape `(vocab, d_model)`, that the lookup is a row-select equivalent to a one-hot matmul, that these are *just parameters* learned by the same gradient descent as everything else, and — the deep one — that the static lookup is **not** the same as the contextual output of the blocks. The weight-tying question is a favorite because it tests whether you see the symmetry between turning tokens into vectors (embedding) and turning vectors back into token scores (unembedding).

**Common confusions**

- "Embeddings are computed by a special algorithm like word2vec." In an LLM they're **learned parameters** trained jointly with the rest of the model by next-token prediction.
- "A token's embedding captures its meaning in context." The **lookup** embedding is **static/context-free**; context is added by the transformer blocks above it.
- "The embedding matrix and the internal token vectors are the model's 'embeddings endpoint'." Different thing — sentence/document embeddings for search are a separate applied use (AI Engineering).
- "Similar IDs mean similar tokens." Token IDs are arbitrary; similarity lives in the learned *vectors*, not the integer values.
- "d_model is the vocab size." No — `d_model` is the vector *width* (e.g. 4096); vocab is the number of tokens (e.g. 100k).
- "Tying weights just saves memory." It saves parameters *and* reflects a real symmetry that often helps quality.

**What follows from this topic**

The embedding matrix is the entry point to the whole network: token IDs `(batch, seq)` become vectors `(batch, seq, d_model)`, and `d_model` is the width every subsequent layer preserves. **Positional encoding** is added to these embeddings so the model knows token *order* (a separate topic — embeddings alone are position-blind). The **attention** and **transformer-block** topics operate entirely in this `d_model`-dimensional space, transforming the static embeddings into contextual ones. At the very end, the **unembedding/LM head** — often the tied embedding matrix — maps the final vectors back to logits over the vocabulary. So this topic defines the space in which all the interesting computation happens, and its shape `(vocab, d_model)` bookends the model at both ends.

### Q1. What is an embedding, and why can't the model just use the token IDs directly?

An **embedding** is a dense vector of `d_model` real numbers that represents a token. The model can't use the raw integer IDs because those integers are **arbitrary labels** with no useful numerical structure: token 5000 isn't "more" than token 10, and IDs 4999 and 5000 aren't semantically related just because they're adjacent. Feeding such integers into a neural net directly is meaningless — the network needs continuous, meaningful inputs it can do linear algebra on.

The embedding step maps each ID to a learned point in a high-dimensional space where **distance and direction encode meaning**. Now "cat" and "dog" can be near each other, math like vector addition/subtraction becomes sensible, and the network can compute smooth functions of the input. In short: token IDs are discrete names; embeddings are the coordinates that let the model *reason geometrically* about tokens. It's the essential first move from symbols to numbers-with-structure.

### Q2. What is the embedding matrix — its shape, and what does a lookup actually do?

The **embedding matrix** `E` has shape `(vocab, d_model)` — one row per token in the vocabulary, each row a `d_model`-dimensional vector.

```python
E.shape                 # (vocab, d_model), e.g. (100000, 4096)
token_id = 5000
vector = E[token_id]    # a single row -> shape (d_model,)
# for a whole sequence of IDs:
ids   = [464, 3797, 3332]          # (seq,)
x     = E[ids]                     # (seq, d_model)  -- just row selection
```

"Embedding a token" is literally **selecting its row** — a gather/indexing operation. Mathematically it's equivalent to multiplying a one-hot vector (a length-`vocab` vector that's 1 at the token's index, 0 elsewhere) by `E`: `one_hot(id) @ E = E[id]`. That's why you sometimes see embeddings described as "a one-hot matmul" — the lookup is the efficient version. For a batch of sequences, input IDs of shape `(batch, seq)` become embeddings of shape `(batch, seq, d_model)`. That tensor is what enters the first transformer block.

### Q3. What is d_model, and how do you choose it?

`d_model` (also called the hidden size or model dimension) is the **width of the vectors** the model carries through every layer — the length of each token's embedding, and the dimension preserved by every transformer block from input to output. Typical values: 768 (GPT-2 small), 1600 (GPT-2 XL), 4096 and up (large models).

It's a fundamental capacity knob. Larger `d_model`:
- more representational capacity per token (richer vectors), and
- more parameters — the embedding matrix `(vocab, d_model)`, every attention projection (`~4*d_model^2` per layer), and the FFN (`~8*d_model^2` per layer) all scale with it (the attention/FFN terms scale *quadratically*).

So `d_model` is the model's **width**, traded off against its **depth** (number of layers `N`). Wider models have more capacity per layer; deeper models compose more transformations. Choosing `d_model`, `N`, the number of heads, and vocab together is the core of model sizing (and connects to scaling laws in the training topics). Every shape in the network — `(batch, seq, d_model)` — is anchored on this one number.

### Q4. How are embeddings learned? Are they hand-designed or precomputed?

Neither hand-designed nor precomputed — in an LLM the embeddings are **ordinary learnable parameters**, initialized randomly and updated by the *same* gradient descent that trains everything else. The embedding matrix is part of the model's weights; during pretraining, backpropagation flows all the way back to it, and each row is nudged to reduce next-token-prediction loss.

```text
init:   E = random (vocab, d_model)
train:  forward pass uses E[ids]; cross-entropy loss;
        backprop computes dLoss/dE for the rows that were used;
        AdamW updates those rows.
result: rows for tokens used in similar contexts drift together;
        the geometry comes to encode meaning -- entirely from data.
```

There's no separate word2vec/GloVe step in a modern LLM; the embeddings are trained *jointly* and *end-to-end* with the rest of the network on the next-token objective. (Classic standalone embedding methods like word2vec exist and share the "meaning as geometry" idea, but LLM token embeddings are learned inside the model.) The upshot: the model *discovers* a good vector for each token as a byproduct of learning to predict — nobody specifies what "cat" should map to.

### Q5. What does "meaning as geometry" mean, and how do you measure similarity?

"Meaning as geometry" is the idea that after training, **semantic relationships become spatial relationships** in the embedding space. Tokens used in similar contexts end up as nearby vectors; unrelated tokens end up far apart; and sometimes consistent relationships become consistent *directions* (the famous, if idealized, "king - man + woman ~ queen" analogy from word embeddings).

The standard similarity measure is **cosine similarity** — the cosine of the angle between two vectors:

```text
cos(a, b) = (a . b) / (|a| * |b|)
  = 1   -> same direction (very similar)
  = 0   -> orthogonal (unrelated)
  = -1  -> opposite
```

Cosine is used (rather than raw Euclidean distance) because it focuses on **direction, not magnitude** — two tokens can be "similar in meaning" regardless of vector length. This geometric view is *why* neural nets handle language: once meaning is coordinates, the model can compute smooth, differentiable functions over it, interpolate, and combine tokens with linear algebra. Attention itself is built on dot products between such vectors — geometry all the way down.

### Q6. Static vs contextual embeddings — what's the difference and why does it matter?

This is the crucial distinction. The embedding-matrix **lookup gives a static embedding**: the vector for "bank" is *identical* whether the sentence is "river bank" or "savings bank," because the lookup only depends on the token ID, not the surrounding words. Static embeddings are context-free.

**Contextual representations** are what the **transformer blocks produce**: as the vector passes up through attention layers, it mixes in information from the surrounding tokens, so the vector at the "bank" position *diverges* depending on context — riverbank-bank and savings-bank end up as different vectors at the output.

```text
lookup (static):      "bank" -> the same vector, always
after block 1..N:     "bank" in "river bank"   -> vector A
                      "bank" in "savings bank"  -> vector B   (A != B)
```

Why it matters: word-sense disambiguation, coreference, and essentially all understanding come from this contextualization — it's what attention *is for*. Interviewers ask this to check you don't conflate the input embedding (static, from the matrix) with the model's internal representations (contextual, from the blocks). The classic BERT/transformer breakthrough was precisely producing *contextual* embeddings instead of static per-word vectors.

### Q7. What is the unembedding matrix / LM head, and how does it relate to the embedding matrix?

The **unembedding matrix** (a.k.a. the **LM head**) sits at the *top* of the model and does the reverse of the embedding matrix. Where embedding maps a token ID -> a `d_model` vector, the unembedding maps a final `d_model` vector -> a score for **every** token in the vocabulary:

```text
embedding:   (vocab, d_model)   ID  -> vector       [bottom of model]
unembedding: (d_model, vocab)   vector -> logits     [top of model]

final_hidden @ W_unembed  ->  logits of shape (seq, vocab)
```

Each column of the unembedding matrix is essentially a "template" vector for one token; the dot product of the final hidden state with that column measures "how much does this position look like it should be followed by token j." Those dot products are the **logits**. So the two matrices are mirror images: one turns tokens into vectors, the other turns vectors back into per-token scores. This symmetry is exactly what motivates **weight tying** (next question) — the two matrices are shaped as transposes of each other and can literally be the same weights.

### Q8. What is weight tying, and why tie the input and output embeddings?

**Weight tying** means using the **same matrix** for the input embedding and the output unembedding (the LM head): the unembedding is just the embedding matrix transposed. Since embedding is `(vocab, d_model)` and unembedding is `(d_model, vocab)`, they're the same numbers arranged as transposes.

Two reasons:
- **Parameter savings** — the embedding and LM head are each `vocab * d_model` parameters (often the single largest matrices in the model). Tying them removes one full copy — hundreds of millions of parameters for a large vocab.
- **A real symmetry / regularization** — the vector that *represents* a token (input) and the vector that *predicts* a token (output template) arguably should be related. Tying encodes that inductive bias and often *improves* generalization, not just memory.

```text
untied:  E (vocab, d_model)  and  W_out (d_model, vocab)   -- two big matrices
tied:    logits = final_hidden @ E^T                        -- reuse E
```

It's not universal — some models keep them separate — but tying is common (GPT-2, many others). Being able to explain *both* the memory and the symmetry justification is the senior answer; saying only "it saves memory" misses that it reflects a genuine input/output duality.

### Q9. Trace the shapes: from token IDs to the input of the first transformer block.

```text
token IDs:        (batch, seq)                 integers
  |  E[ids]  (row lookup into (vocab, d_model))
embeddings:       (batch, seq, d_model)         dense vectors
  |  + positional encoding  (same shape, added elementwise)
block input:      (batch, seq, d_model)
```

Concretely: you start with a batch of tokenized sequences, shape `(batch, seq)` of integers. Indexing the embedding matrix `E` of shape `(vocab, d_model)` by those IDs produces `(batch, seq, d_model)` — every token is now a `d_model`-vector. You then **add positional information** (same shape) so the model knows order (embeddings alone are position-blind — covered in the positional-encoding topic). The result, still `(batch, seq, d_model)`, is what feeds the first transformer block. Every block preserves this shape, so `(batch, seq, d_model)` is the "highway width" of the entire network from here to the final norm. Being able to recite this shape chain — and note that `d_model` never changes through the stack — is exactly the fluency interviewers probe.

### Q10. Why cosine similarity rather than Euclidean distance for comparing embeddings?

Because **cosine similarity ignores magnitude and compares direction**, which is usually what "semantic similarity" should mean. In embedding spaces, a vector's *length* can be influenced by things like token frequency, while its *direction* tends to carry the semantic content. Two tokens that mean similar things should count as similar even if one vector happens to be longer.

```text
cos(a,b) = (a.b)/(|a||b|)     direction only, range [-1, 1]
euclidean(a,b) = |a - b|      sensitive to both direction AND magnitude
```

If you used raw Euclidean distance, two semantically identical directions with different magnitudes would look "far apart," and a very long vector could dominate comparisons. Cosine normalizes that away by dividing out the magnitudes. (When vectors are unit-normalized, cosine and Euclidean actually rank neighbors identically, so people often L2-normalize and then either works.) In practice cosine is the default for embedding similarity — in the model's internal geometry and in the applied "embeddings for search/RAG" use case. That said, attention itself uses raw **dot products** (not normalized), which is why scaling by sqrt(d_k) matters there — a related but distinct topic.

### Q11. Are the model's token embeddings the same as an "embeddings API" for search?

No — they're related in spirit but different in practice, and conflating them is a common tell. The model's **token embeddings** are the internal `(vocab, d_model)` lookup rows for individual tokens, used as the network's input layer. An **"embeddings endpoint"** (the applied use, covered in AI Engineering) produces a single vector for a whole **sentence, paragraph, or document**, designed for semantic search, clustering, or RAG retrieval.

The differences:
- **Granularity** — per-token vs per-passage.
- **Purpose** — internal model input vs external similarity/retrieval.
- **How derived** — a raw lookup vs typically pooling/aggregating a model's *contextual* outputs (often from an encoder tuned for retrieval).

They share the "meaning as geometry, compare by cosine" idea, which is why the terminology overlaps. But when an interviewer asks about "the embedding matrix," they mean the internal token lookup discussed in this topic; when they ask about "embeddings for RAG," they mean sentence-level vectors from a retrieval model. Knowing which is which — and that document embeddings usually come from *contextual* representations, not the static token lookup — signals you understand both layers.

### Q12. Do similar token IDs imply similar tokens? Why or why not?

No. **Token IDs are arbitrary labels** assigned during vocabulary construction (roughly by merge order or frequency rank), and their numeric values carry no semantic meaning. Token 4999 and token 5000 are almost certainly unrelated; "cat" might be ID 9834 and "dog" ID 12; the integers say nothing about similarity.

Similarity lives entirely in the **learned embedding vectors**, not the IDs. Two tokens are "close" if `E[id_a]` and `E[id_b]` have high cosine similarity — a property that emerges from *training*, not from the IDs being numerically near. This is exactly *why* the embedding step exists: to replace meaningless integer labels with meaningful coordinates. If IDs already encoded similarity, you wouldn't need embeddings. A related confusion is thinking adjacency in the vocabulary implies relatedness — it doesn't; the vocabulary order is an artifact of how BPE merges were counted, not a semantic map. Meaning is in the vectors, assigned by gradient descent.

### Q13. How many parameters are in the embedding layer, and is that a lot?

The embedding matrix has exactly `vocab * d_model` parameters. Worked examples:

```text
GPT-2 small:  vocab 50257, d_model 768   ->  ~38.6M params
large model:  vocab 100000, d_model 4096  ->  ~410M params
```

Is that a lot? It depends on the model's total size:
- For **small models**, the embedding (and untied LM head) can be a *large fraction* of all parameters — in GPT-2 small, ~38M out of 124M total, roughly a third when you count input embeddings. That's a big reason weight tying matters at small scale.
- For **very large models**, the transformer blocks dominate (each layer is `~12*d_model^2` params and there are many layers), so the embedding becomes a smaller share — but it's still one of the single biggest *individual* matrices, and it's `O(vocab * d_model)`, which grows if you enlarge the vocabulary.

This is the parameter-count side of the vocab-size trade-off from the Tokenization topic: a bigger vocab shortens sequences but directly inflates this matrix (and the LM head, if untied). It also connects to the full parameter-count formula in the architecture/training topics: total ~= embeddings + N * (attention + FFN).

### Q14. Spot the misconception: "The embedding for 'apple' already knows whether I mean the fruit or the company."

That's wrong — it confuses **static** lookup embeddings with **contextual** representations. The embedding-matrix row for the token(s) in "apple" is a *single fixed vector*, identical regardless of context. It cannot, by itself, distinguish fruit-apple from company-Apple; the lookup depends only on the token ID.

Disambiguation happens **later**, in the transformer blocks, via attention. As the vector rises through the layers, attention pulls in signal from neighboring tokens ("...ate an apple" vs "...Apple released"), so the *contextual* representation at that position diverges toward the intended sense. The static embedding is a starting point that encodes the token's *average/prior* behavior across all contexts; context resolves it.

```text
lookup:     "apple" -> one vector (context-free, "prior")
attention:  mixes in "ate an" or "released the iPhone"
output:     contextual vector -> fruit-sense or company-sense
```

So the correct statement is: the embedding encodes what "apple" tends to mean in general; the *model* figures out which sense *here* by contextualizing it through attention. Getting this right shows you understand where meaning-in-context actually comes from.

### Q15. Why does turning discrete symbols into continuous vectors matter for training the model at all?

Because neural networks are trained by **gradient descent**, which requires the model to be a **differentiable** function of its inputs and parameters — and you can't take gradients with respect to a discrete integer token ID. Embeddings make the model differentiable at the input: the token ID selects a row, and that row is a vector of **continuous, trainable parameters** you *can* differentiate and update.

```text
discrete ID  --(non-differentiable)-->  can't backprop into "5000"
              but E[5000] is real-valued params -> dLoss/dE flows here
```

This is what lets the input representation be *learned* alongside the rest of the network: gradients flow back through the whole stack into the embedding rows, so the model discovers useful coordinates for each token as it learns to predict. More broadly, embedding is the move that turns a symbolic problem (sequences of discrete tokens) into a **continuous optimization** problem (adjust vectors and matrices to minimize loss) — the only kind of problem gradient descent can solve. Without embeddings, there'd be no differentiable path from the loss back to "which token was the input," and the whole training machinery wouldn't apply. Continuous geometry isn't just convenient for representing meaning; it's what makes the model *trainable*.
## Positional Encodings

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

Attention has no built-in sense of order. This topic explains why that is a problem, and the mechanisms LLMs use to fix it: **sinusoidal** encodings (fixed, from the original transformer), **learned absolute** positions (GPT-2 style), **RoPE** (rotary position embedding — the dominant modern choice), and **ALiBi** (a distance bias on attention scores). It covers the crucial distinction between **absolute** and **relative** position, whether position is **added to the embedding** or **applied inside attention**, and the practical problem of **length extrapolation / context extension** — how a model trained at 4k tokens is stretched to 32k or beyond via RoPE scaling and interpolation. The 16 questions here range from "why does order even matter" up to "derive RoPE and explain why it extrapolates." This sits between the Embeddings topic (which gives each token a vector) and the Attention topic (which mixes those vectors) — position is the bridge that makes attention order-aware.

**Mental model**

Strip position away and self-attention treats the input as a **bag of tokens**, not a sequence. For a set, "dog bites man" and "man bites dog" are identical — same tokens, same multiset — so a position-blind model must produce the same output for both, which is obviously wrong for language. The fix is to stamp each token with *where* it sits before (or during) attention, so that otherwise-identical tokens at different positions become distinguishable, and the relative gap between two tokens becomes something attention can measure. There are two philosophies. The **absolute** camp says "give position 5 a signature vector and add it in." The **relative** camp says "what attention actually cares about is the *distance* between query and key (i-j), not their absolute indices" — and builds that directly into the dot product. RoPE is the elegant compromise: it applies an absolute, per-position rotation to Q and K such that their dot product depends only on their relative offset. That is why RoPE both behaves like relative position and extends gracefully to lengths never seen in training.

**Key terms**

- **Permutation invariance** — attention is a set operation; shuffling the inputs shuffles the outputs identically. Position breaks this symmetry.
- **Absolute position** — the index of a token (0, 1, 2, ...). Encoded by sinusoidal or learned vectors.
- **Relative position** — the offset between two tokens (i - j). What attention scores really depend on; encoded by RoPE and ALiBi.
- **Sinusoidal encoding** — fixed sine/cosine waves of geometrically-spaced frequencies, added to embeddings. No parameters.
- **Learned absolute** — a trainable `(max_len, d_model)` position table; simple but capped at `max_len` and doesn't extrapolate.
- **RoPE (rotary)** — rotates the Q and K vectors by an angle proportional to position; the dot product then encodes relative distance. Dominant today (LLaMA, most modern models).
- **ALiBi** — adds a linear penalty `-m * distance` to attention scores; no position vectors at all, strong length extrapolation.
- **Length extrapolation** — running at a longer sequence length than trained on without retraining.
- **Position interpolation** — squeezing longer positions back into the trained range (e.g. rescale RoPE angles) to extend context.
- **NTK / YaRN scaling** — frequency-aware RoPE rescaling schemes that extend context with less quality loss than naive interpolation.

**Why interviewers ask this**

Position encoding is a favourite because it exposes whether a candidate understands attention *mechanically* or just as a black box. A junior answer is "you add positional encodings so the model knows word order." A senior answer explains **why** attention needs them (permutation invariance), **where** they act (added to embeddings vs applied to Q/K inside attention), and the **absolute-vs-relative** distinction that makes RoPE special. The follow-up — "how do you extend a 4k model to 128k?" — is a direct 2024-2026 production concern, and answering it well (interpolation, NTK, YaRN, why naive extrapolation fails) signals you have actually worked with long-context models rather than only read the original paper. It is a compact question that scales from warm-up to research-level.

**Common confusions**

- "Attention knows order because it processes tokens in sequence." It does not — attention sees all tokens at once as a set; order only exists if you inject it.
- "Positional encoding is a separate token." No — for sinusoidal/learned it is **added element-wise** to the token embedding, same shape, not appended.
- "RoPE is added to the embedding like sinusoidal." No — RoPE is **not added**; it is a rotation applied to Q and K vectors *inside* each attention layer.
- "More positions always means longer usable context." Only if the position scheme extrapolates. Learned absolute tables hard-stop at `max_len`; naive RoPE degrades past its training length without scaling.
- "ALiBi and RoPE both add position vectors." ALiBi adds no vectors at all — it biases the raw attention scores by distance.

**What follows from this topic**

Position feeds directly into **Attention: Query, Key, Value** — RoPE literally modifies Q and K before the dot product, and ALiBi modifies the score matrix. It connects to **Self-Attention, Cross-Attention and Causal Masking** (the mask and the position bias are both applied to the same `(seq, seq)` score matrix). And it underpins the long-context and efficiency story in the architecture topics: context extension is fundamentally a positional-encoding problem layered on top of attention's O(n^2) cost.

### Q1. Why does word order matter if attention is permutation-invariant? What actually breaks without positional encoding?

Self-attention is a **set operation**. Each output position is a weighted average of Value vectors, where the weights come from dot products between queries and keys. Nothing in that computation references *where* a token sits — so if you permute the input tokens, you get the same outputs in permuted order.
