Formally, attention is **equivariant** to permutation: `Attention(P @ x) = P @ Attention(x)` for any permutation P.

For language that is fatal. Consider:

```text
"the dog bites the man"
"the man bites the dog"
```

Same tokens, same multiset — a position-blind model must assign them identical representations and therefore identical next-token predictions. But they mean opposite things. Without position, the model literally cannot tell subject from object, cannot do syntax, cannot count.

The fix is to break the symmetry: stamp each token with information about its position *before or during* attention, so that "dog at position 1" and "dog at position 4" carry different signatures and the model can learn order-dependent behaviour.

### Q2. What is sinusoidal positional encoding and why sinusoids specifically?

The original transformer (Vaswani 2017) adds a fixed, non-learned vector to each token embedding. For position `pos` and embedding dimension `i`:

```python
import numpy as np

def sinusoidal(seq_len, d_model):
    pos = np.arange(seq_len)[:, None]          # (seq, 1)
    i = np.arange(d_model)[None, :]            # (1, d_model)
    freq = 1.0 / (10000 ** (2 * (i // 2) / d_model))
    angles = pos * freq                        # (seq, d_model)
    pe = np.zeros((seq_len, d_model))
    pe[:, 0::2] = np.sin(angles[:, 0::2])      # even dims: sine
    pe[:, 1::2] = np.cos(angles[:, 1::2])      # odd dims: cosine
    return pe                                   # add this to embeddings
```

Each dimension is a sine (or cosine) wave, with wavelengths forming a **geometric progression** from ~2*pi up to ~10000*2*pi. Low dimensions oscillate fast (fine-grained position), high dimensions oscillate slowly (coarse position) — like the bits of a binary counter, but continuous.

Why sinusoids: (1) they are **bounded** in [-1, 1], so they don't blow up the embedding; (2) a linear function can map `PE(pos)` to `PE(pos + k)` for any fixed offset k, which lets the model learn to attend by **relative** distance even though the encoding is absolute; (3) being fixed and periodic, they in principle extend to positions longer than seen in training (though quality still degrades). Shape is `(seq, d_model)`, added element-wise to the token embeddings.

### Q3. Sinusoidal vs learned absolute positional embeddings — what's the tradeoff?

Both are **absolute** and both are **added** to the token embedding with shape `(seq, d_model)`. They differ in where the numbers come from:

| | Sinusoidal (fixed) | Learned absolute |
|---|---|---|
| Parameters | None | `(max_len, d_model)` trainable table |
| Origin | Deterministic sine/cosine formula | Learned from data |
| Max length | Any (formula extends) | Hard cap at `max_len` |
| Extrapolation | Degrades but defined | Undefined past `max_len` |
| Used by | Original Transformer | GPT-2, BERT, early GPT-3 |

Learned embeddings are a simple `nn.Embedding(max_len, d_model)` indexed by position — the model figures out its own position signatures. They often match or slightly beat sinusoidal in-distribution, but they **cannot represent positions beyond the table**: a model with a 1024-row table simply has no vector for position 1025. Sinusoidal has a formula for every position, so it degrades more gracefully. In practice both have largely been superseded by RoPE and ALiBi, which encode *relative* position and extrapolate far better.

### Q4. Explain RoPE (rotary position embedding). How does rotating Q and K encode relative position?

RoPE encodes position by **rotating** the query and key vectors by an angle proportional to their position, instead of adding a position vector. Split each Q (and K) vector into 2D pairs of coordinates. For position `m`, rotate pair `j` by angle `m * theta_j`, where the `theta_j` are the same geometrically-spaced frequencies as sinusoidal.

The key identity: rotating query at position `m` and key at position `n`, then taking their dot product, yields a value that depends only on the **relative offset** `m - n`:

```text
<R(m) q,  R(n) k>  =  <R(m-n) q,  k>        (rotation matrices compose)
```

So although the rotation applied to each token is **absolute** (depends on its own index), the *interaction* that attention actually computes is **relative**. That is the whole trick.

```python
def apply_rope(x, pos, theta_base=10000.0):
    # x: (seq, d), split into d/2 pairs; rotate pair j by pos * theta_j
    d = x.shape[-1]
    j = np.arange(0, d, 2)
    theta = theta_base ** (-j / d)            # (d/2,) frequencies
    ang = pos[:, None] * theta[None, :]       # (seq, d/2) angles
    cos, sin = np.cos(ang), np.sin(ang)
    x1, x2 = x[..., 0::2], x[..., 1::2]        # even, odd coords
    out = np.empty_like(x)
    out[..., 0::2] = x1 * cos - x2 * sin       # 2D rotation per pair
    out[..., 1::2] = x1 * sin + x2 * cos
    return out
```

RoPE is applied to Q and K **inside every attention layer** (not to V, not to the embedding). Because it is relative and multiplicative, it extrapolates far better than learned absolute tables and has become the default in LLaMA, Mistral, Qwen and most modern LLMs.

### Q5. Why is RoPE applied to Q and K inside attention rather than added to the embedding?

Because RoPE's benefit — turning absolute rotations into a **relative** dot product — only materialises *at the moment Q and K meet* in `Q @ K^T`. The relative-offset identity `<R(m)q, R(n)k> = <R(m-n)q, k>` is a statement about a dot product, so the rotation has to be sitting on the vectors that get dotted. If you instead added a position vector to the token embedding (sinusoidal style), it would flow through the Value path and the feed-forward network too, mixing position into content in ways that don't give the clean relative-distance property.

Applying it per-layer, only to Q and K, means: (1) Values stay position-free, so the thing being averaged is pure content; (2) every layer re-derives position from the same rotation, keeping it consistent; (3) there are **no extra parameters** — RoPE is a fixed function of position, like sinusoidal, but acting multiplicatively where it matters.

### Q6. What is ALiBi and how does it differ from RoPE?

ALiBi (Attention with Linear Biases) uses **no position vectors and no rotations at all**. Instead it adds a penalty to the raw attention scores that grows linearly with the distance between query and key:

```text
score(i, j) = q_i . k_j  +  m * (j - i)     # j - i <= 0 under causal mask
```

where `m` is a fixed, head-specific slope (each head gets a different slope, geometrically spaced). Nearby keys get almost no penalty; distant keys get a large negative bias, so attention naturally decays with distance. It is applied to the `(seq, seq)` score matrix, right where the causal mask goes.

| | RoPE | ALiBi |
|---|---|---|
| Mechanism | Rotate Q, K | Add linear distance bias to scores |
| Where | On Q/K vectors | On the score matrix |
| Position type | Relative (via rotation) | Relative (via distance) |
| Parameters | None | None (fixed per-head slopes) |
| Extrapolation | Good with scaling | Very strong out of the box |

ALiBi's headline property is exceptional length extrapolation — a model trained at 1k tokens runs sensibly at much longer lengths — because "penalise distant tokens more" is a rule that stays valid at any length. RoPE is more common in practice and, with interpolation/YaRN, also extends well.

### Q7. Absolute vs relative position — why does the distinction matter so much?

**Absolute** encodings tag each token with its index: position 0, 1, 2, ... **Relative** encodings care only about the gap between two tokens: is the key 1 step back, 5 steps back, 100 steps back?

The distinction matters because **language is mostly translation-invariant**. The relationship "the adjective right before this noun" is the same whether the phrase appears at position 3 or position 3000. A relative scheme captures that directly — the same offset produces the same bias everywhere — so patterns learned early in a sequence transfer to late positions. An absolute scheme has to *learn* that "position 3 -> 4" and "position 3000 -> 3001" are the same kind of adjacency, separately for every pair, which wastes capacity and fails to generalise to unseen positions.

This is exactly why RoPE and ALiBi (both effectively relative) dominate and why they extrapolate: relative rules remain valid at lengths never seen in training, whereas an absolute learned table simply has no entry for position `max_len + 1`.

### Q8. How do you extend a model's context window? Explain RoPE interpolation.

The naive approach — just feed more tokens than you trained on — fails because RoPE's high-frequency rotations wind past angles the model ever saw, producing out-of-distribution attention patterns and gibberish.

**Position interpolation** (Chen et al. 2023) fixes this by **squeezing** the new positions back into the trained range. If you trained at length L and want length 4L, scale every position index by L/(4L) = 1/4 before applying RoPE:

```text
pos_effective = pos * (L_train / L_target)
```

Now position 4L maps to an angle the model saw at position L, so attention stays in-distribution. A short fine-tune adapts the model to the denser positions.

Refinements are frequency-aware:

- **NTK-aware scaling** — don't scale all frequencies equally; stretch low frequencies (coarse position) more and leave high frequencies (fine, local position) nearly intact, preserving local resolution.
- **YaRN** — a tuned combination of NTK scaling plus an attention-temperature adjustment; extends context with minimal quality loss and little fine-tuning. Widely used for 32k-128k+ context models.

The through-line: extending context is a **positional-encoding** problem. You are re-mapping position angles so the model's learned attention behaviour still applies at lengths it never trained on. The applied "context window" that AI-Engineering discusses is the product-level consequence of this internal rescaling.

### Q9. Where in the forward pass does positional information enter, for each scheme?

It depends on the scheme, and this is a common interview trap:

```text
input IDs ---> token embeddings (vocab, d_model lookup)
                       |
   [sinusoidal / learned absolute]  <-- ADDED here, once, before block 1
                       |
                 transformer block 1
                       |
                 ...   |   ...
```

- **Sinusoidal / learned absolute**: added to the token embedding **once**, at the bottom, before any attention. Shape `(seq, d_model)`, element-wise add.
- **RoPE**: **not** at the embedding. Applied **inside every attention layer**, to Q and K only, just before `Q @ K^T`.
- **ALiBi**: also **not** at the embedding. Added to the **score matrix** `(seq, seq)` inside every attention layer, right where the causal mask is applied.

So a quick way to classify a scheme: does it touch the embedding (absolute-additive) or the attention computation (relative — RoPE on vectors, ALiBi on scores)?

### Q10. Walk through the shapes: adding sinusoidal encoding to a batch of embeddings.

```python
# tokens: (batch, seq) integer IDs
emb = embedding_table[tokens]          # (batch, seq, d_model)
pe  = sinusoidal(seq_len, d_model)     # (seq, d_model), no batch dim
x   = emb + pe[None, :, :]             # broadcast over batch -> (batch, seq, d_model)
```

The positional encoding has **no batch dimension** — position 5 is position 5 for every example in the batch — so it broadcasts across `batch`. The add is element-wise; shapes must match on `(seq, d_model)`. Output is unchanged in shape: `(batch, seq, d_model)`, ready for the first transformer block. Two mistakes to avoid: (1) making `pe` depend on batch — it shouldn't; (2) truncating or padding `pe` to the wrong `seq` — it must match the actual sequence length of this batch.

### Q11. What happens if you feed a learned-absolute-position model a sequence longer than its position table?

It breaks, hard. A learned scheme is `nn.Embedding(max_len, d_model)`; indexing it at position `max_len` (0-based, so the `max_len + 1`-th token) is **out of bounds** — there is literally no learned vector for that position. Implementations either throw an index error or, if you naively extend the table with random/zero rows, feed the model position signatures it has never seen, producing garbage.

This is the central weakness of learned absolute positions and a big reason the field moved to RoPE/ALiBi: they are **functions** of position (a rotation, a linear bias) defined for *any* index, so there is no hard table boundary. Even then, RoPE past its training length needs interpolation/YaRN to stay coherent — "defined for any position" is necessary but not sufficient for good extrapolation.

### Q12. Why is RoPE the dominant choice in modern LLMs?

Several properties line up:

1. **Relative by construction** — the Q/K dot product depends on `m - n`, matching language's translation-invariance without a learned table.
2. **No parameters** — it is a fixed function of position, so it adds zero weights and never runs out of table entries.
3. **Values stay clean** — position only touches Q and K, so the averaged Values remain pure content.
4. **Extends well** — with interpolation/NTK/YaRN it stretches from a few-k training length to 100k+ context with modest fine-tuning; this made long-context models practical.
5. **Composable and cheap** — it is just a per-position rotation, trivially fused into the attention kernel and compatible with FlashAttention and KV caching.

The combination — relative, parameter-free, extendable, efficient — is why LLaMA, Mistral, Qwen, DeepSeek and most 2023-2026 decoder-only models use RoPE by default.

### Q13. Can two identical tokens have different representations after positional encoding? Show why.

Yes — that is precisely the point of positional encoding. Take the word "the" appearing at positions 0 and 4 with the same token embedding `e`:

```text
absolute (sinusoidal/learned):
   x_0 = e + PE(0)
   x_4 = e + PE(4)      ->  x_0 != x_4  (different position vectors added)

RoPE:
   q_0 = R(0) @ (e @ W_q)
   q_4 = R(4) @ (e @ W_q)  ->  rotated by different angles, q_0 != q_4
```

So even with an identical starting embedding, the two occurrences become distinguishable — by an added vector (absolute) or a different rotation (RoPE). Without this, a position-blind model would collapse both "the"s into the same representation and could never learn that the first "the" and the fourth "the" play different syntactic roles.

### Q14. Do encoder-only (BERT) and decoder-only (GPT) models use position differently?

The *need* for position is the same — both are permutation-invariant without it — but the surrounding setup differs:

- **BERT (encoder-only, bidirectional)** originally used **learned absolute** position embeddings, added to token (and segment) embeddings. Attention is unmasked, so position must let a token distinguish left context from right context.
- **GPT-2/3 (decoder-only, causal)** also started with **learned absolute** positions, but modern decoder-only LLMs (GPT-NeoX onward, LLaMA, etc.) moved to **RoPE** or **ALiBi**.

The causal mask (in GPT) already tells a token which others are in the past, but the mask alone gives no notion of *how far back* — it is still order-agnostic among visible tokens. So both bidirectional and causal models need an explicit position signal on top of (or independent of) masking. Position and masking are orthogonal: masking says *which* tokens you may see, position says *where they are*.

### Q15. Spot the misconception: "The causal mask already encodes position, so decoder LLMs don't need positional encodings."

Wrong. The causal mask enforces *visibility* — token `i` may attend to tokens `<= i` — but among the tokens it *can* see, attention is still a set operation with **no sense of distance or order**. Concretely, with only a mask and no position encoding, a token at position 5 attending over positions 0-5 cannot tell whether a given key is 1 step back or 4 steps back; all visible keys are just an unordered set. So "man bites dog" and a reordering of the same visible tokens would still be indistinguishable within the allowed window.

The mask restricts the *shape* of attention (lower-triangular), but position information (sinusoidal, learned, RoPE, or ALiBi) is what supplies *ordering* within that triangle. Both are needed, and they are applied to the same `(seq, seq)` score matrix but do different jobs.

### Q16. How does RoPE interact with the KV cache during generation?

Cleanly — which is another reason it won. During autoregressive decoding you cache the K and V vectors of past tokens. With RoPE you apply the rotation **at the position each token is generated**, then store the *already-rotated* K in the cache. When a new token at position `n` computes its query `R(n) q`, it dots against the cached rotated keys `R(j) k_j`, and the relative-offset identity gives the correct `m - n` dependence for free — no need to re-rotate the whole cache each step.

Contrast with a naive absolute scheme that adds a position vector to the residual stream: you would have to be careful the cached representations already include the right position, and re-indexing on context extension is awkward. RoPE's per-token rotation is applied once, cached, and composes correctly with every future query. This is why RoPE, KV caching, and FlashAttention all coexist smoothly in production inference. (See the KV-cache discussion in the generation topics for the memory side of this.)

## Attention: Query, Key, Value

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

This is the heart of the transformer. It covers the three learned projections — **Query, Key, Value** — that every token produces (`Q = x @ W_q`, `K = x @ W_k`, `V = x @ W_v`), and the single formula that defines attention: `softmax(Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k)) @ V`. It builds the intuition (Query asks, Key advertises, dot product scores relevance, softmax makes weights, output is a **weighted average of Values** — a soft, content-based lookup), works through every **shape** in the computation, explains **why we divide by sqrt(d_k)**, and confronts the cost: the attention matrix is `(seq, seq)`, so attention is **O(n^2)** in sequence length. The 16 questions run from "what are Q, K, V" to "derive scaled dot-product attention and justify the scaling factor" and "why is attention quadratic and what fixes it." Master this topic and the rest of the transformer — masking, multi-head, the block — is assembly on top of it.

**Mental model**

Think of attention as a **soft dictionary lookup**. In a normal hash map you have keys, you look up one key, and you get its value. Attention softens every part of that. Each token emits a **Query** ("what information am I looking for?"), and every token also advertises a **Key** ("what information do I offer?") and holds a **Value** ("the information itself"). To decide how much token `i` should draw from token `j`, you take the **dot product** of `i`'s query with `j`'s key — a high dot product means "this key matches what I'm asking for." You do this for all pairs, getting a `(seq, seq)` score matrix, then **softmax each row** so the scores over all keys become weights that sum to 1. The output for token `i` is the **weighted average of all Values**, weighted by those attention weights. So instead of retrieving one value, you retrieve a blend, weighted by content-based relevance. Q, K, V are all just linear projections of the input — the model *learns*, via `W_q`, `W_k`, `W_v`, what to ask, what to advertise, and what to pass along.

**Key terms**

- **Query (Q)** — `x @ W_q`; what a token is looking for. Shape `(seq, d_k)`.
- **Key (K)** — `x @ W_k`; what a token advertises. Shape `(seq, d_k)`.
- **Value (V)** — `x @ W_v`; the content a token contributes. Shape `(seq, d_v)`.
- **Attention scores** — `Q @ K^T`, the raw relevance between every query-key pair. Shape `(seq, seq)`.
- **Scaling** — dividing scores by `sqrt(d_k)` to keep them from growing with dimension.
- **Softmax** — turns each row of scores into a probability distribution (weights sum to 1).
- **Attention weights** — the post-softmax `(seq, seq)` matrix; how much each token attends to each other token.
- **Weighted average of Values** — the output; `weights @ V`, shape `(seq, d_v)`.
- **d_k** — the per-head key/query dimension; the scaling denominator is `sqrt(d_k)`.
- **O(n^2)** — attention's cost, quadratic in sequence length because the score matrix is `(seq, seq)`.
- **Content-based lookup** — attention retrieves by matching content (dot product), not by fixed position.

**Why interviewers ask this**

If there is one thing an LLM interviewer will make you derive on a whiteboard, it is scaled dot-product attention. It is the load-bearing idea of the whole field, so it cleanly separates people who *use* transformers from people who *understand* them. A junior answer recites the formula. A senior answer explains *why each piece is there*: why three separate projections instead of one, why a dot product measures relevance, why softmax and not just normalisation, why the `sqrt(d_k)` factor specifically (variance control to avoid saturating softmax and killing gradients), and what the shapes are at each step. The quadratic-cost follow-up ("why is long context expensive, and what fixes it?") ties directly to real production constraints — FlashAttention, GQA, the KV cache. Getting attention right is the price of admission for any serious LLM role.

**Common confusions**

- "Q, K, V are different inputs." No — in self-attention they are three **linear projections of the same input** `x`, via `W_q`, `W_k`, `W_v`.
- "Attention outputs the max-scoring value." No — it outputs a **weighted average** of all values (softmax is soft, not argmax).
- "The scaling factor is a minor detail." It is not — without `/sqrt(d_k)`, dot products grow with dimension, softmax saturates, and gradients vanish; training degrades badly.
- "Softmax is applied over the whole matrix." No — softmax is **per row** (per query), so each token's attention weights over the keys sum to 1.
- "Attention is O(n)." It is **O(n^2)** — the score matrix is `(seq, seq)`; doubling the context quadruples the attention compute and memory.

**What follows from this topic**

The projections and the score matrix are exactly what **Positional Encodings** modifies — RoPE rotates Q and K, ALiBi biases the scores. The `(seq, seq)` matrix is where **causal masking** sets the upper triangle to -inf (next topic). Splitting Q/K/V into `h` heads gives **multi-head attention**, and wrapping attention in a residual + norm gives the **transformer block**. The O(n^2) cost drives the entire efficiency story — FlashAttention, GQA/MQA, and the KV cache — in the architecture topics. Everything downstream is attention plus scaffolding.

### Q1. Derive scaled dot-product attention from scratch. What is each Q, K, V and why three of them?

Start from what we want: for each token, produce a new representation that is a **content-weighted blend of the other tokens' information**. Build it in steps.

1. **Project the input three ways.** Given input `x` of shape `(seq, d_model)`, learn three matrices and produce:

```text
Q = x @ W_q    # (seq, d_k)  what each token is asking for
K = x @ W_k    # (seq, d_k)  what each token offers
V = x @ W_v    # (seq, d_v)  what each token will contribute
```

Three projections because the roles are different: the thing you *search with* (Q), the thing you *match against* (K), and the thing you *retrieve* (V) should live in different learned spaces. Using one vector for all three would force "what I look for" to equal "what I advertise" to equal "what I contribute" — far less expressive.

2. **Score relevance** with a dot product of every query against every key:

```text
scores = Q @ K^T           # (seq, seq); scores[i,j] = q_i . k_j
```

A large dot product means query `i` aligns with key `j`.

3. **Scale** by `sqrt(d_k)` (justified in Q4) to control variance:

```text
scores = scores / sqrt(d_k)
```

4. **Softmax each row** so token `i`'s weights over all keys form a distribution summing to 1:

```text
weights = softmax(scores, axis=-1)   # (seq, seq)
```

5. **Weighted average of Values**:

```text
out = weights @ V          # (seq, d_v)
```

Putting it together: `Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k)) @ V`. Each output row is a soft, content-based lookup over the whole sequence.

### Q2. Write the full formula and annotate every shape.

```text
Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax( Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k) ) @ V
```

With batch and heads folded out for clarity (single head):

```text
x        : (seq, d_model)          input
W_q,W_k  : (d_model, d_k)          query/key projections
W_v      : (d_model, d_v)          value projection

Q = x @ W_q   -> (seq, d_k)
K = x @ W_k   -> (seq, d_k)
V = x @ W_v   -> (seq, d_v)

Q @ K^T                 -> (seq, seq)     # every query dotted with every key
/ sqrt(d_k)             -> (seq, seq)     # scale, shape unchanged
softmax(.., axis=-1)    -> (seq, seq)     # row-wise weights, each row sums to 1
weights @ V             -> (seq, d_v)     # weighted average of values
```

So attention maps `(seq, d_model)` in to `(seq, d_v)` out, passing through a `(seq, seq)` score/weight matrix in the middle. That middle matrix is the quadratic term. Usually `d_k = d_v = d_model / h` per head, and an output projection `W_o` maps the concatenated heads back to `d_model`.

### Q3. Explain the "soft dictionary lookup" intuition. Why is the output a weighted average of Values?

A hard lookup: given a key, return exactly its value. Attention softens every step so it is **differentiable** and can blend.

- The **Query** is your search request: "I'm a verb looking for my subject."
- Each **Key** advertises: "I'm a noun," "I'm punctuation," "I'm an adjective."
- The **dot product** `q_i . k_j` scores how well key `j` matches query `i` — high when the request and the advertisement align.
- **Softmax** turns the row of scores into weights that sum to 1: it's like saying "put 70% of my attention on token 3, 20% on token 1, 10% spread over the rest."
- The output is `sum_j weight_ij * v_j` — the **weighted average of Values**. You don't pick one token's value; you pull a *blend*, dominated by the most relevant tokens but including a little of everything.

It is a weighted average (not an argmax pick) precisely because softmax is soft. That softness is what makes attention trainable by gradient descent — a hard max would have zero gradient almost everywhere. It also lets a token gather from *several* relevant sources at once, which is what real language understanding needs.

### Q4. Why divide by sqrt(d_k)? Walk through the variance argument.

Because unscaled dot products **grow with dimension**, and large scores saturate softmax into a near one-hot distribution where gradients vanish.

Suppose the components of `q` and `k` are independent, mean 0, variance 1. Their dot product is a sum of `d_k` such products:

```text
q . k = sum_{i=1..d_k} q_i * k_i
```

Each term has variance ~1, and they're independent, so the sum has variance `~d_k` and standard deviation `~sqrt(d_k)`. As `d_k` grows (say 64, 128), the raw scores swing over a range of roughly `+/- sqrt(d_k)` — tens in magnitude.

Feed scores that large into softmax and it saturates: one entry goes to ~1, the rest to ~0. The problem is the **gradient of softmax is tiny in the saturated region** — `p(1-p)` near 0 when `p` is near 0 or 1 — so the model can barely learn; this is the vanishing-gradient failure mode.

Dividing by `sqrt(d_k)` rescales the scores back to **unit variance** regardless of dimension:

```text
Var(q . k / sqrt(d_k)) ~ d_k / d_k = 1
```

Now softmax gets inputs of a sane magnitude, stays in its responsive region, and gradients flow. That is the entire justification — it's variance normalisation so the softmax doesn't get pushed into saturation as head dimension scales.

### Q5. Implement scaled dot-product attention in a few lines.

```python
import numpy as np

def softmax(z, axis=-1):
    z = z - z.max(axis=axis, keepdims=True)     # numerical stability
    e = np.exp(z)
    return e / e.sum(axis=axis, keepdims=True)

def attention(x, W_q, W_k, W_v, mask=None):
    Q = x @ W_q                                 # (seq, d_k)
    K = x @ W_k                                 # (seq, d_k)
    V = x @ W_v                                 # (seq, d_v)
    d_k = Q.shape[-1]
    scores = Q @ K.T / np.sqrt(d_k)             # (seq, seq)
    if mask is not None:
        scores = np.where(mask, scores, -np.inf)  # causal / padding mask
    weights = softmax(scores, axis=-1)          # (seq, seq), rows sum to 1
    return weights @ V                          # (seq, d_v)
```

That is the entire mechanism. Everything else in a transformer — multi-head, residuals, norm, the FFN — wraps around this. Note the two production-critical details already present: subtracting the row max inside softmax (numerical stability) and the optional `mask` that sets forbidden scores to -inf before softmax.

### Q6. Why is attention O(n^2)? What exactly grows quadratically?

The **score matrix** `Q @ K^T` has shape `(seq, seq)` — one entry for every ordered pair of tokens. For a sequence of length `n`:

```text
Q: (n, d_k)   K: (n, d_k)
Q @ K^T -> (n, n)        # n*n entries
softmax over (n, n)
(n, n) @ V(n, d_v) -> (n, d_v)
```

Both the **compute** (forming and using the n-by-n matrix costs ~n^2 * d) and the **memory** (storing the n-by-n weights) scale with n^2. Double the context from 2k to 4k and attention does ~4x the work and holds ~4x the matrix. This is why long context is expensive and why so much engineering targets it.

Fixes fall into two camps: (1) **exact but IO-efficient** — FlashAttention never materialises the full n-by-n matrix in slow memory, tiling the computation so it stays O(n^2) in flops but drastically cheaper in memory traffic; (2) **approximate / sub-quadratic** — sliding-window/local attention (attend only to nearby tokens), sparse attention, and linear-attention variants trade some exactness for O(n) or O(n log n). For generation specifically, the **KV cache** avoids recomputing past keys/values each step. These are covered in the efficiency topics.

### Q7. In self-attention, where do Q, K, and V come from? Correct the misconception that they're separate inputs.

They come from the **same input**. In self-attention, one sequence `x` of shape `(seq, d_model)` is projected three ways by three learned matrices:

```text
Q = x @ W_q
K = x @ W_k
V = x @ W_v
```

Same `x`, three different weight matrices. So "Query," "Key," and "Value" are not three separate data streams — they are three *learned views* of the identical token representations. That is exactly why it is called **self**-attention: the sequence attends to itself.

The misconception usually comes from cross-attention (next topic), where Q genuinely comes from one sequence (the decoder) and K, V from another (the encoder). But in the self-attention that makes up a GPT, all three trace back to the same residual-stream `x`. What differs is only the projection matrices, which the model learns so that "what I ask," "what I advertise," and "what I contribute" can be different functions of the same token.

### Q8. What does the attention matrix look like and how do you read a row vs a column?

After softmax you have a `(seq, seq)` matrix `A` where `A[i, j]` = how much token `i` attends to token `j`.

```text
          key j ->
        the  cat  sat  on
row i the [.7  .1  .1  .1]   <- row sums to 1 (token "the"'s attention)
    cat [.2  .6  .1  .1]
    sat [.1  .3  .5  .1]
     on [.1  .1  .3  .5]
```

- **Read a row** = "where does token `i` look?" Row `i` is token `i`'s query distribution over all keys; it **sums to 1** (softmax is row-wise). This is the usual interpretation.
- **Read a column** = "who looks at token `j`?" Column `j` shows how much each token attends *to* `j`; columns do **not** sum to 1.

Under causal masking (next topic) the matrix is **lower-triangular** — the upper-right (future) entries are zero. Different heads produce different such matrices, and inspecting them is how researchers find "induction heads," positional heads, and syntax heads.

### Q9. What would break if you removed the softmax and just used raw scores as weights?

Several things:

1. **Weights wouldn't be normalised.** Raw `Q @ K^T` rows don't sum to 1, so the "weighted average" would actually be an arbitrary linear combination whose scale drifts with sequence length and content — the output magnitude would explode or shrink unpredictably, destabilising the residual stream.
2. **No competition between keys.** Softmax's exponential makes attention *selective* — a slightly higher score gets a disproportionately higher weight, so the model can focus. Linear weights spread attention flatly and make sharp, decisive lookups impossible.
3. **Negative and unbounded weights.** Raw scores can be negative or huge; "averaging" with negative weights isn't a meaningful convex blend of Values.
4. **Masking gets awkward.** The clean "-inf -> exp -> 0" trick for causal masking relies on softmax; without it you'd need ad hoc zeroing.

Softmax gives a **normalised, non-negative, competitive** weighting — a proper convex combination of Values — which is exactly what a differentiable lookup needs.

### Q10. Why project into Q, K, V at all — why not just take dot products of the raw embeddings?

Because raw embeddings conflate three roles that need to be different. If you dotted embeddings directly, "how much A attends to B" would be forced to be *symmetric* and tied to raw token similarity — token A looks for token B exactly as much as B looks for A, and only similar-meaning tokens could attend to each other.

The learned projections break that:

- `W_q` and `W_k` are **different**, so `q_i . k_j != q_j . k_i` in general — attention is **asymmetric**. A verb can strongly attend to its subject without the subject attending back equally. This asymmetry is essential for syntax.
- The Q/K space can be a *relationship* space, unrelated to semantic similarity: two tokens that aren't similar in meaning (a pronoun and its antecedent) can still be made to attend via learned projections.
- `W_v` lets the **content passed along** differ from the content used for matching — you match on one aspect, retrieve another.

So the projections give the model the freedom to learn *what to match on* and *what to move*, independently, and asymmetrically. Raw-embedding dot products would be a crippled special case.

### Q11. What are d_k and d_v, and can they differ?

- **d_k** is the dimension of the Query and Key vectors — they must match because you dot them together (`q . k` requires equal length). It is also the scaling denominator: `/ sqrt(d_k)`.
- **d_v** is the dimension of the Value vectors, and hence of the attention output before the output projection.

They **can** differ in principle — nothing forces `d_v = d_k`, because V is only ever weighted-summed, never dotted with Q. In practice most implementations set `d_k = d_v = d_model / h` for a clean multi-head split and so the concatenated heads recompose to `d_model`. Keeping them equal is a convention, not a requirement; some architectures do use `d_v != d_k`.

### Q12. Trace a single query vector through attention. What does token 3 actually compute?

Take token 3's query `q_3` (shape `(d_k,)`). Its output is computed as:

```text
1. score against every key:
   s = [ q_3.k_0, q_3.k_1, q_3.k_2, q_3.k_3, ... ]     # length seq

2. scale:
   s = s / sqrt(d_k)

3. (if causal) mask the future:
   s[j] = -inf for j > 3

4. softmax -> weights w (length seq, sums to 1, zeros on future):
   w = softmax(s)

5. weighted sum of values:
   out_3 = sum_j w[j] * v_j        # shape (d_v,)
```

So token 3 asks its question `q_3`, measures how well every visible token's key answers it, converts those into a probability distribution, and returns a blend of those tokens' Values. If `q_3` matches `k_1` strongly, `out_3` will be dominated by `v_1`. That single vector `out_3` then goes into the residual stream and on to the FFN. Repeat for all positions in parallel — that parallelism over positions is the transformer's speed advantage over RNNs.

### Q13. How do multiple heads change the picture, and why is that better than one big head?

Multi-head attention runs `h` attention computations in **parallel**, each with its own smaller `W_q, W_k, W_v` of dimension `d_k = d_model / h`, then **concatenates** the `h` outputs and applies an output projection `W_o`:

```text
head_i = Attention(x @ W_q_i, x @ W_k_i, x @ W_v_i)   # (seq, d_model/h)
out    = concat(head_1, ..., head_h) @ W_o            # (seq, d_model)
```

Why it beats a single `d_model`-wide head: a single softmax can only express **one** attention pattern per position — one blend. Splitting into heads lets the model attend to **different things simultaneously** — one head tracks the subject-verb link, another tracks the previous token, another tracks long-range coreference. Each head has a lower-dimensional, more focused subspace to match in.

Crucially the **total compute is about the same** as one big head (the dimensions split, they don't multiply), but the *expressiveness* is much higher because you get `h` independent attention patterns that are then mixed by `W_o`. This is one of the key insights of the original transformer.

### Q14. Compare attention to an RNN. Why did attention win for sequence modelling?

```text
RNN:            h_t = f(h_{t-1}, x_t)      # sequential, one step at a time
Attention:      out = softmax(QK^T/sqrt(d_k)) V   # all positions at once
```

| | RNN | Self-attention |
|---|---|---|
| Path between two tokens | O(distance) steps | O(1) — direct |
| Parallelism over time | None (sequential) | Full (all positions at once) |
| Long-range dependencies | Fade (vanishing gradient) | Direct, constant-path |
| Cost per layer | O(n * d^2) | O(n^2 * d) |

RNNs process tokens one at a time, so information from token 1 must survive being passed through hundreds of recurrent steps to reach token 500 — it decays (vanishing gradients), and training can't be parallelised across the sequence. Attention gives every token a **direct, constant-length path** to every other token via the score matrix, so long-range dependencies are as easy as short-range ones, and the whole sequence is processed **in parallel** — a huge win on GPUs. The price is O(n^2) cost, but that parallelism is exactly what made training on trillions of tokens feasible. That trade — quadratic cost for parallelism and direct long-range paths — is why the transformer displaced the RNN.

### Q15. What is the residual/output projection W_o for, after attention?

After multi-head attention you `concat` the `h` head outputs into a `d_model`-wide vector, then multiply by `W_o` (shape `(d_model, d_model)`):

```text
out = concat(head_1, ..., head_h) @ W_o
```

`W_o` does two jobs: (1) it **mixes** information across heads — concatenation alone keeps heads in separate slices, and `W_o` lets the model combine "what head 1 found" with "what head 3 found" into a unified representation; (2) it maps the concatenated output back into the `d_model` residual-stream space so it can be **added to the residual** (`x = x + MHA(...)`). Without `W_o` the heads would remain siloed and the output wouldn't be in the right space to re-enter the stream. It is a small but essential part of the multi-head design.

### Q16. Given d_model and h, what are the parameter and compute costs of one attention layer?

**Parameters.** Four projections, each `(d_model, d_model)` (the per-head matrices stacked across all heads recombine to full `d_model`):

```text
W_q, W_k, W_v, W_o  each ~ d_model * d_model
total ~ 4 * d_model^2   (plus small biases)
```

So attention holds roughly `4 * d_model^2` parameters per layer — independent of `h`, since splitting into heads partitions the same matrices rather than adding new ones. (For comparison, the FFN holds ~`8 * d_model^2`, which is why the FFN dominates the parameter count.)

**Compute** for sequence length `n`:

```text
projections:   ~ n * d_model^2      (linear in n)
scores QK^T:   ~ n^2 * d_model      (quadratic in n)
weights @ V:   ~ n^2 * d_model      (quadratic in n)
```

For short sequences the `n * d_model^2` projection term dominates; for long sequences the `n^2 * d_model` attention term takes over — which is the O(n^2) wall that FlashAttention, sliding-window attention, and GQA/MQA all attack. The per-head dimension `d_k = d_model / h` doesn't change these totals, only how the work is partitioned.

## Self-Attention, Cross-Attention & Causal Masking

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

Three closely-related mechanisms built on the Q/K/V attention from the previous topic. **Self-attention**: Q, K, V all come from the same sequence (a token attends to its own context) — the engine of both BERT and GPT. **Cross-attention**: Q comes from one sequence (a decoder) while K and V come from another (an encoder) — the bridge in encoder-decoder models like translation. And **causal / autoregressive masking**: the trick that stops a token from attending to the future by setting the upper-triangular scores to **-inf before softmax**, so those weights become exactly 0. The topic contrasts **bidirectional (BERT)** attention, where every token sees the whole sequence, with **causal (GPT)** attention, where each token sees only the past, and explains **why decoder-only LLMs must be causal** (next-token prediction would be trivially cheatable otherwise). The 16 questions span from "what's the difference between self- and cross-attention" to "why exactly -inf and not a large negative number" and "why can't a token see the future." These are the mechanisms that turn generic attention into a working language model.

**Mental model**

Everything here is about **who is allowed to attend to whom**. Start with the `(seq, seq)` score matrix from the previous topic. Three questions determine the model's character. First, *where do Q, K, V come from?* Same sequence -> self-attention; Q from a decoder and K/V from an encoder -> cross-attention. Second, *is the future visible?* If a token may attend both left and right, it is **bidirectional** (BERT), good for understanding a complete input. If a token may attend only to itself and earlier tokens, it is **causal** (GPT), required for generation. Third, *how do we enforce "only the past"?* We add a mask to the scores before softmax that sets every future position to **-inf**; after softmax those become exp(-inf) = 0, so no attention weight leaks backward from the future. The mask is just a lower-triangular pattern layered onto the same score matrix that positional encodings also touch. Get these three switches right and you can describe any transformer — BERT, GPT, or T5 — as the same attention machine with different wiring.

**Key terms**

- **Self-attention** — Q, K, V from the same sequence; tokens attend within one input.
- **Cross-attention** — Q from the decoder, K/V from the encoder; the decoder reads the source.
- **Causal / autoregressive mask** — a lower-triangular constraint; token `i` may attend only to positions `<= i`.
- **-inf before softmax** — how the mask is applied: set forbidden scores to -inf so their post-softmax weight is 0.
- **Bidirectional** — every token attends both left and right (BERT); no mask.
- **Causal (unidirectional)** — every token attends only to the past (GPT); lower-triangular mask.
- **Encoder-only** — BERT-style; bidirectional, for understanding/classification.
- **Decoder-only** — GPT-style; causal, for generation. Modern LLMs.
- **Encoder-decoder** — T5/original transformer; encoder (bidirectional) + decoder (causal) linked by cross-attention.
- **Padding mask** — masks out padding tokens in batched variable-length inputs (distinct from the causal mask).
- **Information leakage** — a token seeing the answer (its own future), which makes next-token training trivial and useless.

**Why interviewers ask this**

This topic is where "I know the attention formula" becomes "I understand how a language model is actually trained and why." The signature question — "why can't a token see the future during training?" — tests whether you understand next-token prediction as a *self-supervised* objective that only works if the label (the next token) is hidden from the input. A junior often says "the mask is for efficiency" or can't explain why -inf specifically. A senior explains the leakage problem, the -inf-before-softmax mechanism, why it must be *before* softmax, and the clean contrast between BERT (bidirectional, masked-language-model objective) and GPT (causal, next-token objective). Cross-attention questions probe whether you understand encoder-decoder architectures and can place modern decoder-only LLMs in that family tree. It is a favourite because a confident, correct answer demonstrates end-to-end understanding of how attention becomes a trainable predictor.

**Common confusions**

- "The causal mask is a performance optimisation." No — it is a **correctness** requirement; without it, next-token training leaks the answer and the model learns nothing useful.
- "Masking removes future tokens from the input." No — all tokens are still processed in parallel; the mask only zeroes the **attention weights** to future positions.
- "Use a large negative number instead of -inf." Approximately fine, but -inf gives *exactly* 0 after softmax; a finite value leaves a tiny nonzero leak, and the standard is -inf (or the dtype's most-negative value).
- "GPT and BERT use the same attention." Same formula, different masking: GPT is causal (masked), BERT is bidirectional (unmasked). That single switch changes everything about how they're trained and used.
- "Cross-attention and self-attention are different formulas." Same scaled-dot-product formula; only the *source* of Q vs K/V differs.

**What follows from this topic**

The mask is applied to the same `(seq, seq)` score matrix that **Attention: Query, Key, Value** builds and that **Positional Encodings** biases — masking (who you may see) and position (where they are) are orthogonal operations on that matrix. The causal setup is what makes **autoregressive generation** and the **KV cache** possible (each new token attends over cached past keys/values). The bidirectional-vs-causal distinction underpins the base-model and pretraining topics: GPT's next-token objective is a direct consequence of causal masking. This is the last piece of the core attention machinery before assembling the full transformer block.

### Q1. What is the difference between self-attention and cross-attention?

Both use the identical scaled-dot-product formula. The difference is **where Q, K, and V come from**.

- **Self-attention**: all three are projections of the *same* sequence. A token attends to other tokens in its own input. Used in every layer of BERT and GPT.

```text
Q = x @ W_q,  K = x @ W_k,  V = x @ W_v      # same x
```

- **Cross-attention**: the **Query comes from one sequence, the Keys and Values from another**. The classic case is an encoder-decoder translator: the decoder (generating the target) queries the encoder's representation of the source sentence.

```text
Q = decoder_x @ W_q                          # from the decoder
K = encoder_x @ W_k,  V = encoder_x @ W_v     # from the encoder
```

Intuition: in cross-attention the decoder asks "which source words are relevant to the target word I'm about to produce?" — Q is the decoder's question, K/V are the source's advertised content. Self-attention is a sequence talking to itself; cross-attention is one sequence reading another. Decoder-only LLMs (GPT) use only self-attention; encoder-decoder models (T5, original transformer) use self-attention in each stack plus cross-attention linking them.

### Q2. Why can't a token attend to the future during training? Explain the leakage problem.

Because the training objective is **next-token prediction**: at every position `i`, the model must predict token `i+1` using only tokens `0..i`. That token `i+1` is simultaneously the **label** for position `i` and an **input** at position `i+1`. If position `i` were allowed to attend to position `i+1`, it could just **copy the answer** — the task becomes trivial and the model learns nothing about language, only "peek to the right."

```text
input:   the  cat  sat  on   the
target:  cat  sat  on   the  mat
                ^ predicting "sat" at this position
                  must NOT see "sat"/"on"/... to its right
```

This is **information leakage**: the future contains the labels, so it must be hidden from the input at each position. The causal mask enforces exactly this — position `i` may attend only to `0..i`. It is not an efficiency trick; it is what makes the self-supervised objective *meaningful*. Remove the mask and training loss would plummet to near zero while the model learned nothing generalisable, and at inference (where the future genuinely doesn't exist yet) it would collapse.

### Q3. How is causal masking implemented? Why -inf before softmax specifically?

You add a mask to the scores that sets every "future" entry to **-inf**, *before* the softmax:

```python
import numpy as np

def causal_mask(seq):
    # True where attention is ALLOWED (j <= i), lower triangular
    return np.tril(np.ones((seq, seq), dtype=bool))

def masked_attention(Q, K, V):
    d_k = Q.shape[-1]
    scores = Q @ K.T / np.sqrt(d_k)            # (seq, seq)
    mask = causal_mask(scores.shape[0])
    scores = np.where(mask, scores, -np.inf)   # future -> -inf
    weights = softmax(scores, axis=-1)         # exp(-inf) = 0
    return weights @ V
```

Why -inf, and why before softmax: softmax computes `exp(score) / sum(exp(scores))`. Setting a forbidden score to **-inf** makes `exp(-inf) = 0`, so that position gets **exactly zero** weight and — crucially — it also drops out of the denominator's sum, so the remaining (allowed) weights still renormalise to sum to 1. If you instead zeroed the weights *after* softmax, the row would no longer sum to 1 and you'd have to renormalise by hand; doing it as -inf before softmax makes the normalisation automatic and clean. It must be before softmax for exactly this reason.

### Q4. Show what the causal attention matrix looks like after masking.

Before softmax, the future (upper triangle, strictly above the diagonal) is set to -inf. After softmax those entries become 0, leaving a **lower-triangular** weight matrix:

```text
scores after masking (-inf = X):        weights after softmax:

      k0  k1  k2  k3                        k0   k1   k2   k3
 q0 [ s   X   X   X ]                  q0 [ 1.0  0    0    0  ]
 q1 [ s   s   X   X ]        --->      q1 [ .4   .6   0    0  ]
 q2 [ s   s   s   X ]                  q2 [ .2   .3   .5   0  ]
 q3 [ s   s   s   s ]                  q3 [ .1   .2   .3   .4 ]
```

Each row still sums to 1, but all mass sits on the diagonal and to its left (the past and present). Token 0 can only attend to itself (weight 1.0). Token 3 can attend to 0-3. Nothing attends forward. This triangular structure is the visual signature of a causal / decoder-only model; a bidirectional (BERT) model's matrix is fully populated with no zeroed triangle.

### Q5. Contrast bidirectional (BERT) and causal (GPT) attention. Why does each suit its task?

| | Bidirectional (BERT) | Causal (GPT) |
|---|---|---|
| Mask | None | Lower-triangular |
| A token sees | Whole sequence (left + right) | Only itself + past |
| Architecture | Encoder-only | Decoder-only |
| Training objective | Masked language modelling (fill blanks) | Next-token prediction |
| Best at | Understanding, classification, embeddings | Generation |
| Generation | Not autoregressive | Autoregressive |

**BERT** masks out ~15% of tokens and asks the model to fill them in using **both** surrounding sides — so full bidirectional context is exactly what the task needs, and there's no leakage because the target tokens are *removed* from the input (replaced with [MASK]). This makes BERT great for understanding a complete input (sentiment, NER, retrieval embeddings) but it can't naturally *generate* text left to right.

**GPT** predicts the next token, so it must be **causal** — seeing the future would leak the answer. That same constraint means GPT is inherently autoregressive: it can generate by sampling the next token and feeding it back. Understanding suffers slightly (each token sees less context) but generation becomes natural. Modern LLMs are decoder-only causal because generation is the goal.

### Q6. Why are modern LLMs decoder-only and causal rather than encoder-decoder?

The original transformer was **encoder-decoder** (for translation: encode source, decode target with cross-attention). But for general-purpose generative LLMs the field converged on **decoder-only, causal**:

1. **The task is generation.** Next-token prediction over huge unlabelled text is the pretraining objective, and it requires causal masking. A decoder-only stack does this with a single, uniform architecture — no separate encoder.
2. **Simplicity and scale.** One stack of identical causal blocks is easier to scale, shard, and optimise than two stacks plus cross-attention. Every parameter does the same kind of work.
3. **In-context learning.** A causal decoder naturally treats the prompt as prefix context — "the input" and "the output" are just earlier and later tokens in the same stream — which is what makes few-shot prompting work without architectural changes.
4. **Data efficiency.** Every token position is a training signal (predict the next one), so a decoder-only model learns from every token of every document.

Encoder-decoder still wins for some seq-to-seq tasks (T5 for translation/summarisation with a clear source/target split), but decoder-only (GPT, LLaMA, etc.) dominates general LLMs because a single causal stack scales and generates cleanly.

### Q7. Is a large negative number (e.g. -1e9) equivalent to -inf for masking?

Nearly, and it's what most real implementations use for numerical safety — but there's a subtle difference. `exp(-1e9)` underflows to 0 in floating point, so in practice a masked position gets weight ~0 and the row renormalises correctly, just like -inf. Frameworks often use the dtype's most-negative representable value (e.g. `torch.finfo(dtype).min`) rather than literal -inf to avoid `-inf` propagating into NaNs during some operations (e.g. if an *entire* row were masked, `softmax(-inf, -inf, ...)` produces 0/0 = NaN, whereas a finite floor degrades more gracefully).

So: conceptually -inf is the "correct" value (gives exactly 0 weight and exact renormalisation); practically a large finite negative is used to sidestep NaN edge cases, and the numerical result is indistinguishable for any position that has at least one visible key. The one thing you must *not* do is use a merely "somewhat negative" value like -10, which would leave a real, nonzero leak of attention to the future.

### Q8. Walk through a masked-attention example end to end with small numbers.

Three tokens, causal. Suppose after `Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k)` the raw scores are:

```text
        k0    k1    k2
 q0 [  2.0   1.0   3.0 ]      # q0 may see only k0
 q1 [  0.5   2.0   1.0 ]      # q1 may see k0,k1
 q2 [  1.0   0.0   2.0 ]      # q2 may see k0,k1,k2
```

Apply the causal mask (set future to -inf):

```text
        k0     k1     k2
 q0 [  2.0   -inf   -inf ]
 q1 [  0.5    2.0   -inf ]
 q2 [  1.0    0.0    2.0 ]
```

Softmax each row (future terms -> exp(-inf)=0, drop out of the sum):

```text
 q0: softmax([2.0])            = [1.00, 0,    0   ]
 q1: softmax([0.5, 2.0])       = [0.18, 0.82, 0   ]
 q2: softmax([1.0, 0.0, 2.0])  = [0.24, 0.09, 0.67]
```

Each row sums to 1 over only its allowed keys. Then `weights @ V` gives each token's output as a blend of the Values it was permitted to see. Token 0 is forced to attend only to itself; token 2 freely blends all three. No future information reaches any earlier token.

### Q9. How does causal masking connect to autoregressive generation and the KV cache?

Causal masking is what *makes* autoregressive generation coherent. Because during training every position `i` only ever saw `0..i`, the model's learned behaviour is exactly "predict the next token from the past." At inference you exploit this: generate token by token, each new token attending only over the tokens produced so far — which is precisely the causal pattern the model was trained under. Training and inference match.

```text
generate: run forward -> take LAST position's logits -> sample next token
          -> append it -> run forward again -> repeat
```

The **KV cache** falls out naturally: since a past token's Key and Value never change (its representation doesn't depend on future tokens — that's the causal property), you compute them once and cache them. Each new token computes only *its own* Q, K, V and attends over the cached K/V of all previous tokens, turning per-step cost from re-encoding the whole prefix (O(n^2)) into ~O(n) work per token. Without causal masking, a token's representation would depend on future tokens and this caching would be impossible. (The cache mechanics and prefill-vs-decode are covered in the generation topics.)

### Q10. What is a padding mask and how does it differ from the causal mask?

They solve different problems and are often combined.

- **Causal mask**: prevents attending to the **future**. Lower-triangular, depends only on position, same for every example in a batch.
- **Padding mask**: prevents attending to **padding tokens**. When you batch sequences of different lengths, you pad the short ones to a common length with a `[PAD]` token; those positions carry no real information and must be masked out so they don't pollute the attention of real tokens. It depends on **each example's actual length**, so it differs across the batch.

```text
sequence:  [ the  cat  sat  PAD  PAD ]
pad mask:  [  1    1    1    0    0  ]   # 0 = disallow attending to it
```

In a decoder LLM you combine both: a position may attend to `j` only if `j` is in the past (causal) **and** `j` is a real token (padding). Both are applied the same way — set disallowed scores to -inf before softmax. The causal mask is about *time*; the padding mask is about *validity of the token*.

### Q11. Does cross-attention use a causal mask?

Generally **no** on the encoder side. In cross-attention the decoder's Query attends over the **encoder's Keys/Values**, and the encoder has already processed the *entire* source sequence (it is bidirectional). There is no "future to leak" in the source — the whole source is legitimately available when generating any target token — so cross-attention over the encoder is **unmasked** (except padding).

The causality lives in the decoder's **self-attention**: when generating the target sequence, the decoder must not see future *target* tokens, so its self-attention is causally masked. So an encoder-decoder model has three attention flavours: encoder self-attention (bidirectional), decoder self-attention (causal), and decoder-to-encoder cross-attention (unmasked over the source). Mixing these up — e.g. claiming cross-attention is causal — is a common error.

### Q12. Spot the bug: a training loop where the model reaches near-zero loss almost immediately.

```python
scores = Q @ K.T / np.sqrt(d_k)
# bug: no causal mask applied
weights = softmax(scores, axis=-1)
out = weights @ V
# ... next-token loss against shifted targets
```

The bug is the **missing causal mask**. Without it, self-attention is bidirectional, so at position `i` the model attends to position `i+1` — which holds the very token it's supposed to predict. It learns the degenerate "look one to the right and copy" solution, so training loss collapses to near zero almost instantly. Tell-tale signs: implausibly fast loss drop, and a model that generates gibberish at inference (where the future doesn't exist to copy from). The fix is to mask the future before softmax:

```python
mask = np.tril(np.ones((seq, seq), dtype=bool))
scores = np.where(mask, scores, -np.inf)
weights = softmax(scores, axis=-1)
```

Suspiciously low training loss in a decoder LM is almost always a masking (leakage) bug.

### Q13. In a decoder-only block, exactly which attention is present and how is it masked?

A decoder-only (GPT-style) block has **one** attention sublayer: **masked (causal) multi-head self-attention**. That's it — no cross-attention (there's no encoder) and no separate bidirectional pass.

```text
x = x + MHA_causal(LN(x))     # causal self-attention sublayer
x = x + FFN(LN(x))            # feed-forward sublayer
```

The causal mask (lower-triangular, -inf on the future before softmax) is applied inside every one of these self-attention sublayers, in every layer of the stack. Contrast: an encoder-only block (BERT) has one **unmasked** bidirectional self-attention sublayer; an encoder-decoder decoder block has **two** attention sublayers — causal self-attention *plus* cross-attention to the encoder. So "which attention and which mask" is a compact way to identify the architecture family. Modern LLMs = the single causal-self-attention block, stacked N times.

### Q14. How does the mask combine with positional encodings on the score matrix?

They are **orthogonal operations applied to the same `(seq, seq)` score matrix**, and it's clarifying to see them together:

```text
scores = Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k)          # base relevance
scores = scores + alibi_bias          # (if ALiBi) position: distance penalty
scores = where(causal_mask, scores, -inf)   # masking: hide the future
weights = softmax(scores, axis=-1)
```

- **Positional encoding** answers *where* a token is: RoPE rotates Q and K before the dot product; ALiBi adds a distance bias directly to the scores.
- **Masking** answers *whether you're allowed to see* a token: causal (future) and padding (invalid) constraints set scores to -inf.

They don't conflict — one shapes the *magnitude* of relevance by distance, the other zeroes out *forbidden* entries entirely. Both happen before softmax, on the same matrix. A clean mental model: build raw scores, add position bias, apply masks, softmax, weight the Values. Position says "how far," mask says "allowed or not."

### Q15. Why does masking happen before softmax and not after?

Because softmax **normalises**, and you want the forbidden positions excluded *from* that normalisation. Do it before:

```text
scores = [2.0, 1.0, -inf]
softmax -> [0.73, 0.27, 0]      # sums to 1 over the two allowed keys
```

The -inf term contributes `exp(-inf)=0` to the denominator, so the two allowed weights automatically renormalise to sum to 1. Correct and free.

Do it after and you break normalisation:

```text
scores = [2.0, 1.0, 0.5]
softmax -> [0.51, 0.19, 0.30]
zero the last -> [0.51, 0.19, 0]   # sums to 0.70, NOT 1
```

Now the row doesn't sum to 1 — the output is a mis-scaled blend, and you'd have to manually renormalise (divide by 0.70). Worse, the masked position already influenced the softmax denominator, subtly distorting the other weights. Masking before softmax makes the exclusion exact and the renormalisation automatic — that's the whole reason for the -inf-before-softmax idiom.

### Q16. Could you make GPT bidirectional? What would you gain and lose?

Mechanically yes — just drop the causal mask so every token attends both directions. But you would break the thing that makes GPT a generative LLM.

**What you'd lose:** next-token prediction training would leak (every position sees its own answer), so you could no longer pretrain with the autoregressive objective, and you could no longer generate autoregressively at inference (a bidirectional model needs the whole sequence up front, including the future you're trying to produce). You'd essentially have turned GPT into a BERT-style encoder.

**What you'd gain:** for pure *understanding* tasks on a complete, fixed input (classification, embeddings, extractive QA), bidirectional context lets each token see more and often gives better representations — which is exactly why BERT is bidirectional and excels at those.

The reason LLMs stay causal is that **generation is the goal**, and generation fundamentally requires that a token be predictable from its past alone. You can't have both open-ended left-to-right generation *and* full bidirectional context — the future you'd attend to doesn't exist yet when you're generating it. So the "limitation" of causal masking is inseparable from the capability of generation.
## Multi-Head Attention

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

This topic explains why a real transformer never uses a single attention operation — it runs **h attention heads in parallel**, each a full scaled-dot-product attention over its own smaller Query/Key/Value projections of dimension `d_k = d_model / h`. It covers the mechanics end to end: how `d_model` is split into `h` slices, how each head attends independently, how the heads are **concatenated** back to `d_model` width, and how a final output projection `W_o` mixes them. It covers the intuition — different heads specialise (one tracks the previous token, one tracks the subject-verb link, one does coreference, one copies positional patterns) — and the parameter accounting that shows multiple small heads cost the *same* total compute as one big head while being strictly more expressive. The 16 questions here assume you already understand single-head scaled-dot-product attention (from the Attention topic) and build the multi-head wrapper on top of it, ending with a runnable ```python sketch and the shapes at every step.

**Mental model**

One attention head is a single "conversation": every token asks one question (its Query) and blends the answers (Values) it finds most relevant. But a token relates to its context in *many* ways at once — grammatically, semantically, positionally. Forcing all of that through one 64-or-more-dimensional averaging channel is a bottleneck: the softmax can only spread attention one way per token. Multi-head attention gives the model `h` **independent** channels, each operating in its own low-dimensional subspace (`d_k = d_model/h`). Head 3 can point at the verb while head 7 points at the pronoun's antecedent, with no interference. The trick is that we don't make each head as wide as the model; we *split* `d_model` into `h` pieces, so `h * d_k = d_model` and the total FLOPs and parameters are essentially unchanged versus one full-width head. After each head produces its own `(seq, d_k)` output, we glue them side by side into `(seq, d_model)` and pass through `W_o` so the model can recombine the per-subspace results. More views of the same data, same compute budget.

**Key terms**

- **head** — one independent scaled-dot-product attention operating in a `d_k`-dimensional subspace.
- **h** — the number of heads (e.g. 12 in GPT-2 small, 32+ in large models).
- **d_k / d_v** — per-head key/query and value dimension, usually `d_model / h`.
- **d_model** — the model's residual-stream width; the input and output dimension of the whole MHA block.
- **W_q, W_k, W_v** — projection matrices `(d_model, d_model)` that produce all heads' Q/K/V at once, then get reshaped into heads.
- **W_o** — output projection `(d_model, d_model)` applied after concatenating heads.
- **concatenation** — stacking the `h` head outputs of width `d_v` back into a single `d_model` vector.
- **subspace** — the slice of representation space one head reads and writes.
- **head specialisation** — the empirical fact that trained heads take on distinct roles (previous-token, syntactic, positional, induction).
- **attention pattern** — a head's `(seq, seq)` softmax matrix; visualising it reveals its role.
- **induction head** — a head pair that implements copy/pattern-completion, central to in-context learning.

**Why interviewers ask this**

Multi-head attention is the single most-asked transformer question because it separates people who *memorised* "attention is all you need" from people who understand the shapes. A junior answer says "it has multiple heads so it learns more." A senior answer states the exact split (`d_k = d_model/h`), explains that heads are concatenated then projected by `W_o`, and can defend why `h` small heads cost the same as one big head — because the projection dimensions shrink proportionally. Interviewers probe whether you know the heads are computed as one batched matmul (not a Python loop), whether you can name what different heads learn, and whether you understand that MHA is the *only* place tokens exchange information in a transformer (the FFN is per-token). Getting the reshape/transpose right on a whiteboard is a strong signal you have actually implemented it.

**Common confusions**

- "Each head is as wide as the model" — no; each head is `d_model/h` wide, so the heads *partition* the width. Total compute is unchanged.
- "Heads are computed one at a time in a loop" — conceptually parallel, and in code they are one batched matmul over a `(batch, h, seq, d_k)` tensor.
- "More heads is always better" — beyond a point `d_k` gets too small to represent useful queries; there's a sweet spot, and GQA/MQA later *reduce* K/V heads on purpose.
- "Concatenation is the final step" — no; after concat you still apply `W_o`, which lets heads interact.
- "Each head has its own separate `W_q` matrix" — usually one `W_q` of shape `(d_model, d_model)` produces all heads, then you reshape; mathematically equivalent to `h` separate matrices.

**What follows from this topic**

Multi-head attention is the first of the two sublayers inside **The Transformer Block**, where it gets wrapped in a residual connection and layer norm and paired with the **Feed-Forward Network**. The per-head KV vectors are exactly what the **KV cache** stores during generation, and shrinking the number of K/V heads is what **GQA / MQA** do to make decoding cheaper. The `O(n^2)` cost of each head's `(seq, seq)` matrix is what **FlashAttention** and long-context tricks attack. Understanding the split here is the prerequisite for all of them.

### Q1. What is multi-head attention and why not just use one big attention head?

Multi-head attention runs **h independent scaled-dot-product attentions in parallel**, each on its own lower-dimensional projection of the input, then concatenates their outputs and mixes them with a projection `W_o`.

The reason for many small heads instead of one big one: a single attention operation produces, for each token, **one** softmax-weighted average of Values. That one distribution can only emphasise one kind of relationship at a time. Language needs several at once — a token's previous word, its syntactic head, a coreferent pronoun, a matching bracket. With `h` heads each token computes `h` separate attention patterns in `h` separate subspaces, so it can attend to the verb in one head and the subject in another without the two averaging into mush.

Crucially it costs the **same**: instead of one head of width `d_model`, you use `h` heads of width `d_k = d_model / h`. The projections shrink proportionally, so total parameters and FLOPs are essentially identical to a single full-width head — you get more expressive power for free.

### Q2. What is d_k and how does it relate to d_model and h?

`d_k` is the **per-head** dimension of the Query and Key vectors (and usually `d_v` for Values equals it). The standard choice is:

```text
d_k = d_model / h
```

So the total width is partitioned across heads: `h * d_k = d_model`. Example: GPT-2 small has `d_model = 768` and `h = 12`, giving `d_k = 64`. Each head's Q, K, V live in a 64-dim space; the 12 heads' 64-dim outputs concatenate back to 768.

This is why "more heads, same compute" holds — as `h` goes up, `d_k` goes down to keep the product fixed. The `sqrt(d_k)` in the attention score scaling uses this per-head `d_k` (64), not `d_model` (768).

### Q3. Walk through the exact shapes in multi-head attention, from input to output.

Take input `x` of shape `(batch, seq, d_model)`. Let `B, T, D = batch, seq, d_model`, and `h` heads with `d_k = D / h`.

```text
x                : (B, T, D)
Q = x @ W_q      : (B, T, D)          # W_q is (D, D)
K = x @ W_k      : (B, T, D)
V = x @ W_v      : (B, T, D)

# split the D dimension into h heads of size d_k, move heads next to batch
Q -> reshape/transpose : (B, h, T, d_k)
K -> reshape/transpose : (B, h, T, d_k)
V -> reshape/transpose : (B, h, T, d_k)

scores = Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k) : (B, h, T, T)   # per-head attention matrix
scores += causal_mask                          # -inf above the diagonal
A = softmax(scores, dim=-1)  : (B, h, T, T)
out = A @ V                  : (B, h, T, d_k)

# merge heads back
out -> transpose/reshape     : (B, T, D)
out = out @ W_o              : (B, T, D)        # W_o is (D, D)
```

The block is **shape-preserving**: `(B, T, D)` in, `(B, T, D)` out — which is exactly why identical blocks can stack. The only place the `(T, T)` cost appears is the per-head `scores` matrix, giving the `O(T^2)` term.

### Q4. Why do multiple small heads beat one large head if the compute is the same?

Because expressiveness comes from having **multiple independent attention distributions**, not from the width of any single one. One large head still outputs a single softmax-weighted average per token — one relationship. Splitting the same budget into `h` heads gives `h` separate `(seq, seq)` patterns and `h` separate value-mixing operations, each free to specialise. Empirically, trained heads take on distinct, interpretable jobs (previous-token heads, syntactic heads, induction heads). A single wide head cannot represent "attend to the verb AND separately attend to the antecedent" in one distribution; two narrow heads can. Same FLOPs, more views. The only cost is that each head is lower-dimensional (`d_k` small), so there's a floor below which heads become too narrow to form useful queries.

### Q5. Write a minimal multi-head self-attention module in PyTorch-ish code.

```python
import torch, torch.nn as nn, torch.nn.functional as F

class MultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, d_model, n_heads):
        super().__init__()
        assert d_model % n_heads == 0
        self.h = n_heads
        self.d_k = d_model // n_heads
        self.qkv = nn.Linear(d_model, 3 * d_model)  # fused W_q, W_k, W_v
        self.w_o = nn.Linear(d_model, d_model)

    def forward(self, x, causal=True):
        B, T, D = x.shape
        q, k, v = self.qkv(x).chunk(3, dim=-1)      # each (B, T, D)
        # (B, T, D) -> (B, h, T, d_k)
        q = q.view(B, T, self.h, self.d_k).transpose(1, 2)
        k = k.view(B, T, self.h, self.d_k).transpose(1, 2)
        v = v.view(B, T, self.h, self.d_k).transpose(1, 2)

        scores = q @ k.transpose(-2, -1) / self.d_k ** 0.5   # (B, h, T, T)
        if causal:
            mask = torch.triu(torch.ones(T, T, device=x.device), diagonal=1).bool()
            scores = scores.masked_fill(mask, float("-inf"))
        att = F.softmax(scores, dim=-1)
        out = att @ v                                 # (B, h, T, d_k)
        out = out.transpose(1, 2).reshape(B, T, D)    # merge heads
        return self.w_o(out)
```

The fused `qkv` linear and the `view/transpose` reshape are the standard nanoGPT tricks: all heads are one batched matmul, never a Python loop.

### Q6. Why are the heads computed as one batched matmul instead of a for-loop?

Because a `for head in range(h)` loop would launch `h` small, GPU-underutilising kernels and be far slower. Instead you keep everything in one tensor of shape `(B, h, T, d_k)` and let the batched matmul treat `(B, h)` as the batch dimensions. `q @ k.transpose(-2, -1)` then computes all `h` per-head `(T, T)` score matrices in a single fused operation. The head split is purely a `view`/`reshape` of contiguous memory — no data is copied per head. This is why "run h heads in parallel" is literal on hardware, not just conceptual.

### Q7. What does W_o do and why is it needed after concatenation?

After the heads produce their outputs and you concatenate them into a `(seq, d_model)` tensor, each `d_k`-wide slice still lives in its **own** head's subspace — the concat just places them side by side with no interaction. `W_o`, a `(d_model, d_model)` projection, is what **mixes** the heads: it lets the model form linear combinations across all heads' outputs, so information found by head 3 can influence the channel written by head 7. Without `W_o` the residual stream would receive `h` disjoint blocks that never talk. It also maps the concatenated result back onto the residual-stream basis so it can be added to `x`. Think of `W_q/k/v` as reading the residual stream and `W_o` as writing back to it.

### Q8. What kinds of relationships do different heads learn?

Empirically, heads specialise into interpretable roles:

- **Previous-token heads** — attend almost entirely to the token one position back.
- **Positional / offset heads** — attend to a fixed relative distance.
- **Syntactic heads** — link a word to its grammatical head (verb to subject, noun to determiner).
- **Coreference heads** — connect a pronoun to its antecedent.
- **Delimiter / punctuation heads** — attend to separators, sentence boundaries, or the start token (attention sink).
- **Induction heads** — a two-head circuit that finds the previous occurrence of the current token and copies what followed it; this underpins much of in-context learning.

You can see these by plotting a head's `(seq, seq)` softmax matrix. Not every head is cleanly interpretable, and roles are learned, not assigned.

### Q9. If d_model = 512 and h = 8, what is d_k, and what are the shapes of W_q and W_o?

`d_k = d_model / h = 512 / 8 = 64`. Each head's Q/K/V vectors are 64-dimensional.

The projection matrices operate on the full width, not per head:

```text
W_q : (d_model, d_model) = (512, 512)   # produces all 8 heads' queries at once
W_k : (512, 512)
W_v : (512, 512)
W_o : (512, 512)
```

You *could* view `W_q` as 8 separate `(512, 64)` blocks — mathematically identical — but in practice it's one `(512, 512)` matrix and the head split is a reshape of its output.

### Q10. How many parameters does a multi-head attention block have?

Four square projections, each `d_model x d_model`, plus optional biases. Ignoring biases:

```text
params(MHA) = 4 * d_model^2         # W_q + W_k + W_v + W_o
```

For `d_model = 768` (GPT-2 small): `4 * 768^2 ≈ 2.36M` per attention block. Note this is **independent of h** — splitting into more heads just reshapes the same matrices, so the parameter count doesn't change with head count. This is why, per layer, attention (`~4 d_model^2`) is smaller than the FFN (`~8 d_model^2`) — most parameters live in the feed-forward network, not attention.

### Q11. Is multi-head attention where tokens exchange information? What does the FFN do by contrast?

Yes — **attention is the only sublayer that moves information between token positions**. Every token's new representation is a weighted mix of *other* tokens' Values, so this is where context enters. The feed-forward network that follows is applied **independently and identically to each position** — it never looks at neighbours. A useful slogan: *attention mixes across tokens, the FFN processes each token*. This division is deliberate: attention decides *what to gather from context*, the FFN decides *what to compute given the gathered context*.
