It also explains why causal masking lives entirely in attention — it's the only place the future could leak in.

### Q12. Does each head need its own Q, K, and V? How does this connect to GQA and MQA?

In vanilla multi-head attention, **yes** — every head has its own independent Q, K, and V projections, so there are `h` query heads, `h` key heads, and `h` value heads. During generation you must cache K and V for every head of every layer, and that KV cache is a major memory and bandwidth cost.

**Grouped-Query Attention (GQA)** and **Multi-Query Attention (MQA)** relax this: they keep `h` **query** heads but share **fewer** K/V heads (GQA groups several query heads onto one K/V head; MQA uses a single K/V head for all queries). Fewer distinct K/V vectors means a much smaller KV cache and faster memory-bound decoding, with little quality loss. So MHA is the fully-independent baseline; GQA/MQA trade some head independence for cheaper inference. This is covered further in the efficiency topics.

### Q13. What is the per-head attention matrix and why is it the O(n^2) bottleneck?

For each head, the scores are `Q @ K^T` where `Q` and `K` are `(seq, d_k)`, producing a `(seq, seq)` matrix — every token scored against every other token. With `h` heads and `batch` examples that's a `(batch, h, seq, seq)` tensor. Its size grows with `seq^2`, so both the compute and (naively) the memory are **O(n^2)** in sequence length. Doubling the context quadruples this matrix. That quadratic term is the reason long context is expensive and the target of **FlashAttention** (which avoids materialising the full `(seq, seq)` matrix in high-bandwidth memory by tiling) and of sliding-window/local attention schemes.

### Q14. In cross-attention (encoder-decoder), where do the Q, K, and V come from in each head?

In **self-attention**, all three come from the same sequence: `Q, K, V = x W_q, x W_k, x W_v`. In **cross-attention**, each head takes its **Query from the decoder** (the sequence being generated) and its **Key and Value from the encoder output** (e.g. the source sentence in translation):

```text
self-attention : Q,K,V all from x       (a token attends to its own sequence)
cross-attention: Q from decoder state,  K,V from encoder output
```

So a decoder token asks "what in the source is relevant to me?" and pulls Values from the encoded source. Decoder-only LLMs (GPT) have **no** cross-attention — only masked self-attention — because there's no separate encoder; the prompt and generation share one causal stream.

### Q15. Spot the bug: a candidate scales attention scores by sqrt(d_model) instead of sqrt(d_k). Why is that wrong?

The scaling exists to keep the dot product `q . k` from growing with the dimension it's summed over. Each head's Q and K are `d_k`-dimensional, so the dot product is a sum of `d_k` terms and its variance scales with `d_k`. Therefore the correct divisor is `sqrt(d_k)`, the **per-head** dimension — not `sqrt(d_model)`.

Using `sqrt(d_model)` (which is `sqrt(h * d_k)`, larger by `sqrt(h)`) would **over-shrink** the scores, flattening the softmax toward uniform and weakening the head's ability to focus early in training. The rule: scale by the size of the vectors actually being dotted, which is `d_k`. In the code that's `scores / self.d_k ** 0.5`.

### Q16. Can you increase heads indefinitely? What is the trade-off as h grows?

No. As `h` grows with `d_model` fixed, `d_k = d_model / h` shrinks. Below some point each head's Query/Key space is too low-dimensional to represent distinct, useful queries — heads become noisy or redundant and quality degrades. There's also a practical floor for hardware efficiency. Conversely, too *few* heads underuses the model's capacity for parallel relationships. Real models pick `h` so `d_k` stays around 64–128 (GPT-2: `d_k = 64`). And the modern move is often the opposite of "more heads": GQA/MQA *reduce* the number of K/V heads to save inference memory. So `h` is a tuning knob balancing expressiveness (more parallel patterns) against per-head dimensionality (enough width to form a good query).

## The Transformer Block

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

This topic assembles the two sublayers you already know — **multi-head attention** and the **feed-forward network** — into the repeatable unit that, stacked N times, *is* the transformer. It covers how each sublayer is wrapped in a **residual (skip) connection** and a **layer normalization**, the exact **pre-LN** data flow `x = x + MHA(LN(x))` then `x = x + FFN(LN(x))`, and why that specific arrangement is what makes very deep stacks trainable. It covers the division of labour — attention mixes information across token positions, the FFN processes each position independently, and the residual stream carries everything forward — plus why blocks are **identical in shape** so they can stack, and the difference between **pre-LN and post-LN** (why modern models put the norm *inside* the residual). The 15 questions build from "what is one block" to "why pre-LN beats post-LN" and "trace a tensor through the block with shapes", ending in a full ```python block.

**Mental model**

Picture a **residual stream**: a `(seq, d_model)` highway of vectors running straight from the embeddings to the final logits. It is never overwritten — each block only *reads* from it and *adds* a correction back. A block does this twice. First it normalises a copy of the stream, runs multi-head attention (letting tokens gather context from each other), and **adds** the result back to the stream. Then it normalises the stream again, runs a per-token feed-forward MLP (letting each token compute on what it just gathered), and adds that back too. Because every sublayer's contribution is *added*, not substituted, gradients flow backward along the identity path unobstructed — the network can be hundreds of layers deep and still train. The layer norm keeps the numbers in a stable range so each sublayer sees well-conditioned inputs. Every block has the same shape in and out (`(batch, seq, d_model)`), so the model is just this one Lego brick repeated N times, each brick free to learn a different transformation.

**Key terms**

- **transformer block / layer** — one attention sublayer + one FFN sublayer, each with residual + norm; the unit that stacks N times.
- **residual connection (skip connection)** — `x + sublayer(x)`; adds the sublayer's output to its input.
- **residual stream** — the running `(seq, d_model)` sum that every block reads from and writes to.
- **layer norm (LN)** — normalises each token vector across its `d_model` features to mean 0, variance 1, then scales/shifts by learned parameters.
- **pre-LN** — norm applied *inside* the residual branch, before the sublayer: `x + Sub(LN(x))`.
- **post-LN** — norm applied *after* the add: `LN(x + Sub(x))` (the original 2017 design).
- **sublayer** — one of the two components (MHA or FFN) wrapped by residual+norm.
- **RMSNorm** — a cheaper norm variant that skips mean-centering; common in modern LLMs.
- **final layer norm** — a single norm after the last block, before the LM head.
- **depth (N)** — the number of stacked blocks; the main knob alongside width `d_model`.

**Why interviewers ask this**

The block is the "can you actually build a transformer?" checkpoint. A junior candidate lists "attention and a feed-forward layer." A senior candidate writes the exact four-line data flow, puts the layer norms in the *pre-LN* position, and can explain that the residual connection is a **gradient highway** without which deep stacks fail to train. Interviewers use it to test whether you understand *why* the arrangement works, not just its parts: why norm the input rather than the output, why add instead of replace, why the block is shape-preserving. It's also where they check you know the difference between what attention does (cross-token mixing) and what the FFN does (per-token computation). Being able to draw the block and trace shapes through it is one of the strongest signals in an LLM interview.

**Common confusions**

- "The residual replaces the input with the sublayer output" — no, it **adds**: `x + Sub(x)`. The original `x` is preserved.
- "Layer norm normalises across the batch" — that's BatchNorm. LayerNorm normalises across the **feature** dimension of each token, independent of batch and sequence.
- "Pre-LN and post-LN are the same" — they differ in *where* the norm sits relative to the add, and that difference decides whether deep models train stably without warmup tricks.
- "There's a norm only between blocks" — each block has two norms (one per sublayer), plus one final norm before the LM head.
- "Blocks share weights" — they don't; each of the N blocks has its own independent parameters. They share only their *shape*.

**What follows from this topic**

The block is built from **Multi-Head Attention** (its first sublayer) and the **Feed-Forward Network** (its second sublayer, the next topic), so those two topics fully specify a block's internals. Stacking N of these blocks, bracketed by the embedding layer and the final norm + LM head, gives the **full architecture** and the parameter count. The residual stream you meet here is the object the **forward pass** propagates, and the pre-LN choice is a stability decision that connects to **RMSNorm** and modern training. Everything after this is either what goes *in* the block or what *wraps* the stack.

### Q1. What is a transformer block and what are its two sublayers?

A transformer block is the repeatable unit of a transformer. It has exactly two sublayers:

1. **Multi-head self-attention** — lets each token gather information from other tokens (cross-position mixing).
2. **Position-wise feed-forward network (FFN/MLP)** — processes each token independently (per-position computation).

Each sublayer is wrapped in a **residual connection** and a **layer normalization**. In the modern pre-LN form:

```text
x = x + MHA(LN(x))     # sublayer 1: attention
x = x + FFN(LN(x))     # sublayer 2: feed-forward
```

The block takes `(batch, seq, d_model)` and returns the same shape, so N identical-shaped blocks stack to form the model. Attention is the only place tokens talk to each other; the FFN is where each token "thinks" about what it gathered.

### Q2. Write out the exact pre-LN data flow for one block.

```python
def block(x):
    # sublayer 1: attention, wrapped in residual + pre-norm
    x = x + mha(layer_norm_1(x))
    # sublayer 2: feed-forward, wrapped in residual + pre-norm
    x = x + ffn(layer_norm_2(x))
    return x
```

Read it as: normalise a copy of the residual stream, run the sublayer on that copy, **add** the result back to the stream. Two sublayers, two norms, two adds. The stream `x` is never overwritten — only incremented. Note the norm is applied to the sublayer's *input* (pre-LN), and there are two separate norms with their own parameters.

### Q3. Why is the residual connection called a "gradient highway", and what breaks without it?

A residual connection computes `y = x + Sub(x)`. During backpropagation, the gradient of `y` with respect to `x` is `1 + d Sub/dx` — the `1` comes from the identity path. That means gradient can flow backward through **every** block without being repeatedly multiplied down by the sublayer's Jacobian. Across dozens or hundreds of layers, the identity path keeps the gradient from vanishing (or exploding), so deep stacks actually train.

Without residuals, each layer multiplies the backward gradient by its weight Jacobian; stack 50 of those and the signal either decays to zero or blows up, and the early layers get essentially no learning signal. Residuals were the key ingredient (from ResNets) that made very deep networks — and thus deep transformers — trainable. They also give the network an easy "do nothing" default: if a sublayer outputs ~0, the block is the identity, so adding layers can't hurt initialisation.

### Q4. What does layer normalization do, and how is it different from batch norm?

**LayerNorm** normalises each token's vector across its `d_model` **feature** dimension: subtract the mean, divide by the standard deviation (computed over the features of that single token), then apply a learned scale `gamma` and shift `beta`.

```text
LN(x) = gamma * (x - mean(x)) / sqrt(var(x) + eps) + beta   # mean/var over d_model features
```

It operates **per token, independent of batch and sequence position**. **BatchNorm**, by contrast, normalises each feature across the **batch** dimension, coupling examples together. LayerNorm is preferred in transformers because sequence lengths and batch composition vary, and generation happens one token at a time — you can't rely on batch statistics. LayerNorm's statistics depend only on the single vector being normalised, so it behaves identically at train and inference time.

### Q5. Compare pre-LN and post-LN. Why do modern models use pre-LN?

```text
post-LN (original 2017):   x = LN(x + Sub(x))     # norm AFTER the add
pre-LN  (modern):          x = x + Sub(LN(x))     # norm INSIDE the residual branch
```

| | post-LN | pre-LN |
|---|---|---|
| Norm position | after the residual add | on the sublayer input |
| Residual path | passes through LN | clean identity, unnormalised |
| Deep-net stability | needs careful LR warmup, can diverge | trains stably, larger LRs |
| Gradient at init | can vanish/explode with depth | well-behaved |

Pre-LN keeps the residual highway a **clean, un-normalised identity path** — the norm only touches the copy fed into the sublayer, so gradients flow straight back through the additions. Post-LN puts a norm *on* the highway, which attenuates the identity gradient and makes very deep models fragile without warmup and careful init. Pre-LN trains more stably at depth and tolerates higher learning rates, so nearly all modern LLMs use it. The trade-off: pre-LN can leave the residual stream's magnitude growing across layers, which is why a **final layer norm** is added before the LM head.

### Q6. Why attention then FFN — what does each contribute, and why that order?

Attention comes first because a token must **gather** relevant context before it can usefully **process** it. The attention sublayer mixes across positions: each token pulls in a weighted blend of other tokens' information. The FFN then runs on each token independently, transforming that gathered representation — thought of as looking up and applying "facts" or features. The slogan: *attention moves information between tokens; the FFN computes on each token*. Doing FFN-then-attention would have each token compute before it has any context to compute on. The residual + norm around each keeps both trainable. This alternation — mix, then process, mix, then process — repeated N times, is how the model builds increasingly abstract representations.

### Q7. Why are all blocks identical in shape, and does that mean they share weights?

Every block maps `(batch, seq, d_model)` to `(batch, seq, d_model)` — input and output shapes are identical. That shape-preservation is what lets you stack N of them: the output of block `i` is a valid input to block `i+1` with no glue code. It's also why you can add or remove layers without changing anything else in the architecture.

Identical **shape** does **not** mean shared **weights**. Each block has its own independent `W_q/k/v/o`, FFN matrices, and layer-norm parameters, all learned separately. Early blocks tend to learn low-level/positional features and later blocks more abstract/semantic ones. (Weight *tying across layers* exists in some specialised models, e.g. Universal Transformers, but standard GPT-style models do not tie block weights.)

### Q8. Trace a tensor through a full block with shapes.

```text
in:  x                         (B, T, D)

# sublayer 1: attention
n1 = LayerNorm(x)              (B, T, D)
a  = MHA(n1)                   (B, T, D)     # internally (B, h, T, d_k), merged back
x  = x + a                     (B, T, D)     # residual add

# sublayer 2: feed-forward
n2 = LayerNorm(x)              (B, T, D)
f  = W2 @ act(W1 @ n2)         (B, T, D)     # W1: D->4D, act, W2: 4D->D
x  = x + f                     (B, T, D)     # residual add

out: x                         (B, T, D)
```

The width `D = d_model` is constant throughout; the FFN briefly expands to `4D` internally but returns to `D`. Nothing changes the outer shape, so this whole thing is one stackable unit.

### Q9. Write a complete transformer block in PyTorch.

```python
import torch.nn as nn

class Block(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, d_model, n_heads, mlp_ratio=4):
        super().__init__()
        self.ln1 = nn.LayerNorm(d_model)
        self.attn = MultiHeadAttention(d_model, n_heads)   # from the MHA topic
        self.ln2 = nn.LayerNorm(d_model)
        self.ffn = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(d_model, mlp_ratio * d_model),
            nn.GELU(),
            nn.Linear(mlp_ratio * d_model, d_model),
        )

    def forward(self, x):
        x = x + self.attn(self.ln1(x))   # pre-LN attention sublayer
        x = x + self.ffn(self.ln2(x))    # pre-LN feed-forward sublayer
        return x
```

Two pre-norm sublayers, each added back to the residual stream. Stack `nn.Sequential(*[Block(...) for _ in range(N)])`, bracket with embeddings and a final norm + LM head, and you have a GPT.

### Q10. Where do the layer norms actually sit in a real GPT — how many are there?

In a pre-LN GPT with N blocks there are `2N + 1` layer norms:

- **Two per block** — `ln1` before attention, `ln2` before the FFN (both inside their residual branches).
- **One final norm** after the last block, just before the LM head.

```text
embeddings -> [ block: ln1, attn, +  ; ln2, ffn, + ] x N -> final_ln -> lm_head
```

The final norm matters specifically for pre-LN: because the residual stream is never normalised on its identity path, its magnitude can grow across layers, so a single normalisation right before projecting to logits keeps the LM head's inputs well-scaled.

### Q11. What is RMSNorm and why do modern models prefer it over LayerNorm?

**RMSNorm** normalises by the root-mean-square of the vector's features but **skips the mean-subtraction** (re-centering) that LayerNorm does:

```text
LayerNorm:  gamma * (x - mean(x)) / sqrt(var(x) + eps) + beta
RMSNorm:    gamma *  x            / sqrt(mean(x^2) + eps)
```

It drops the mean and the bias term. Empirically the re-centering contributes little to transformer performance, while RMSNorm is **cheaper** (fewer reductions and elementwise ops) and slightly more stable. Because a language model runs a norm twice per block across many layers, that saving adds up. Models like LLaMA use RMSNorm in the same pre-norm positions where GPT-2 used LayerNorm — the placement is identical; only the normalisation formula is simpler.

### Q12. In pre-LN, is the residual stream ever normalised on its main path? What consequence follows?

No. In pre-LN the norm is applied only to the **copy** that feeds each sublayer (`Sub(LN(x))`); the main residual path is a clean, un-normalised identity that just accumulates additions. The consequence is twofold: (1) gradients flow backward through that identity path unimpeded, which is exactly why pre-LN trains stably at depth; but (2) since nothing rescales the stream, its magnitude tends to **grow** as more sublayer outputs are added across layers. That growth is benign for training but means the final activations can be large, so a **final layer norm** is placed before the LM head to bring them back to a well-conditioned range for the logit projection.

### Q13. What would go wrong if you removed layer norm entirely but kept residuals?

You'd keep the gradient highway from residuals but lose input conditioning. Without normalisation, the scale of activations entering each attention and FFN sublayer would drift — the residual stream's magnitude grows across layers (additions accumulate), so deep sublayers would receive increasingly large, poorly-scaled inputs. That pushes softmax and activation functions into saturated regions, produces unstable gradients, and makes the model highly sensitive to learning rate and initialisation. Training typically diverges or stalls for anything but very shallow models. Norm's job is to hand each sublayer a mean-stable, unit-scale input regardless of how large the residual stream has grown, keeping the whole deep stack in a trainable regime.

### Q14. Some models add a learned scale on the residual branch (e.g. LayerScale). Why?

At initialisation you often want each sublayer to contribute **almost nothing**, so the block starts near the identity and the deep stack is stable from step one. A small learned per-channel scalar on the sublayer output — `x = x + alpha * Sub(LN(x))` with `alpha` initialised tiny — lets the network *gradually* turn each sublayer on as training finds it useful. This eases optimisation of very deep models (dozens to hundreds of layers), reducing early instability. It's an optional refinement on top of the standard residual+pre-LN block; the base architecture already biases toward identity because a zero-output sublayer leaves the stream unchanged, but explicit residual scaling makes that bias tunable.

### Q15. Spot the misconception: "each block overwrites the previous block's representation." Correct it.

That's wrong. A block does **not** overwrite anything — it **adds** to the residual stream: `x = x + Sub(LN(x))`. The previous representation is preserved and only *incremented* by each sublayer's correction. This is the whole point of residual connections: the stream is a running accumulator that every block reads from and writes small updates into, not a value that gets replaced layer by layer. A useful frame from interpretability is that the residual stream is a shared communication channel — early blocks write features that later blocks read and build on, all via addition. Because contributions accumulate rather than replace, information from any layer can survive all the way to the final logits, and a sublayer that outputs zero simply leaves the stream untouched.

## Feed-Forward Networks & Activations

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

This topic covers the second sublayer of every transformer block: the **position-wise feed-forward network (FFN, also called the MLP)**. It is a small two-layer neural net applied **independently and identically to each token**: `FFN(x) = W2 @ act(W1 @ x + b1) + b2`, with a hidden dimension usually **4 * d_model** — expand, apply a non-linearity, project back. It covers the activation functions — why modern models use **GELU** or **SwiGLU** rather than plain ReLU — and the two big structural facts an interviewer wants: (1) the FFN is **where most of a transformer's parameters live** (far more than attention), and (2) it is thought to act like a **key-value memory** that stores learned facts and features. It also covers the sharp contrast with attention: the FFN mixes nothing across positions — each token is processed alone — whereas attention is the only cross-token operation. The 15 questions build from "what is the FFN and its shape" to param counting, activation trade-offs, the key-value-memory view, and SwiGLU's gated form.

**Mental model**

If attention is where tokens *talk to each other*, the FFN is where each token *thinks by itself*. After attention has gathered relevant context into a token's vector, the FFN takes that single `d_model` vector, blows it up into a much wider hidden space (typically 4x), applies a non-linear activation, and squashes it back down to `d_model`. The wide hidden layer is the point: with `4 * d_model` neurons, the first matrix `W1` can be read as a big set of **keys** (each hidden unit detects some pattern in the input vector), the activation gates which of those units fire, and the second matrix `W2` reads out the corresponding **values** (each firing unit adds its learned vector to the output). That is why people describe the FFN as an associative key-value memory: it's where the model appears to store "when the context looks like X, contribute fact/feature Y." Every token runs through the *same* FFN weights with *no* interaction between positions — it's a per-token lookup-and-transform, the pure computation step between the cross-token mixing steps.

**Key terms**

- **FFN / MLP** — the two-layer feed-forward sublayer applied per token.
- **position-wise** — the same FFN is applied to each token position independently, with shared weights.
- **W1 (up-projection)** — `(d_model, d_ff)` expands the token vector to the hidden dimension.
- **W2 (down-projection)** — `(d_ff, d_model)` projects back to model width.
- **d_ff (hidden dim)** — the FFN's inner width, usually `4 * d_model` (larger relative for gated variants).
- **activation** — the non-linearity between the two layers (ReLU, GELU, SwiGLU).
- **GELU** — Gaussian Error Linear Unit; smooth, probabilistic gating; the GPT/BERT default.
- **SwiGLU** — a gated activation (Swish gate times a linear path) used in LLaMA/PaLM.
- **key-value memory** — the interpretation of `W1`/`W2` as pattern-detectors (keys) and stored outputs (values).
- **per-token independence** — no information flows between positions inside the FFN.
- **expansion ratio** — `d_ff / d_model`, typically 4 (or ~8/3 for SwiGLU to match params).

**Why interviewers ask this**

The FFN is where candidates reveal whether they understand a transformer's *parameter budget* and its *division of labour*. Many people over-index on attention and are surprised to be told the FFN holds roughly two-thirds of the non-embedding parameters. A senior answer states the `4 * d_model` hidden dim, writes the `W2 @ act(W1 @ x)` form, counts the params (`~8 * d_model^2` per layer vs `~4 * d_model^2` for attention), and explains the key-value-memory interpretation. Interviewers also probe activations — why GELU/SwiGLU beat ReLU, and what "gated" means — and the position-wise property (the FFN never mixes tokens; only attention does). Getting the per-token independence right shows you understand *why* attention is the special, information-moving sublayer. It's a favourite because it's simple to state yet exposes whether you know where the model's capacity actually sits.

**Common confusions**

- "The FFN mixes information between tokens" — it does not; it's applied independently per position. Only attention mixes tokens.
- "Most parameters are in attention" — the opposite; the FFN's `~8 d_model^2` per layer outweighs attention's `~4 d_model^2`.
- "Hidden dim equals d_model" — no, it's typically `4 * d_model`; the expand-then-contract shape is the whole point.
- "ReLU vs GELU barely matters" — smooth activations (GELU/SwiGLU) consistently train better in transformers; SwiGLU is a common upgrade.
- "SwiGLU is just a different activation function" — it's a **gated** layer with an extra weight matrix (a gate path times a value path), so its shapes differ from a plain FFN.
- "The FFN is where attention happens" — separate sublayers; the FFN has no queries, keys over positions, or softmax.

**What follows from this topic**

The FFN is the second sublayer inside **The Transformer Block**, paired with **Multi-Head Attention** and wrapped in the same residual + norm. Because it holds most of the parameters, it dominates the **parameter-count** and FLOPs estimates in the full-architecture topic, and it's the main target of **Mixture of Experts (MoE)**, which swaps the single dense FFN for many expert FFNs and routes each token to a few. Its per-token independence is the complement to attention's cross-token mixing — together they define what one block computes. The key-value-memory view connects to interpretability questions about *where facts live* in an LLM.

### Q1. What is the feed-forward network in a transformer, and what is its formula?

The FFN (also called the MLP) is the second sublayer in each transformer block: a small two-layer fully-connected network applied to each token vector. Its standard form is:

```text
FFN(x) = W2 @ act(W1 @ x + b1) + b2
```

- `W1` is the **up-projection** `(d_model, d_ff)`, expanding the token to a wider hidden dimension `d_ff` (usually `4 * d_model`).
- `act` is a non-linear activation (GELU, ReLU, or a gated variant like SwiGLU).
- `W2` is the **down-projection** `(d_ff, d_model)`, mapping back to model width.

It's applied **position-wise**: every token runs through the same weights independently, with no interaction between positions. Expand, non-linearly gate, contract — that's the whole sublayer. It sits after attention inside the block, wrapped in a residual connection and layer norm.

### Q2. Why is the hidden dimension usually 4 * d_model?

The FFN deliberately projects **up** to a much wider hidden space before projecting back down, and `4x` is the long-standing default (from the original transformer). The width is where the sublayer's expressive power lives: a wider hidden layer means more hidden units, each able to detect a different pattern in the input vector, so the FFN can represent more features / "memories." Too narrow and it bottlenecks the per-token computation; `4 * d_model` was found to be a good capacity/compute trade-off and stuck. Example: GPT-2 small has `d_model = 768`, so `d_ff = 3072`. Gated variants like SwiGLU often use ~`8/3 * d_model` for the two gate matrices so the total parameter count matches a `4x` plain FFN.

### Q3. Show that most of a transformer's parameters live in the FFN, not attention.

Per layer, count the big matrices (ignore biases and norms):

```text
attention: W_q, W_k, W_v, W_o, each (d_model, d_model)
         = 4 * d_model^2

FFN:       W1 (d_model, 4*d_model) + W2 (4*d_model, d_model)
         = 4*d_model^2 + 4*d_model^2
         = 8 * d_model^2
```

So the FFN has roughly **twice** the parameters of attention in every layer — about two-thirds of each block's non-embedding parameters. Concretely for `d_model = 768`: attention ≈ 2.36M, FFN ≈ 4.72M per layer. This is the headline fact: when people picture "the model's knowledge," most of the weights implementing it sit in the feed-forward networks, not in attention.

### Q4. Why do modern models use GELU or SwiGLU instead of ReLU?

**ReLU** (`max(0, x)`) is cheap but hard-gates: it zeroes all negatives with a non-smooth kink, which can kill gradients for units stuck in the negative region ("dying ReLU") and gives a discontinuous derivative.

**GELU** (Gaussian Error Linear Unit) multiplies the input by the probability it's kept under a Gaussian gate, so it's a **smooth** approximation that lets small negative values through and has a continuous derivative. Empirically it trains transformers slightly better; it's the GPT-2/BERT default.

**SwiGLU** goes further with a **gated** design: it splits the up-projection into two paths, passes one through a Swish activation, and multiplies them elementwise. The learned multiplicative gate is more expressive per parameter, and models like LLaMA and PaLM found it improves quality, so it's common in recent LLMs. The pattern across the field: smooth and/or gated activations beat plain ReLU in transformers, at modest extra cost.

### Q5. What does "position-wise" mean, and how does the FFN differ from attention?

"Position-wise" means the FFN is applied to **each token position separately, with the same shared weights**, and with **no interaction between positions**. Token 5's FFN output depends only on token 5's vector; it never sees token 4 or 6.

```text
attention: output[i] depends on ALL positions   -> mixes across tokens
FFN:       output[i] depends only on input[i]    -> per-token, no mixing
```

This is the fundamental division of labour in a block: **attention moves information between tokens; the FFN computes on each token in isolation.** It's also why causal masking lives only in attention — the FFN can't leak future information because it never looks at other positions at all. Implementation-wise the FFN is just a matmul broadcast over the `(batch, seq)` dimensions.

### Q6. Explain the "key-value memory" interpretation of the FFN.

View the two matrices as a memory lookup. The up-projection `W1` has `d_ff` rows; each row is a **key** — a direction that the input token vector is compared against (via dot product), so each hidden unit measures "how much does this token match pattern k?". The activation keeps the strongly-matching units and suppresses the rest — a soft selection. The down-projection `W2` has `d_ff` columns; each column is a **value** — the vector that gets added to the output when its hidden unit fires. So the FFN computes: *for each stored pattern the token matches, add that pattern's associated output vector.*

This is why the FFN is thought to be where a model **stores facts and features**: `W1` recognises "the context is about the Eiffel Tower / a plural subject / Python code," and `W2` writes back the corresponding learned contribution. Interpretability work (e.g. probing and editing individual FFN neurons) supports this associative-memory reading, and it's why fact-editing methods target FFN weights.

### Q7. Write a standard (GELU) FFN in PyTorch and give the shapes.

```python
import torch.nn as nn

class FFN(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, d_model, mult=4):
        super().__init__()
        self.w1 = nn.Linear(d_model, mult * d_model)   # up:   (D, 4D)
        self.act = nn.GELU()
        self.w2 = nn.Linear(mult * d_model, d_model)   # down: (4D, D)

    def forward(self, x):
        # x: (B, T, D)
        h = self.act(self.w1(x))   # (B, T, 4D)
        return self.w2(h)          # (B, T, D)
```

Shapes: input `(B, T, D)` expands to `(B, T, 4D)` after `w1`+activation, then contracts back to `(B, T, D)` after `w2`. The `(B, T)` dimensions are just batched over — every token is transformed by the same weights independently.

### Q8. How does SwiGLU differ structurally from a plain FFN?

A plain FFN has one up-projection. SwiGLU uses **two** parallel up-projections and multiplies them, with a Swish (SiLU) gate on one:

```text
plain FFN:  W2 @ act(W1 @ x)
SwiGLU:     W2 @ ( SiLU(W_gate @ x) * (W_up @ x) )
```

`W_gate` produces the **gate** path (passed through SiLU), `W_up` produces the **value** path, and they're multiplied elementwise before the down-projection `W2`. That's three matrices instead of two, so to keep the parameter count comparable to a `4 * d_model` plain FFN, SwiGLU typically shrinks the hidden dimension to about `8/3 * d_model`. The multiplicative gate lets the network learn *input-dependent* scaling of each hidden unit — strictly more expressive than a single activation — which is why LLaMA/PaLM-style models adopt it.

### Q9. Given d_model = 4096 and a 4x FFN, how many parameters are in one FFN (ignoring biases)?

```text
d_ff = 4 * d_model = 16384
W1: d_model * d_ff = 4096 * 16384 = 67,108,864
W2: d_ff * d_model = 16384 * 4096 = 67,108,864
total ≈ 134.2M parameters per FFN
```

About **134 million** parameters in a single layer's FFN. Compare attention in the same layer: `4 * d_model^2 = 4 * 4096^2 ≈ 67.1M`. So the FFN is ~2x attention per layer — concrete confirmation that the feed-forward networks dominate the model's parameter budget. Across, say, 32 layers that's ~4.3B parameters just in FFNs.

### Q10. Does the FFN see other tokens? What does this imply for causal masking and parallelism?

No — the FFN processes each token strictly independently; token `i`'s output is a function of token `i`'s vector alone. Two implications:

1. **Causal masking is unnecessary in the FFN.** The "don't look at the future" constraint only needs enforcing where positions interact, which is attention. The FFN can't leak future tokens because it never reads them.
2. **It's embarrassingly parallel across positions.** During training and prefill, every token's FFN runs at once as a batched matmul; there's no sequential dependency. (The sequential dependency in generation comes from attention needing the growing KV cache, not from the FFN.)

This per-token independence is the exact complement to attention's job and is why the two alternate in every block.

### Q11. How does Mixture of Experts change the FFN?

MoE replaces the single dense FFN in a block with **many** parallel FFNs ("experts") plus a small **router** network. For each token, the router picks a few experts (e.g. top-2 of 64) and sends the token only through those; their outputs are combined. The key property: the model has a huge number of FFN parameters (all the experts), but each token only activates a small, fixed subset, so the **per-token FLOPs stay roughly constant** while total capacity grows enormously.

```text
dense FFN:  every token -> the one FFN
MoE FFN:    router picks top-k experts -> token -> only those k FFNs
```

Because most parameters already live in FFNs, MoE targets exactly the right place to scale parameter count cheaply. Costs are added routing complexity, load-balancing across experts, and higher memory to hold all experts. This is how models get to "trillion parameters" while keeping inference affordable.

### Q12. Spot the misconception: "the FFN's hidden layer is the same size as d_model." Correct it.

That's wrong — the defining feature of the FFN is that it **expands** to a wider hidden dimension, typically `d_ff = 4 * d_model`, before contracting back. If the hidden layer were the same width as `d_model`, you'd lose most of the sublayer's capacity: the whole point of the up-projection is to give the token vector a large space in which many independent pattern-detectors (the "keys") can operate before the down-projection reads out their combined "values." The block's shape is expand-nonlinearity-contract (`D -> 4D -> D`), not `D -> D -> D`. Gated variants like SwiGLU adjust the exact multiplier (~`8/3` across their two matrices) to match parameters, but they still expand well beyond `d_model`.

### Q13. Why does the FFN need a non-linearity at all — what if it were just W2 @ (W1 @ x)?

Without the activation, `W2 @ (W1 @ x)` is just `(W2 @ W1) @ x` — a single linear map, because composing two linear layers yields one linear layer. The whole two-matrix structure would collapse to an equivalent `(d_model, d_model)` matrix, gaining nothing over a plain projection and unable to represent anything non-linear. The activation between `W1` and `W2` is what makes the FFN a genuine function approximator: it lets the hidden units gate on and off depending on the input, so different regions of input space get different transformations. That non-linear gating is exactly what the key-value-memory view relies on — a unit "fires" (contributes its value) only when its key pattern is present. Remove it and there are no distinct memories, just one linear blend.

### Q14. Where do the biases go, and why do many modern models drop them?

In the classic FFN, `W1` has a bias `b1` added before the activation and `W2` has a bias `b2` added after:

```text
FFN(x) = W2 @ act(W1 @ x + b1) + b2
```

Many recent models (e.g. LLaMA, and often in attention/norm layers too) **remove the bias terms**. Reasons: biases add parameters and a little compute for negligible quality benefit in large models; the following layer norm already provides a learnable shift, making some biases redundant; and dropping them slightly simplifies and speeds the network. So you'll see the leaner `W2 @ act(W1 @ x)` (or the SwiGLU gated form) with no biases in modern LLMs, while the textbook/original formulation keeps `b1` and `b2`. Functionally the biases are a minor detail; the up-project, activate, down-project structure is what matters.

### Q15. How much of the forward-pass compute does the FFN account for, and why does that matter?

Since the FFN has roughly `8 * d_model^2` parameters per layer versus attention's `4 * d_model^2`, its matmuls dominate the **per-token** FLOPs — for short-to-moderate sequences the FFN is the largest single chunk of compute, not attention. (Attention's cost has an extra `O(seq^2)` term from the `(seq, seq)` score matrix, so at very long context attention's share grows; but its parameter-driven matmuls are smaller.) This matters for two reasons: (1) inference and training cost estimates (`FLOPs ≈ 6 * N * D`) are dominated by these dense FFN matmuls, so optimising them (quantization, better kernels) pays off most; and (2) it's exactly why MoE targets the FFN — routing each token to only a few experts cuts the biggest compute/parameter block while keeping per-token FLOPs flat. Understanding that the FFN is both the parameter *and* compute heavyweight explains where the field spends its optimisation effort.
## Normalization & Residual Connections

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

Two small ideas that do the heavy lifting of making deep transformers actually trainable: **residual (skip) connections** and **normalization**. Neither adds "intelligence" — attention and the FFN do the computing — but without them you cannot stack more than a handful of transformer blocks before gradients vanish or explode and training diverges. This topic covers: (1) the **residual connection** `x + sublayer(x)` and why it acts as a gradient highway / identity path; (2) **LayerNorm** (normalize across features per token, with a learnable gain and bias) versus **RMSNorm** (a cheaper variant that skips mean subtraction, now standard in modern LLMs); (3) the **placement** question — **pre-LN** (norm inside the residual branch) versus **post-LN** (the original Transformer's design) and why pre-LN gives far more stable training with less warmup fragility; and (4) how these two mechanisms together are what makes 100-layer transformers possible. The 16 questions move from "what is a residual" to "why does pre-LN remove the need for learning-rate warmup".

**Mental model**

Picture a very deep stack of blocks. Each block wants to *add a small refinement* to the running representation, not replace it. The residual connection encodes exactly that: `x_out = x_in + sublayer(x_in)`. The representation flows down an uninterrupted **identity highway**, and each sublayer merely writes a delta onto it. In the backward pass the same highway carries the gradient: because `d(x + f(x))/dx = 1 + f'(x)`, there is always a `1` term, so the gradient can reach early layers even if `f'` is tiny. That is why 50-100 layer nets train at all. Normalization is the second guardrail: after summing many contributions, activations drift in scale, so you renormalize each token's feature vector to a controlled mean/variance (LayerNorm) or just controlled magnitude (RMSNorm), keeping every layer's inputs in a sane range. Residuals let signal *flow*; norm keeps that signal *well-scaled*. Together they turn "deep = untrainable" into "deep = better".

**Key terms**

- **Residual / skip connection** — `x + sublayer(x)`; adds the sublayer's output back onto its input.
- **Identity path / gradient highway** — the `+ x` term that carries gradient unattenuated to earlier layers.
- **Vanishing gradient** — gradient shrinks toward 0 as it backprops through many layers; residuals prevent it.
- **LayerNorm** — normalize each token's vector across its `d_model` features to mean 0, variance 1, then scale by learnable `gamma` and shift by `beta`.
- **RMSNorm** — `x / rms(x) * gamma`, where `rms(x) = sqrt(mean(x^2) + eps)`; no mean subtraction, no bias — cheaper, used in LLaMA, etc.
- **gamma / beta (gain / bias)** — learnable per-feature scale and shift so the net can undo normalization if useful.
- **Post-LN** — original Transformer: `x = LN(x + sublayer(x))` (norm outside the residual).
- **Pre-LN** — modern: `x = x + sublayer(LN(x))` (norm inside the residual branch).
- **Warmup** — slowly ramping the learning rate at the start of training; post-LN needs it, pre-LN much less so.
- **BatchNorm** — normalizes across the batch dimension; NOT used in transformers (batch/sequence sizes vary, autoregressive).

**Why interviewers ask this**

This is where interviewers separate people who have *read about* transformers from people who understand *why they train*. A junior can draw the attention formula but shrugs at "why the `+ x`?". A senior explains the gradient-highway argument, knows that pre-LN is why modern models train without the fragile warmup schedules of the 2017 paper, and can state the LayerNorm-vs-RMSNorm trade-off (cost and the empirical finding that mean-centering barely matters). It is also a favourite because the two ideas are *simple* — one addition and one normalization — so there's nowhere to hide: either you can explain why they matter or you can't. Bonus signal: knowing transformers use LayerNorm not BatchNorm, and being able to say why (per-token, sequence-length-independent, no batch coupling).

**Common confusions**

- "Residuals help the model represent identity functions." — Not the point; the point is the *gradient* path in the backward pass.
- "LayerNorm normalizes across the batch." — No, that's BatchNorm. LayerNorm normalizes across features *within one token*, independent of batch and sequence length.
- "RMSNorm and LayerNorm are the same." — RMSNorm drops the mean-subtraction and the bias; it only rescales by root-mean-square. Cheaper, and empirically nearly as good.
- "Pre-LN vs post-LN is a minor detail." — It changes training stability dramatically; post-LN often needs careful warmup and can diverge, pre-LN is robust and is why deep models are now routine.
- "Normalization makes the model more accurate directly." — It stabilizes optimization; the accuracy gain is indirect (you can now train deeper/faster without diverging).

**What follows from this topic**

Residuals and norm are the glue of **The transformer block** (attention + FFN, each wrapped in norm + residual). Once you trust that a block trains stably, **Stacking Depth: The Full Architecture** shows how identical blocks stack into an N-layer network, and **The Forward Pass** traces shapes through that stack. The final LayerNorm before the LM head (covered in those topics) is the same LayerNorm discussed here. Pre-LN's stability is also what makes very large N (depth) practical, feeding the depth-vs-width discussion in the architecture topic.

### Q1. What is a residual connection and why does every transformer block use one?

A residual (or skip) connection adds a sublayer's input back onto its output:

```text
output = x + sublayer(x)
```

instead of `output = sublayer(x)`. In a transformer block both sublayers use it:

```text
x = x + MHA(LN(x))    # attention sublayer
x = x + FFN(LN(x))    # feed-forward sublayer
```

Why it matters: it lets each sublayer learn a small *delta* on top of a representation that flows through unchanged by default. If a sublayer has nothing useful to add, driving its output toward 0 recovers the identity `x`, which is easy to learn. More importantly, it gives gradients a direct path back to early layers (next question). Empirically, residuals are the single change that turned "you can stack ~6 layers" into "you can stack dozens to hundreds".

### Q2. Explain precisely how residual connections prevent vanishing gradients.

Consider one residual block `y = x + f(x)`. Differentiate with respect to the input:

```text
dy/dx = 1 + df/dx
```

There is always a `+1` term. Now stack L such blocks; by the chain rule the gradient of the loss w.r.t. an early activation is a *product* of these per-layer Jacobians:

```text
dLoss/dx_0  =  dLoss/dx_L  *  product over layers of (1 + df_i/dx)
```

Without the residual each factor would be just `df_i/dx`, and if those are small (say ~0.5), a product of 50 of them is `0.5^50 ~= 1e-15` — the gradient vanishes and early layers never learn. With the residual each factor is `1 + something`, so there is always a path where the gradient passes through essentially unchanged (the `1` multiplies straight through). The gradient can still flow through the `f'` terms too, but it never has to. That "always a 1" is the gradient highway.

### Q3. What does LayerNorm compute, step by step, and over which axis?

LayerNorm normalizes each token's feature vector across the `d_model` feature axis (NOT across batch or sequence). For a single token vector `x` of length `d_model`:

```python
def layernorm(x, gamma, beta, eps=1e-5):
    mu = x.mean(axis=-1, keepdims=True)          # mean over features
    var = x.var(axis=-1, keepdims=True)          # variance over features
    x_hat = (x - mu) / sqrt(var + eps)           # zero mean, unit variance
    return gamma * x_hat + beta                  # learnable scale & shift
```

So every token is independently rescaled to mean 0, variance 1, then the learnable per-feature `gamma` (gain) and `beta` (bias) let the network recover any scale/shift it actually wants — including undoing the normalization. Because it operates per token over the feature axis, it is independent of batch size and sequence length, which is exactly what you need for variable-length autoregressive generation.

### Q4. Why LayerNorm and not BatchNorm in transformers?

BatchNorm normalizes each feature across the *batch* dimension, using batch statistics. That is a poor fit for language models:

| | BatchNorm | LayerNorm |
|---|---|---|
| Normalizes across | Batch dimension | Feature dimension (per token) |
| Depends on batch size | Yes (unstable for small batches) | No |
| Depends on other examples | Yes | No |
| Train vs inference stats | Different (running averages) | Same |
| Variable sequence length | Awkward | Natural |

Transformers process variable-length sequences, often with small or size-1 batches at inference (one user, one prompt), and generate autoregressively where coupling a token's normalization to unrelated batch members makes no sense. LayerNorm's per-token, batch-independent normalization sidesteps all of that: the same computation runs identically in training and inference, for any batch or sequence length.

### Q5. What is RMSNorm and how does it differ from LayerNorm?

RMSNorm is a cheaper normalization that skips mean-centering. It rescales by the root-mean-square of the vector only:

```python
def rmsnorm(x, gamma, eps=1e-6):
    rms = sqrt((x * x).mean(axis=-1, keepdims=True) + eps)
    return x / rms * gamma        # no mean subtraction, no beta bias
```

Differences from LayerNorm:

- **No mean subtraction** — LayerNorm subtracts the mean; RMSNorm does not re-center, it only controls magnitude.
- **No bias term** — typically just a learnable `gamma`, no `beta`.
- **Cheaper** — fewer reductions and operations per call, which adds up over many layers and tokens.

The empirical finding (Zhang & Sennrich, 2019) is that the re-centering LayerNorm does contributes little; the important part is the rescaling. Modern LLMs (LLaMA, Mistral, many others) use RMSNorm for the speed with no measurable quality loss.

### Q6. Why do LayerNorm and RMSNorm include learnable gamma (and beta) parameters?

Because forcing every layer's activations to exactly mean 0 / variance 1 is too rigid — the network may *want* a particular scale or shift for a given feature. The learnable `gamma` (per-feature gain) and, in LayerNorm, `beta` (per-feature bias) restore that freedom:

```text
out = gamma * normalize(x) + beta
```

If the optimal thing is to leave activations normalized, the net leaves `gamma = 1, beta = 0`. If some feature should be amplified or offset, it learns non-trivial values — in the limit it could even undo the normalization entirely. So normalization gives a good, stable *starting* distribution, and `gamma`/`beta` let the network deviate from it where useful. They are ordinary learned parameters, updated by backprop like any weight.

### Q7. What is the difference between pre-LN and post-LN, and which do modern LLMs use?

It's where the norm sits relative to the residual add.

```text
Post-LN (original 2017 Transformer):
    x = LN(x + sublayer(x))         # norm AFTER the residual add

Pre-LN (modern GPT-style):
    x = x + sublayer(LN(x))         # norm INSIDE the residual branch
```

In **post-LN** the normalization sits on the main residual path, so the clean identity highway is interrupted by a LayerNorm at every layer — the gradient gets rescaled at each step and, in deep nets, this makes training fragile (prone to divergence, needs careful warmup). In **pre-LN** the residual path `x + ...` is a pure, unnormalized identity highway; the norm only touches the *branch* feeding the sublayer. That keeps the gradient highway clean end-to-end, so training is far more stable at depth. Essentially all modern decoder-only LLMs use pre-LN (usually pre-RMSNorm).

### Q8. Why does pre-LN reduce or remove the need for learning-rate warmup?

Warmup — starting with a tiny learning rate and ramping up over the first few thousand steps — exists because early in training, large updates blow up the loss. Post-LN transformers are especially sensitive: because the norm sits on the residual path, gradients at initialization can be badly scaled (they grow with depth), so a normal learning rate immediately diverges, and you need warmup to tiptoe past the unstable early phase. Pre-LN keeps the residual path an unnormalized identity, so at initialization gradients are well-behaved and roughly depth-independent. The result is a much larger stable learning-rate range from step 0, so pre-LN models can use little or no warmup and are far more forgiving of hyperparameters. This robustness is a big part of why deep models became routine to train.

### Q9. Walk through the data and gradient flow of one pre-LN transformer block.

```text
Forward (shapes all (batch, seq, d_model)):
    residual = x
    a = LN_1(x)                 # normalize the branch input
    a = MHA(a)                  # mix info across positions
    x = residual + a            # residual add 1  (highway preserved)

    residual = x
    b = LN_2(x)
    b = FFN(b)                  # process each position independently
    x = residual + b            # residual add 2
    return x                    # shape unchanged: (batch, seq, d_model)
```

Forward: the representation `x` flows straight down the two `residual + ...` adds; each sublayer sees a *normalized* copy and writes a delta back onto the highway. Backward: the gradient enters at the block output and splits at each add — one copy goes straight up the residual highway (the `+1` path), one copy goes back through the sublayer and its LayerNorm. Because the highway copy is never attenuated, the gradient reaches the block below intact. Stack N of these and gradients still reach layer 0.

### Q10. Together, how do residuals and normalization make 100-layer transformers trainable?

They attack the two failure modes of deep nets:

- **Residual connections** solve *signal/gradient flow*: the identity highway means both the forward activation and the backward gradient can traverse all N layers without being multiplied down to zero. Each layer contributes an additive refinement rather than a fragile transformation the next layer depends on.
- **Normalization** solves *scale drift*: summing many residual contributions inflates activation magnitudes; LayerNorm/RMSNorm reset each token to a controlled scale before every sublayer, keeping inputs in the regime where attention and the FFN behave well and gradients stay bounded.

- **Pre-LN placement** ties them together: norm on the branch, identity on the highway — stable gradients at any depth, minimal warmup.

Remove residuals and a 100-layer net's gradients vanish; remove norm and activations/gradients blow up. With both (pre-LN), depth becomes a free axis to scale, which is exactly what large models exploit.

### Q11. Are LayerNorm/RMSNorm parameters, and roughly how many are there per layer?

Yes — `gamma` (and `beta` for LayerNorm) are learned parameters, updated by backprop. But they are tiny relative to the block. Each norm has `d_model` gain values (LayerNorm adds another `d_model` for the bias). A pre-LN block has two norms, so:

```text
RMSNorm block:  2 * d_model                params for norms
LayerNorm block: 2 * (2 * d_model) = 4*d_model
```

For `d_model = 4096` that's ~8k-16k parameters per block for norms, versus tens of millions for the attention and FFN weights in the same block. So norms are numerically negligible in the parameter count (the FFN dominates — see the architecture topic) but load-bearing for training stability. There is also usually one final norm before the LM head.

### Q12. Where exactly do the norms sit in a full model — how many are there?

In a pre-LN decoder-only model:

```text
embeddings
  -> block 1:  LN -> MHA -> add,  LN -> FFN -> add
  -> block 2:  LN -> MHA -> add,  LN -> FFN -> add
  -> ...
  -> block N:  ...
  -> FINAL LN                 # one extra norm here
  -> LM head (unembedding)
  -> logits
```

So there are `2 * N` norms inside the blocks (one before attention, one before the FFN in each of the N blocks) plus **one final norm** applied after the last block and before the LM head / unembedding projection. That final norm matters: pre-LN leaves the residual stream un-normalized, so without a closing norm the activations feeding the output projection would be poorly scaled. Post-LN models don't need this extra final norm because their norm already sits at the end of each block.

### Q13. Is BatchNorm ever a drop-in replacement here? What breaks?

No. Swapping LayerNorm for BatchNorm breaks in several ways specific to language modeling:

- **Autoregressive coupling** — BatchNorm mixes statistics across the batch, so one sequence's normalization would depend on unrelated sequences in the batch. At single-example inference (one user prompt) there's no batch to normalize over.
- **Variable length** — sequences differ in length; per-feature batch statistics across ragged sequences are ill-defined.
- **Train/inference mismatch** — BatchNorm uses running averages at inference that differ from training-time batch stats, adding a train/serve gap; LayerNorm computes the identical thing in both.
- **Small/streaming batches** — decode-time batch sizes are tiny or 1, where BatchNorm statistics are noisy or undefined.

LayerNorm sidesteps all of this by normalizing each token independently over its own features. That per-token independence is why it, not BatchNorm, became standard in transformers.

### Q14. Spot the misconception: "the residual connection is there so the model can represent the identity function."

That's a half-truth aimed at the wrong benefit. Yes, `x + sublayer(x)` makes it *easy* to approximate identity (drive the sublayer output to 0), but the reason we add residuals is the **backward pass**: the `+ x` guarantees a `+1` in the layer's Jacobian, so gradients flow to early layers without vanishing through dozens of multiplications. The "easy identity" property is a forward-pass consequence of the same wiring, and it's mildly helpful (a block that has nothing to add can stay out of the way), but if identity representation were the only goal you wouldn't see the dramatic difference in *trainability at depth* that residuals actually produce. Frame it as a gradient highway, and mention the identity property as a secondary nicety.

### Q15. Why not just initialize weights carefully instead of using normalization?

Careful initialization (e.g. scaling so activation variance is preserved at layer 0) helps at the *start* of training, but it doesn't hold up. As soon as weights update, activation scales drift — some features grow, some shrink — and the carefully-tuned starting variance no longer applies. Normalization re-establishes a controlled scale *at every forward pass, throughout training*, not just at initialization. It also decouples layers: each sublayer sees inputs in a predictable range regardless of what the layers below did this step, which smooths the loss landscape and lets you use larger learning rates. Good init and normalization are complementary — modern models do both — but you can't replace a per-step, adaptive mechanism (norm) with a one-time choice (init).

### Q16. In `x = x + sublayer(LN(x))`, why is the norm on the branch and not on the residual stream itself?

Because the whole point of pre-LN is to keep the **residual stream** — the running `x` that every block reads from and writes to — as a clean, unnormalized identity highway from embeddings all the way to the final norm. If you normalized the stream itself (`x = LN(x + sublayer(...))`, i.e. post-LN), you'd re-insert a scaling operation onto the highway at every layer, degrading the gradient path and reintroducing the depth instability pre-LN was designed to fix. Putting `LN` only on the *branch input* means the sublayer still receives well-scaled inputs (so attention/FFN behave), while the additive highway stays untouched. Think of it as: normalize what you *read* for computation, but never disturb the shared stream you *accumulate* onto.

## Stacking Depth: The Full Architecture

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

How one transformer block becomes a whole language model. Everything so far — embeddings, attention, the FFN, norm, residuals — describes a *single* block whose input and output are both shape `(batch, seq, d_model)`. Because input shape equals output shape, blocks are **stackable**: you run N identical-in-shape blocks in sequence, and that stack *is* the model's depth. This topic assembles the complete **decoder-only GPT** end to end: token IDs -> embedding + positional -> N transformer blocks -> final norm -> **unembedding / LM head** `(d_model, vocab)` -> logits. It covers the **parameter-count arithmetic** (embeddings `vocab*d_model`, plus per block roughly `4*d_model^2` for attention and `8*d_model^2` for the FFN, times N), worked on a concrete example; **weight tying** between the input embedding and the output head; and the **depth (N) versus width (d_model)** trade-off. The 15 questions run from "how do blocks stack" to "compute the parameter count of a given config" and "when do you add layers versus widen them".

**Mental model**

A GPT is a sandwich. The **bread** is the vocabulary interface: an embedding matrix on the way in that turns token IDs into vectors, and an unembedding matrix (the LM head) on the way out that turns the final vector back into a score per vocabulary token. The **filling** is a tall stack of identical transformer blocks, each one reading the `(batch, seq, d_model)` residual stream, adding its refinement, and passing the same-shaped tensor to the next. Depth is literally how many blocks; width is `d_model`, the size of each token's vector. Every block does the same *kind* of work (mix across positions via attention, then process each position via the FFN), but learns different weights, so early layers tend to capture surface/syntactic structure and later layers more abstract/semantic structure. Nothing exotic happens at the top — the final block's output is normalized once and projected to `vocab` logits. Scaling the model means making the stack taller (more N), the vectors wider (bigger `d_model`), or both.

**Key terms**

- **Decoder-only** — the modern GPT architecture: a stack of causally-masked transformer blocks, no encoder.
- **Embedding matrix** — `(vocab, d_model)`; maps each token ID to its row vector.
- **Positional encoding** — position information added/applied so attention isn't order-blind.
- **Transformer block / layer** — one attention + FFN unit wrapped in norm + residuals; the repeated unit.
- **N / depth** — number of stacked blocks (e.g. 12, 32, 96).
- **d_model / width** — dimension of the residual stream / each token vector.
- **Final LayerNorm** — the single norm after the last block, before the head.
- **LM head / unembedding** — `(d_model, vocab)` projection producing one logit per vocab token.
- **Weight tying** — sharing the same matrix for input embedding and output head (transpose).
- **Logits** — the raw `(batch, seq, vocab)` output scores, pre-softmax.
- **Parameter count** — total learned weights; dominated by the per-layer attention+FFN times N (plus embeddings).

**Why interviewers ask this**

Assembling the full stack is the "can you actually build a GPT?" checkpoint. Anyone can name attention; fewer can say what wraps around N blocks to turn them into a text model — the embedding in, the final norm, the head out, and that logits (not probabilities) come out. The parameter-count question is a favourite because it's concrete and unfakeable: a senior can derive that the FFN (~`8*d_model^2`) dominates each layer, multiply by N, add the embedding term, and land within a factor of the true count for a real model. It also surfaces architectural judgement — the depth-vs-width trade-off, weight tying to save parameters, why embeddings are a big chunk of small models but a rounding error in large ones. Getting this right signals you understand the model as an engineered artifact with a memory and compute budget, not just a diagram.

**Common confusions**

- "Each layer has different structure." — No; every block is identical in shape and *type* of operation, only the learned weights differ.
- "The model outputs words/probabilities." — It outputs **logits**; softmax (at sampling) turns them into probabilities, and only then do you pick a token.
- "Embeddings are most of the parameters." — Only for small models or huge vocabularies; in large models the N stacked FFNs dominate.
- "Attention has the most parameters per layer." — The FFN usually has ~2x the attention's parameters (`~8*d_model^2` vs `~4*d_model^2`).
- "The LM head is a separate learned matrix." — Often it's *tied* to the embedding matrix (same weights, transposed), saving `vocab*d_model` parameters.
- "More layers is always better than wider." — It's a trade-off; both help, with diminishing returns and different compute/memory profiles.

**What follows from this topic**

This topic gives the static structure; **The Forward Pass** animates it — pushing a real batch through the same stack and tracking shapes to the logits. The blocks being stacked are the pre-LN blocks from **Normalization & Residual Connections**, and the internals of each (attention, multi-head, FFN) come from the attention and feed-forward topics. The logits produced at the top feed directly into **Sampling & Decoding**, and the parameter-count reasoning here underpins the scaling-law and training-compute discussion (FLOPs ~ 6 * N_params * D_tokens) in the training topics.

### Q1. Why can transformer blocks be stacked, and what does "stacking" mean concretely?

Because a block's output has the **same shape as its input**: `(batch, seq, d_model)` in, `(batch, seq, d_model)` out. That shape-preservation is what makes them composable — the output of block i is a valid input to block i+1, indefinitely.

```python
x = embed(token_ids) + positional      # (batch, seq, d_model)
for block in blocks:                    # N identical-shaped blocks
    x = block(x)                        # shape unchanged each time
x = final_norm(x)
logits = x @ lm_head                    # (batch, seq, vocab)
```

"Stacking to N layers" just means running N such blocks in sequence, each with its *own* learned weights. They're identical in architecture, not in parameters. The residual stream `x` threads through all of them, each block adding its refinement. Depth = N is thus a pure hyperparameter you can dial up without changing any block's design.

### Q2. Draw the full decoder-only GPT from token IDs to logits.

```text
token IDs                      (batch, seq)             integers in [0, vocab)
    |
    v  embedding lookup + positional
hidden states                  (batch, seq, d_model)
    |
    v  transformer block 1     (LN -> MHA -> add, LN -> FFN -> add)
    v  transformer block 2
    v  ...
    v  transformer block N     (shape preserved throughout)
    |
    v  final LayerNorm
    |
    v  LM head / unembedding   weight (d_model, vocab)
    |
    v
logits                         (batch, seq, vocab)      raw scores, NOT probabilities
```

The three parts: (1) a vocabulary-to-vectors front end (embedding + position), (2) the N-block stack that does all the computation on a fixed-shape residual stream, (3) a vectors-to-vocabulary back end (final norm + unembedding) yielding one logit per vocab token at every position. Softmax/sampling happens *after* this, not inside the model.

### Q3. What are the roles of the embedding matrix and the LM head, and what are their shapes?

They are the two vocabulary interfaces, mirror images of each other.

- **Embedding matrix** — shape `(vocab, d_model)`. Maps each input token ID to a dense vector by row lookup: `emb[token_id]`. Turns discrete symbols into geometry the network can compute on.
- **LM head / unembedding** — shape `(d_model, vocab)`. Projects each final hidden vector back to `vocab` scores (logits): `hidden @ W_head`. Row/column `v` scores "how much does this position predict token v next".

```text
in:   token_id  --lookup-->  (d_model,) vector      via (vocab, d_model)
out:  (d_model,) vector  --project-->  (vocab,) logits   via (d_model, vocab)
```

They're conceptually inverse operations on the same vocabulary space, which is exactly why they're often *tied* (next question): one matrix serves both directions.

### Q4. What is weight tying between the embedding and the LM head, and why do it?

Weight tying uses the **same matrix** for the input embedding and the output head (the head being the embedding transposed):

```python
emb = nn.Embedding(vocab, d_model)      # (vocab, d_model)
logits = hidden @ emb.weight.T          # head reuses emb.weight, shape (d_model, vocab)
```

Rationale:

- **Parameter savings** — you drop a whole `vocab * d_model` matrix. For `vocab=50k, d_model=768` that's ~38M parameters saved — significant for smaller models.
- **Consistency / regularization** — a token's input representation and the direction that predicts it as output share a space, which empirically helps (Press & Wolf, 2017).

The trade-off is a small loss of flexibility (input and output roles are forced to share geometry), but in practice tying is common. Note some very large models untie them again because the `vocab*d_model` cost is negligible relative to the stack and untying gives a bit more capacity.

### Q5. Derive the parameter count of one transformer block.

Two sublayers dominate. Assume hidden feed-forward dimension `d_ff = 4 * d_model` (the usual choice) and ignore biases/norms (negligible).

Attention — four `d_model x d_model` projections (`W_q, W_k, W_v, W_o`):

```text
attn_params = 4 * d_model * d_model = 4 * d_model^2
```

FFN — two matrices, `(d_model, d_ff)` up and `(d_ff, d_model)` down, with `d_ff = 4*d_model`:

```text
ffn_params = d_model*d_ff + d_ff*d_model
           = 2 * d_model * (4*d_model) = 8 * d_model^2
```

So per block:

```text
block_params ~= 4*d_model^2 + 8*d_model^2 = 12 * d_model^2
```

Key takeaways: the FFN holds **~2x** the attention's parameters, and a block scales with the **square** of width. (SwiGLU FFNs use three matrices at ~2/3 the hidden width, landing near the same count.)

### Q6. Estimate the total parameter count of a full model from its config.

Add the embedding term to N blocks:

```text
total ~= vocab*d_model  +  N * 12 * d_model^2
         \___________/     \_________________/
          embeddings         the N-block stack
```

(With weight tying the embedding is counted once and serves as the head too; untied adds another `vocab*d_model`.) For large models the second term dominates because it grows with both N and `d_model^2`, while the embedding grows only linearly in `d_model`. This is also why you'll hear "the FFNs are where the parameters live" — across N layers the `8*d_model^2` FFN term is the single biggest bucket. The formula gets you within a small factor of the real published counts, which is all an interviewer wants.

### Q7. Work a concrete example: parameter count for d_model=768, N=12, vocab=50257.

Use `total ~= vocab*d_model + N * 12 * d_model^2` (this is roughly GPT-2 small).

```text
d_model^2      = 768 * 768        = 589,824
per block      = 12 * 589,824     = 7,077,888        (~7.08M)
all N blocks   = 12 * 7,077,888   = 84,934,656       (~84.9M)
embeddings     = 50257 * 768      = 38,597,376       (~38.6M)  (tied: counted once)

total          ~= 84.9M + 38.6M   ~= 123.5M parameters
```

That lands right on GPT-2 small's ~124M. Notes: the embedding (~39M) is a *large* fraction here (~31%) because the model is small and the vocab is big; in a 70B model the same `vocab*d_model` term would be a rounding error. Within a block the FFN (~4.7M) is twice the attention (~2.4M), as the `8` vs `4` coefficients predict.

### Q8. In a large model, what dominates the parameter count — embeddings, attention, or FFN?

The **FFNs, summed across all N layers**. Per block the FFN is `~8*d_model^2` versus attention's `~4*d_model^2`, so the FFN alone is two-thirds of each block, and there are N of them. The embedding term `vocab*d_model` is only *linear* in `d_model`, so as models scale (large N, large `d_model`) it becomes a shrinking fraction:

```text
small model (d_model 768, N 12):  embeddings ~31% of params
large model (d_model 8192, N 80): embeddings ~1-2% of params
```

So the mental ranking for big models is: FFN weights (biggest) > attention weights > embeddings (smallest). This is also why people say the FFN acts as the model's main "memory"/knowledge store — it's simply where most of the weights are.

### Q9. What is the depth-versus-width trade-off (N vs d_model)?

Both add capacity, differently:

- **Depth (N)** — more sequential blocks = more rounds of "mix across positions, then refine each position". Depth builds *compositional / hierarchical* representations (each layer transforms the previous), but it's inherently sequential: layer i+1 waits on layer i, so very deep models are harder to parallelize and can be trickier to train stably (mitigated by pre-LN + residuals).
- **Width (d_model)** — bigger token vectors = more features per position, more attention heads, a bigger FFN. Width parallelizes well on GPUs (bigger matmuls) and cheaply grows parameters (params scale as `d_model^2`).

Practical guidance: there's a rough sweet-spot aspect ratio (`d_model` grows faster than N as models scale). Too deep and thin under-uses hardware and risks optimization issues; too wide and shallow can lack representational depth. Real large models scale both, with width taking the larger share of the growth.

### Q10. If you double d_model, how do parameters and compute change? What about doubling N?

- **Double `d_model`** — per-block params `~12*d_model^2` scale with the **square**, so block params **4x**; the embedding term `vocab*d_model` merely doubles. Compute (matmuls) also scales ~`d_model^2` per token, so FLOPs roughly **4x**. Doubling width is expensive — quadratic.
- **Double N** — params in the stack scale **linearly**, so stack params (and the stack's FLOPs) **2x**; the embedding term is unchanged. Depth is linear.

```text
2x d_model  ->  ~4x block params, ~4x block FLOPs   (quadratic)
2x N        ->  ~2x stack params, ~2x stack FLOPs   (linear)
```

So widening buys capacity faster (per unit) but at quadratic cost and quadratic compute; deepening is linear in both. This asymmetry is part of why scaling recipes grow width and depth together rather than just stacking endless thin layers.

### Q11. Why is there a single final LayerNorm after the last block?

In a pre-LN model the residual stream is never normalized on its main path — each block only normalizes its *branch* input, and adds an un-normalized delta back to the stream. After N blocks that accumulated stream can have a large, uncontrolled scale. Feeding it straight into the LM head would produce poorly-scaled logits and unstable training. The **final LayerNorm** fixes that: it normalizes the last hidden states once, right before the unembedding projection, so the head sees well-scaled inputs.

```text
x = blocks(x)          # un-normalized residual stream
x = final_norm(x)      # the one closing norm
logits = x @ W_head    # now well-scaled
```

Post-LN models don't need this extra norm because their per-block norm already sits at each block's output, including the last one.

### Q12. Do all N blocks share weights? Do they learn different things?

No sharing — each of the N blocks has its **own independent set of weights** (its own `W_q/k/v/o`, its own FFN matrices, its own norms). They're identical only in *architecture and shape*. (Some research variants tie weights across layers to save parameters, but standard GPTs do not.)

And yes, they specialize despite identical structure. Empirically:

- **Early layers** tend to capture local/surface features — token identity, nearby syntax, simple patterns.
- **Middle layers** build more relational structure — coreference, longer-range syntax, some semantics.
- **Late layers** assemble the abstract, task-relevant representation that the head reads off to predict the next token.

So depth gives a *pipeline* of transformations, each block refining what the previous produced — same machine, different learned function at each stage.

### Q13. What actually changes between one block and the next during a forward pass — the shape or the content?

Only the **content**; the shape is invariant. Every block takes `(batch, seq, d_model)` and returns `(batch, seq, d_model)`. What changes is the *values* in that tensor: each block reads the current residual stream and adds a refinement, so the same token position holds a progressively more processed representation as it moves up the stack.

```text
after embedding:  (batch, seq, d_model)   # raw token+position vectors
after block 1:    (batch, seq, d_model)   # same shape, refined values
after block N:    (batch, seq, d_model)   # same shape, fully processed
```

This shape-invariance is precisely what allows arbitrary depth and is what makes the "residual stream" framing apt: it's one fixed-width bus that every layer reads from and writes to. Shapes only change at the very ends (embedding lookup in, unembedding projection out).

### Q14. How does this decoder-only stack differ from the original encoder-decoder Transformer?

The 2017 Transformer had two stacks; GPT keeps one.

| | Encoder-decoder (2017) | Decoder-only (GPT) |
|---|---|---|
| Stacks | Encoder stack + decoder stack | Single decoder stack |
| Attention masking | Encoder bidirectional; decoder causal | Causal only |
| Cross-attention | Decoder attends to encoder output | None (no encoder) |
| Typical use | Seq-to-seq (translation) | Open-ended generation |
| Example | T5, original Transformer | GPT, LLaMA |

A decoder-only model drops the encoder and the cross-attention sublayer entirely; each block is just causally-masked self-attention + FFN. Everything in this topic — stacking, param counts, final norm, head — describes that single-stack design, which is what essentially all modern generative LLMs use. (Encoder-only models like BERT are the other one-stack variant, but bidirectional and not generative.)

### Q15. Given a target parameter budget, how would you pick N and d_model?

Start from the dominant term `total ~= N * 12 * d_model^2` (+ embeddings), then apply shape heuristics rather than maximizing one axis:

1. **Pick a sensible aspect ratio.** Empirically `d_model / N` sits in a broad band (very roughly tens-to-low-hundreds); you don't want an extremely deep-thin or wide-shallow model. Scaling studies suggest growing width somewhat faster than depth as budget increases.
2. **Solve for the pair** that hits the budget at that ratio. E.g. for ~1B params you might land near `d_model ~ 2048, N ~ 24` rather than `d_model 512, N ~ 400` (too deep) or `d_model 16k, N 1` (absurdly wide).
