3. **Respect hardware and stability** — width parallelizes better on GPUs; extreme depth stresses optimization (pre-LN helps but latency and pipeline cost grow).
4. **Set `d_ff = 4*d_model`, heads so `d_k = d_model/h` is ~64-128.**

The point for an interview: it's a *joint* choice guided by the quadratic-in-width param formula and empirical aspect-ratio rules, not "max out layers".

## The Forward Pass

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

One clean, end-to-end trace of a prompt through a decoder-only model, following the **tensor shapes** at every step so nothing is hand-waved. Where the architecture topic drew the static stack, this topic *runs* it: input token IDs `(batch, seq)` -> embeddings `(batch, seq, d_model)` -> through the N blocks (shape preserved) -> final norm -> LM head -> **logits `(batch, seq, vocab)`**. It nails down the parts people gloss: what "the final layer outputs" actually is; that the model produces a next-token prediction *at every position* simultaneously, but for generation you slice out the **last position's** logits `(vocab,)`; the **LM head / unembedding** projection; and the crucial, constantly-confused point that **logits are unnormalized scores, not probabilities** — softmax lives in sampling, not in the model. The 15 questions go from "what shape is the input" and "what comes out of the model" to "why logits at every position", "why only the last position for generation", and "spot the bug: someone thinks the model outputs probabilities".

**Mental model**

Think of the forward pass as a shape-preserving pipe with two nozzles. You pour in a grid of integers — `batch` sequences, each `seq` tokens long. The embedding nozzle inflates each integer into a `d_model`-vector, giving `(batch, seq, d_model)`. Then the tensor flows through N transformer blocks that **never change its shape** — same `(batch, seq, d_model)` in and out of every block, values getting richer each time. A final norm tidies the scale, and the output nozzle (the LM head) projects each `d_model`-vector to `vocab` numbers, giving `(batch, seq, vocab)`. Those numbers are **logits**: raw, unbounded scores, one per vocabulary token, at *every* position. Position i's logits are the model's prediction of token i+1. During training you use all positions at once; during generation you only care about the *last* one, softmax it into probabilities, sample a token, append it, and run the pipe again. The model itself stops at logits — it never emits a word or a probability on its own.

**Key terms**

- **Token IDs** — integer inputs, shape `(batch, seq)`, each in `[0, vocab)`.
- **Batch** — number of independent sequences processed together.
- **seq** — sequence length (number of tokens in the context).
- **Hidden states** — the `(batch, seq, d_model)` tensor flowing through the stack; the residual stream.
- **Shape-preserving** — each block maps `(batch, seq, d_model)` to the same shape.
- **Final LayerNorm** — the single norm applied to the last hidden states before the head.
- **LM head / unembedding** — `(d_model, vocab)` projection turning each hidden vector into vocab logits.
- **Logits** — raw `(batch, seq, vocab)` scores; unnormalized, can be negative, don't sum to 1.
- **Last-position logits** — the `(vocab,)` slice used to predict the next token in generation.
- **Softmax** — turns logits into a probability distribution; applied at sampling, not inside the model.
- **Parallel prediction** — training predicts the next token at all positions in one pass.

**Why interviewers ask this**

Tracing shapes end-to-end is the fastest way to tell who actually understands the model versus who has memorized a diagram. The trap questions are precise and revealing: "what shape comes out?" (`(batch, seq, vocab)`, not `(vocab,)`), "are those probabilities?" (no — logits), "which position do you use to generate?" (the last), "why does the model predict at every position if you only want the next token?" (training efficiency / teacher forcing). A senior answers all four crisply and can write the six-line forward function with correct shapes; a junior conflates logits with probabilities or thinks the model outputs a single token. Because it's all mechanical and checkable, there's no bluffing — this question quickly calibrates depth.

**Common confusions**

- "The model outputs a word / a token." — It outputs a `(batch, seq, vocab)` tensor of logits; picking a token happens later in sampling.
- "Logits are probabilities." — No; they're unnormalized scores (any real value, don't sum to 1). Softmax converts them.
- "There's one prediction per sequence." — There's a prediction at *every* position; you usually only *use* the last for generation.
- "Softmax is the model's last layer." — In standard decoder-only LMs, softmax is applied during sampling/loss, not as a model layer that emits probabilities.
- "The final LayerNorm is optional." — In pre-LN models it's needed to scale the residual stream before the head.
- "Shapes change as you go deeper." — Only at the ends; the N blocks are all shape-preserving.

**What follows from this topic**

The logits produced here are exactly the input to **Sampling & Decoding** — temperature, top-k, top-p all operate on that last-position `(vocab,)` logit vector, and softmax finally appears there. The all-positions logits feed the **cross-entropy next-token loss** in the pretraining topic (teacher forcing predicts every position at once). The shape-preserving stack is the one assembled in **Stacking Depth: The Full Architecture**, built from the pre-LN blocks of **Normalization & Residual Connections**. And the "run forward, take last logits, append, repeat" loop previews **Autoregressive Generation & the KV Cache**, which optimizes exactly this repeated forward pass.

### Q1. Trace a prompt through the model with shapes at every step.

```python
def forward(token_ids):                 # (batch, seq)   integers in [0, vocab)
    x = embedding[token_ids]            # (batch, seq, d_model)
    x = x + positional(seq)             # (batch, seq, d_model)  add position info
    for block in blocks:                # N shape-preserving blocks
        x = block(x)                    # (batch, seq, d_model)  each iteration
    x = final_norm(x)                   # (batch, seq, d_model)
    logits = x @ W_head                 # (batch, seq, vocab)    W_head: (d_model, vocab)
    return logits                       # (batch, seq, vocab)    raw scores
```

Summary of the shape journey:

```text
(batch, seq)  ->  (batch, seq, d_model)  ->  [N blocks, unchanged]  ->  (batch, seq, vocab)
```

Two shape changes only, both at the ends: the embedding lookup expands the last axis from an index to `d_model`, and the LM head projects `d_model` to `vocab`. Everything in between preserves `(batch, seq, d_model)`. The output is logits — not a token, not a probability.

### Q2. What exactly does "the final layer outputs" mean — what comes out of the model?

It means the **logits tensor**, shape `(batch, seq, vocab)`. For each of the `batch` sequences, for each of the `seq` positions, the model emits `vocab` numbers — one raw score per token in the vocabulary. That's the model's entire output; there is no word, no chosen token, no probability inside it.

```text
model output = logits[batch][position][token_id] = a real-valued score
```

Concretely, `logits[0, 5, :]` is a `(vocab,)` vector scoring every possible token as the continuation after position 5 of sequence 0. "The final layer" (the LM head) is a linear projection, so its output is unbounded scores, not a normalized distribution. Turning those scores into an actual next token — softmax then sample — is a *separate* step outside the model (the sampling topic).

### Q3. What shape are the input token IDs, and how do they become embeddings?

Input is `(batch, seq)` — a grid of integers, each in `[0, vocab)`, produced by the tokenizer. The embedding step is a **row lookup** into the `(vocab, d_model)` embedding matrix:

```python
token_ids                        # (batch, seq)         e.g. (2, 10)
x = embedding[token_ids]         # (batch, seq, d_model) e.g. (2, 10, 768)
```

Each integer indexes one row of the embedding matrix, replacing a scalar index with its `d_model`-length vector. So the last axis grows from "an index" to "a dense vector", turning `(batch, seq)` into `(batch, seq, d_model)`. This is the only place raw token identity enters the network. Positional information is then added (or applied via RoPE) so the otherwise order-agnostic attention can tell positions apart. From here on, everything is float vectors.

### Q4. Confirm the shape stays constant through the blocks. Why does that matter?

Yes — every transformer block maps `(batch, seq, d_model)` to `(batch, seq, d_model)`. Attention mixes information across the `seq` axis and the FFN transforms the `d_model` axis, but neither changes the tensor's shape; residual adds require input and output shapes to match.

```text
into block i:   (batch, seq, d_model)
out of block i: (batch, seq, d_model)   # identical for all i in 1..N
```

Why it matters: shape-invariance is what makes the stack *arbitrary-depth*. Because output shape equals input shape, block i+1 accepts block i's output unconditionally, so you can chain N blocks with no plumbing between them. It also means the "residual stream" is one fixed-width bus threading the whole model — every layer reads and writes the same `(batch, seq, d_model)` representation. Shapes only change at the embedding (in) and the LM head (out).

### Q5. What does the LM head / unembedding do, and what shape does it produce?

The LM head is a linear projection from the model's hidden width to the vocabulary, weight shape `(d_model, vocab)`. Applied to the final hidden states it produces one logit per vocab token at every position:

```python
x = final_norm(x)            # (batch, seq, d_model)
logits = x @ W_head          # (batch, seq, d_model) @ (d_model, vocab)
                             # -> (batch, seq, vocab)
```

So it maps the last axis from `d_model` to `vocab`. Intuitively, each column of `W_head` is a "detector direction" for one token; the dot product of a position's hidden vector with that column scores how strongly the model predicts that token next. It's the mirror of the embedding lookup (vector back to vocabulary), which is why the head is often the tied transpose of the embedding matrix. Output: `(batch, seq, vocab)` logits.

### Q6. Are the logits probabilities? If not, what are they and how do you get probabilities?

No — logits are **unnormalized raw scores**. Any real value (positive or negative), they don't sum to 1, and larger just means "more favored". To get a probability distribution over the vocabulary you apply softmax (in sampling, optionally with temperature):

```python
logits = model(ids)[:, -1, :]        # (vocab,) last-position logits, e.g. [2.1, -0.4, 5.0, ...]
probs  = softmax(logits / temperature)  # (vocab,) now in [0,1], sums to 1
```

softmax exponentiates and normalizes: `p_i = exp(logit_i) / sum_j exp(logit_j)`. Only after this do you have probabilities you can sample from or read off. The model deliberately stops at logits because (a) the loss (cross-entropy) and various sampling strategies want the raw scores, and (b) softmax is numerically better folded into those downstream steps. Calling the model's output "probabilities" is the single most common mistake here.

### Q7. The model computes logits at every position, but you only want the next token. Explain.

Because at position i the model outputs its prediction for token i+1 — the whole `(batch, seq, vocab)` tensor is `seq` next-token predictions in parallel, one per position. That's intentional and cheap:

- **Training** uses *all* positions at once: with a causal mask, predicting the next token at every position simultaneously (teacher forcing) gives `seq` supervised signals from one forward pass — hugely efficient.
- **Generation** only needs the prediction *after the last real token*, so you slice the last position:

```python
logits = model(ids)          # (batch, seq, vocab)   all positions
next_logits = logits[:, -1, :]   # (batch, vocab)    just the last -> predicts the next token
```

So the model doesn't do extra work "wastefully" — parallel per-position prediction is exactly what makes training fast. At inference you compute the same tensor (or, with a KV cache, effectively just the last position) and read off the last slice.

### Q8. For generation, which slice of the logits do you use, and what's its shape?

The **last position's** logits: `logits[:, -1, :]`, shape `(batch, vocab)` (or `(vocab,)` for a single sequence). Position `-1` corresponds to the final token of the current context, and its logits predict the *next* token — exactly what you want to generate.

```python
logits = model(context_ids)      # (batch, seq, vocab)
last   = logits[:, -1, :]        # (batch, vocab)   the next-token prediction
probs  = softmax(last / temp)    # (batch, vocab)
next_id = sample(probs)          # (batch,)         one new token per sequence
```

You ignore positions `0..seq-2` for generation (they predict tokens you already have). Then you append `next_id` to the context and repeat. This is why generation is a loop over single last-position slices, and why the KV cache optimization targets exactly this pattern: only the newest position's Q needs computing each step, attending over cached K/V for all prior positions.

### Q9. Write the minimal generation loop built on the forward pass.

```python
def generate(prompt_ids, n_new, temperature=1.0):
    ids = prompt_ids                      # (1, seq)
    for _ in range(n_new):
        logits = model(ids)               # (1, seq, vocab)   full forward pass
        last   = logits[0, -1, :]         # (vocab,)          last-position logits
        probs  = softmax(last / temperature)   # (vocab,)     now a distribution
        next_id = sample(probs)           # scalar            pick one token
        ids = concat(ids, next_id)        # (1, seq+1)        append, feed back in
    return ids
```

The three load-bearing lines: run the forward pass to get logits, **take the last position**, softmax **then** sample. Note the model is called on the whole growing `ids` each step here — correct but O(n^2) redundant work; the KV cache (its own topic) makes each step compute only the new token's contribution. Also note softmax appears *here*, in the loop, not inside `model()`. This loop is the essence of autoregressive generation.

### Q10. Spot the bug: a colleague says "the model's last layer is a softmax that outputs word probabilities."

Two errors. First, the model's last layer is the **LM head**, a *linear* projection `(d_model, vocab)` that outputs **logits** — unbounded raw scores — not a softmax and not probabilities. Second, it outputs scores over **vocabulary tokens**, not "words" (tokens are subwords), and at *every position*, giving `(batch, seq, vocab)`.

```text
their mental model:  hidden -> softmax -> word probabilities
actual:              hidden -> final_norm -> LM head (linear) -> logits (batch, seq, vocab)
                     softmax happens later, in sampling / loss
```

Why the distinction matters: cross-entropy loss and sampling strategies (temperature, top-k, top-p) operate on the *logits*; folding softmax into the model would throw away the raw scores those steps need and is numerically worse. The fix: "the model ends at logits; softmax is applied downstream during sampling (or inside the loss), not as the final layer."

### Q11. Why is softmax applied at sampling time rather than being a layer inside the model?

Three reasons:

- **Downstream needs raw logits.** The training loss (cross-entropy) is computed as `log_softmax` internally for numerical stability, and sampling controls (temperature, top-k, top-p) manipulate logits *before* normalizing. Emitting probabilities from the model would discard the raw scores these steps require.
- **Numerical stability.** Combining the softmax with the log (in the loss) as a single `log_softmax` avoids overflow/underflow from exponentiating large logits — better than softmaxing inside the model then taking a log outside.
- **Flexibility.** Temperature scaling `softmax(logits / T)` and truncation (top-k/top-p) are decode-time choices; keeping the model at logits lets you change sampling behavior without touching the model.

So the model is defined to output logits, and softmax is a thin, configurable step applied at sampling (or folded into the loss). This is standard for decoder-only LMs — "the model outputs logits" is the precise statement.

### Q12. What is the shape and meaning of logits[b, i, :]?

`logits[b, i, :]` is a `(vocab,)` vector: for sequence `b`, at position `i`, the model's raw score for **every** vocabulary token as the prediction of the token at position `i+1`.

```text
logits shape:      (batch, seq, vocab)
logits[b, i, :]:   (vocab,)   scores over the whole vocab
argmax over it:    the greedy next-token guess after position i
```

Meaning: because the model is causal, position `i` only saw tokens `0..i`, and its job is to predict token `i+1`. So this vector is "given the prefix up to token i, how much do I favor each possible next token". At the last position (`i = seq-1`) it's the next-token prediction used for generation; at earlier positions it's the training signal for the token that actually followed. High score = favored; you'd softmax it to get probabilities.

### Q13. Does batch size change anything about the forward pass logic?

No — batch is just a parallel stack of independent sequences; the per-sequence computation is identical. Every operation broadcasts over the `batch` axis, which never interacts across sequences (attention mixes across `seq`, not `batch`; LayerNorm is per token; the FFN is per position).

```text
batch=1:  (1, seq, d_model)   ...  (1, seq, vocab)
batch=B:  (B, seq, d_model)   ...  (B, seq, vocab)   same ops, B sequences at once
```

The only thing batching changes is throughput (more sequences per forward pass) and memory. The logic — embed, N shape-preserving blocks, final norm, head, logits — is unchanged, and each sequence's logits depend only on that sequence's tokens. (This independence is exactly why LayerNorm, not BatchNorm, is used: no cross-sequence coupling.) At generation you often batch multiple prompts, slice each one's last-position logits, and sample per sequence.

### Q14. How does the forward pass at training time differ from at generation time?

The forward pass itself is the same computation; how you *use* the output differs.

| | Training | Generation |
|---|---|---|
| Input | Full known sequence | Growing context, token by token |
| Positions used | **All** positions' logits | Only the **last** position |
| Purpose | Cross-entropy loss vs true next tokens | Sample the next token |
| Passes | One pass covers the whole sequence | One pass per new token (loop) |
| Teacher forcing | Yes (feed ground-truth prefix) | No (feed the model's own output) |
| KV cache | Not needed (single pass) | Yes (avoid recomputing the prefix) |

In training you exploit that the model predicts *every* next token at once: one forward pass over a length-`seq` sequence yields `seq` supervised predictions, compared to the shifted targets via cross-entropy. In generation you can't see the future, so you run the pass, take the last logits, sample, append, and repeat — turning one static pass into an autoregressive loop.

### Q15. End to end: what are the only two places the tensor shape changes, and why there?

Only at the **embedding (input)** and the **LM head (output)**; the entire N-block interior is shape-preserving.

```text
(batch, seq)                      # token IDs
   |  embedding lookup  <-- shape change 1: index -> d_model vector
(batch, seq, d_model)
   |  N transformer blocks        # NO shape change (residual stream)
(batch, seq, d_model)
   |  final norm                  # NO shape change
(batch, seq, d_model)
   |  LM head projection  <-- shape change 2: d_model -> vocab
(batch, seq, vocab)               # logits
```

Why exactly there: the two ends are the **vocabulary interfaces**. The embedding converts discrete token indices into `d_model` vectors (index -> geometry), and the LM head converts the final `d_model` vectors back into `vocab` scores (geometry -> vocabulary). Everything in between operates entirely within the fixed-width residual stream `(batch, seq, d_model)`, because attention (mix over `seq`) and the FFN (transform `d_model`) both preserve shape so residual adds line up. Two nozzles, one shape-preserving pipe.
## Sampling & Decoding

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

How the model's raw output — a vector of **logits** over the whole vocabulary at the last position — is turned into an actual next token. This is the bridge between the forward pass (which ends at logits) and autoregressive generation (which consumes one token per step). The 16 questions here walk through: logits vs probabilities, the **softmax**, **temperature** scaling, **top-k** and **top-p / nucleus** truncation, **repetition penalty**, **greedy** vs **sampling**, **beam search** and why LLMs rarely use it, why the same prompt gives different answers, and why even temperature 0 is not perfectly deterministic. Everything here is per-step and cheap — the heavy compute already happened in the transformer. This is also the layer product engineers tune via API knobs, but here we explain the *internal mechanism*, not the applied recipe (see the AI Engineering primer for the product view).

**Mental model**

The transformer's last layer emits **logits** of shape `(vocab,)` for the position you want to extend — one unnormalized score per possible next token. Decoding is a two-stage pipeline: (1) **shape the distribution** — divide logits by temperature, then optionally zero out unlikely tokens via top-k / top-p, then renormalize with softmax; (2) **pick** — either take the argmax (greedy) or draw a random sample from the resulting distribution. Think of it as a dial from deterministic to creative: temperature T→0 collapses to always picking the single most likely token; T=1 samples from the model's honest distribution; T>1 flattens it toward uniform (wilder, more error-prone). top-k and top-p are safety rails that clip the long tail of garbage tokens *before* you roll the dice, so raising temperature stays coherent. The key realization: the model is not "deciding" — it outputs a probability distribution, and decoding is a policy for sampling from it.

**Key terms**

- **logits** — raw unnormalized scores over the vocab, shape `(vocab,)`; can be any real number, not probabilities.
- **softmax** — `p_i = exp(z_i) / sum_j exp(z_j)`; maps logits to a probability distribution (positive, sums to 1).
- **temperature (T)** — divides logits before softmax: `softmax(logits / T)`. T<1 sharper, T>1 flatter, T→0 ≈ argmax.
- **greedy decoding** — always take `argmax(logits)`; deterministic, can be repetitive/bland.
- **sampling** — draw the next token randomly from the softmax distribution; the source of output variety.
- **top-k** — keep only the k highest-probability tokens, renormalize, sample from those.
- **top-p / nucleus** — keep the smallest set of tokens whose cumulative probability >= p, sample from those.
- **repetition / presence / frequency penalty** — subtract from logits of already-seen tokens to reduce loops.
- **beam search** — track the b highest-probability *sequences* in parallel; maximizes sequence likelihood.
- **min-p** — keep tokens with prob >= (p * max_prob); an adaptive alternative to top-p.
- **determinism** — same inputs -> same output; only guaranteed at T=0 *and* with identical numerics/batching.

**Why interviewers ask this**

Sampling separates candidates who treat the model as a black box from those who understand its output is a distribution. A junior says "the model outputs text"; a senior says "the model outputs logits over the vocab, and decoding samples from softmax(logits/T)." The classic probe — "why does the same prompt give different answers?" — instantly reveals whether you know the randomness lives in the *sampler*, not the weights. Follow-ups test depth: can you explain why temperature is a division and not a multiplication, why top-p adapts to the distribution's shape where top-k doesn't, why beam search (great for translation) hurts open-ended chat, and the subtle one — why even temperature 0 can drift due to floating-point non-associativity, batching, and MoE routing. Getting these right signals you could debug a flaky generation or set inference defaults correctly.

**Common confusions**

- "Logits are probabilities" — no; logits are unnormalized and can be negative. Softmax makes them a distribution.
- "Temperature adds randomness" — temperature only *reshapes* the distribution; the randomness comes from the sampling step. At T=0 there is no randomness regardless.
- "top-k and top-p do the same thing" — top-k keeps a fixed count; top-p keeps a variable count sized to the distribution's confidence. On a peaked distribution top-p may keep 1 token; on a flat one, many.
- "Greedy gives the best/most-likely sequence" — greedy maximizes each *step* locally, which is not the globally most-likely sequence; beam search approximates that, and even that is often not what you want for chat.
- "Temperature 0 is fully deterministic" — deterministic in the sampling rule, but numerics (GPU reduction order, batch composition, MoE expert routing) can still flip a near-tie argmax.

**What follows from this topic**

Decoding consumes the last-position logits produced by the **forward pass** and feeds its chosen token into the loop described in **Autoregressive Generation & the KV Cache** — sampling is the per-step decision inside that loop. The distribution being sampled is entirely a product of what the model learned in **Pretraining** (and reshaped by post-training). The temperature/top-p knobs you expose here are the same ones the applied AI Engineering primer covers from the API side; here you now know what they do to the tensor.

### Q1. Walk through how a single next token gets chosen, from logits to a sampled token ID.

The transformer's final LM head produces **logits** for the last position: a vector of shape `(vocab,)`, one raw score per candidate token. Decoding turns that into one token ID:

```text
logits            (vocab,)   raw scores, e.g. [2.1, -0.4, 5.3, ...]
  -> divide by T            temperature scaling: logits / T
  -> truncate (optional)    top-k / top-p zero out the tail
  -> softmax                probs = exp(z)/sum(exp(z)), sums to 1
  -> pick                   argmax (greedy) OR random draw (sampling)
  -> next_token_id
```

Only the **last** position's logits matter for generation — the earlier positions were already committed. Logits are unnormalized: softmax is what makes them a probability distribution you can sample from.

### Q2. What is a logit, and why isn't it a probability?

A logit is the raw pre-softmax score the model assigns to each vocab token — the direct output of the final linear layer (LM head), shape `(vocab,)`. Logits are unbounded real numbers: they can be large, negative, or zero, and they do **not** sum to anything meaningful. To interpret them as probabilities you apply **softmax**:

```text
p_i = exp(z_i) / sum_j exp(z_j)
```

Softmax is monotonic (bigger logit -> bigger probability), makes every value positive, and forces the vector to sum to 1. The argmax token is the same before and after softmax — softmax only matters once you want to *sample* or read off calibrated probabilities.

### Q3. Explain the softmax function and why exponentiation is used.

Softmax maps a logit vector to a probability distribution:

```text
softmax(z)_i = exp(z_i) / sum_j exp(z_j)
```

Exponentiation does two jobs: it makes every output positive (so it's a valid probability), and it turns *additive* gaps in logits into *multiplicative* ratios in probability — a token whose logit is 2 higher is `exp(2) ~= 7.4x` more likely, regardless of the absolute level. It also connects cleanly to cross-entropy training: `-log(softmax)` is the loss, and the gradient of softmax+cross-entropy simplifies to `p - y`. In practice you subtract the max logit first (`exp(z_i - max(z))`) for numerical stability — this shifts all logits down so `exp` never overflows, and leaves the result unchanged.

### Q4. What does temperature do, mathematically and intuitively?

Temperature scales logits *before* softmax:

```text
p = softmax(logits / T)
```

Dividing by T>1 shrinks the gaps between logits, so softmax comes out flatter — more probability mass leaks to unlikely tokens, giving more random/creative (and more error-prone) output. Dividing by T<1 magnifies the gaps, so softmax sharpens toward the top token — more focused and repetitive. As T→0 the largest logit dominates completely and softmax approaches a one-hot at the argmax, i.e. **greedy**. As T→infinity it approaches uniform. Intuition: temperature is a confidence dial on the *same* underlying preferences — it never changes which token is most likely, only how decisively the sampler favors it.

```python
import numpy as np

def apply_temperature(logits, T):
    if T == 0:
        out = np.zeros_like(logits)
        out[np.argmax(logits)] = 1.0  # greedy limit
        return out
    z = logits / T
    z = z - z.max()                    # numerical stability
    e = np.exp(z)
    return e / e.sum()
```

### Q5. Why is it "divide by temperature" and not "multiply"? What happens at T<1 vs T>1?

The convention comes from statistical physics (the Boltzmann distribution), where high temperature means high entropy / more randomness. Dividing logits by a large T *reduces* their spread, which *increases* entropy of the softmax — so "high temperature = more random" matches intuition. Concretely:

```text
T = 0.5  -> logits doubled  -> distribution sharper -> more deterministic
T = 1.0  -> logits unchanged -> the model's honest distribution
T = 2.0  -> logits halved   -> distribution flatter -> more random
T -> 0   -> approaches argmax (greedy)
```

If you multiplied instead, the direction would invert and the T=1 identity would break. Keeping it a division makes T=1 the natural "as-trained" setting.

### Q6. Explain top-k sampling and its main weakness.

top-k keeps only the **k** tokens with the highest logits, discards the rest (sets them to `-inf`), renormalizes with softmax over the survivors, and samples from that. It caps the risk of drawing a truly bad token from the long tail while still allowing variety among the top candidates.

```python
def top_k(logits, k):
    idx = np.argpartition(logits, -k)[-k:]   # indices of k largest
    mask = np.full_like(logits, -np.inf)
    mask[idx] = logits[idx]
    return mask                              # feed to softmax
```

Weakness: **k is fixed** but the distribution's shape isn't. When the model is very confident (one token at 0.95), k=40 drags in 39 junk tokens. When it's genuinely uncertain (mass spread over hundreds), k=40 may chop off good options. A fixed cutoff doesn't adapt — which is exactly what top-p fixes.

### Q7. Explain top-p (nucleus) sampling and how it differs from top-k.

top-p keeps the **smallest set of tokens whose cumulative probability >= p** (the "nucleus"), then samples from that set after renormalizing. You sort tokens by probability descending, accumulate until you cross p (e.g. 0.9), and drop everything after.

```python
def top_p(probs, p):
    order = np.argsort(probs)[::-1]          # high -> low
    cum = np.cumsum(probs[order])
    cutoff = np.searchsorted(cum, p) + 1     # first index crossing p
    keep = order[:cutoff]
    out = np.zeros_like(probs)
    out[keep] = probs[keep]
    return out / out.sum()
```

| | top-k | top-p |
|---|---|---|
| Cutoff | fixed count (k) | adaptive count (cumulative prob >= p) |
| Peaked dist | may keep junk | keeps few (maybe 1) |
| Flat dist | may drop good tokens | keeps many |
| Adapts to confidence | no | yes |

top-p adapts the candidate set to how confident the model is, which is why it's the more common default. The two are often combined (top-k then top-p as a backstop).

### Q8. What is a repetition penalty and why is it needed?

LLMs, especially with low temperature or greedy decoding, fall into loops ("the the the" or repeating a sentence) because a token that appeared once often has a self-reinforcing high logit. A repetition penalty subtracts from (or divides) the logits of tokens that have already appeared, before sampling:

```text
frequency penalty: logit[t] -= alpha * count(t in output)
presence penalty:  logit[t] -= beta  if t appeared at all
repetition penalty: logit[t] /= r    if t appeared (r > 1)
```

Frequency penalty scales with how many times a token was used; presence penalty is a flat one-time hit; the multiplicative repetition penalty (used by many local runtimes) divides positive logits and multiplies negative ones by r. All three discourage the model from reusing tokens, trading some fluency for diversity. Too strong and it avoids necessary words (articles, a subject's name).

### Q9. Greedy vs sampling — compare them and say when each is appropriate.

| | Greedy (argmax) | Sampling |
|---|---|---|
| Rule | pick highest-logit token every step | draw from softmax(logits/T) |
| Determinism | deterministic | random (seedable) |
| Output feel | safe, repetitive, can loop | varied, creative, occasional errors |
| Good for | extraction, classification, code where one answer is right | chat, brainstorming, creative writing |

Greedy commits to the locally most probable token, which is great when there's a single correct continuation (structured output, deterministic tests) but tends toward bland or looping text in open-ended generation. Sampling with temperature + top-p injects controlled randomness so the model explores plausible alternatives. Most chat deployments sample; most eval/tool pipelines use greedy (T=0) for reproducibility.

### Q10. What is beam search and why do LLMs rarely use it for open-ended generation?

Beam search keeps the **b** most probable partial *sequences* (beams) at each step instead of one, expanding each and retaining the top b by cumulative log-probability, so it approximates the globally most-likely sequence rather than greedily best-per-step:

```text
step: for each of b beams, expand all vocab continuations,
      score by sum of log p, keep the top b sequences overall
```

It shines in tasks with a "correct" high-likelihood target — machine translation, summarization, speech. But for open-ended chat it's a poor fit: (1) the most-probable sequence is often generic and repetitive ("I don't know, I don't know") because high likelihood correlates with blandness; (2) it kills diversity — all beams converge to similar text; (3) it costs b times the compute and complicates the KV cache. LLMs favor sampling (top-p) which produces more natural, varied output. Beam search optimizes likelihood; chat wants something more human than the likeliest string.

### Q11. Why does the same prompt give different answers on different runs?

Because generation **samples** from a probability distribution rather than reading off a fixed answer. At each step the model produces logits, softmax turns them into probabilities, and the sampler draws a token at random (weighted by those probabilities). Different random draws -> different tokens -> the sequence diverges, and each divergence changes the next distribution, compounding the difference. Turn temperature to 0 (greedy) and — numerics aside — you get the same output every time, because argmax removes the random draw. So variability is a property of the *decoding policy*, not the weights: the model is identical run to run; the dice are not. Providers expose a `seed` so you can fix the RNG and reproduce a sampled run.

### Q12. Why can even temperature 0 produce slightly different outputs?

T=0 removes sampling randomness — you take the argmax — but several non-sampling sources remain:

- **Floating-point non-associativity**: `(a + b) + c != a + (b + c)` in float. GPU reductions sum in whatever order the parallel schedule dictates, so logits can differ in the last bits between runs. When two tokens are near-tied, that noise flips the argmax.
- **Batching / kernel selection**: the same request batched with different neighbors, or a different GPU/kernel/library version, changes reduction order and thus the tiny numerical result.
- **MoE routing**: in Mixture-of-Experts models, token-to-expert routing can depend on the batch composition, so which experts fire (and thus the logits) shifts subtly.

None of these are "sampling" — they're numerical determinism gaps. They matter only when the top-1 margin is razor-thin, which is why T=0 is *mostly* but not *perfectly* reproducible across environments.

### Q13. Write the full sampling function combining temperature, top-k, and top-p.

```python
import numpy as np

def sample_next(logits, T=1.0, k=None, p=None, rng=np.random):
    logits = logits.astype(np.float64)

    if T == 0:                                  # greedy
        return int(np.argmax(logits))

    logits = logits / T                         # temperature

    if k is not None:                           # top-k mask
        kth = np.sort(logits)[-k]
        logits = np.where(logits < kth, -np.inf, logits)

    z = logits - logits.max()                   # stable softmax
    probs = np.exp(z); probs /= probs.sum()

    if p is not None:                           # top-p (nucleus)
        order = np.argsort(probs)[::-1]
        cum = np.cumsum(probs[order])
        cut = np.searchsorted(cum, p) + 1
        keep = order[:cut]
        mask = np.zeros_like(probs); mask[keep] = probs[keep]
        probs = mask / mask.sum()

    return int(rng.choice(len(probs), p=probs)) # sample
```

Order matters: temperature reshapes, top-k/top-p truncate the tail, then softmax renormalizes over survivors, then you draw. This is essentially what an inference server does per decode step.

### Q14. How do top-k and top-p behave on a very peaked vs a very flat distribution?

Take two cases. **Peaked** (model confident): probs like `[0.94, 0.03, 0.01, ...]`. top-k with k=40 keeps 40 tokens including 37 near-zero ones — mostly harmless since they rarely get drawn, but at high temperature that tail can still fire. top-p with p=0.9 keeps just the first token (0.94 already exceeds 0.9), effectively greedy — it *tightens* automatically. **Flat** (model uncertain): probs spread thinly over 500 tokens. top-k=40 hard-caps to 40, possibly dropping equally-good options; top-p=0.9 keeps however many are needed to reach 0.9 (could be 200), preserving the genuine spread. The lesson: top-p's candidate set *scales with confidence* while top-k's is rigid, which is why top-p degrades more gracefully across contexts.

### Q15. What are logprobs and what can you do with them?

logprobs are `log(softmax(logits))` for tokens — the log-probability the model assigned to each token it generated (or to the top alternatives at each step). Providers can return them per token. Uses:

- **Confidence / uncertainty**: a very negative logprob means the model was unsure at that step; useful for hallucination flags or "abstain if low confidence."
- **Perplexity / scoring**: `perplexity = exp(-mean(logprobs))` scores how well the model predicts a given text — used to rank candidates or evaluate.
- **Constrained selection**: compare summed logprobs of candidate completions (e.g. answer "A" vs "B") to pick the model's preferred one without generating freely.
- **Classification**: read the logprob of "yes" vs "no" at the first generated position instead of parsing prose.

logprobs expose the distribution the sampler is drawing from, which is why they're the hook for evals and calibration.

### Q16. If you wanted deterministic, reproducible generation, what would you set and what caveats remain?

Set **temperature = 0** (or equivalently greedy decoding) so the sampler always takes the argmax and there's no random draw; if the API only samples, also fix the **seed** and set top-p/top-k to neutral values. That pins the *algorithmic* randomness. Caveats that survive: floating-point non-associativity in GPU reductions, changes in **batch composition** (your request grouped with different others), different **hardware / kernel / library versions**, and **MoE routing** dependent on the batch — any of which can flip a near-tie argmax. So "deterministic" holds strongly *within the same serving environment and batching*, but is not guaranteed bit-for-bit across deployments. For eval reproducibility, pin T=0, seed, model version, and ideally run single-request (batch size 1) on fixed hardware.

## Autoregressive Generation & the KV Cache

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

How an LLM actually *generates* a sequence: not in one shot, but token by token in a loop, each new token conditioned on everything before it. This topic covers the generation loop (forward pass -> sample -> append -> repeat until a stop condition), why the naive version is quadratically wasteful, and the central optimization that makes inference practical — the **KV cache**. It also covers the two phases every request goes through, **prefill** (encode the whole prompt at once) and **decode** (emit one token at a time), why they have completely different performance characteristics, and where latency and GPU memory actually go. The 15 questions here connect the forward pass and sampling into a working generator and explain the systems reality behind "why is the first token slow but the rest stream fast" and "why does a long conversation eat GPU memory."

**Mental model**

Generation is a `while` loop wrapped around the forward pass. You feed the current sequence in, the model outputs logits at every position, but you only care about the **last** one; you sample a token from it, glue that token onto the sequence, and run again. The naive problem: each step re-runs attention over the entire growing prefix, recomputing the Keys and Values for tokens that haven't changed — pure waste that makes generating n tokens cost O(n^2) work. The fix: a token's K and V vectors only depend on that token (and its position), so once computed they never change. **Cache them.** Then each new step computes Q/K/V for only the *one* new token, appends its K/V to the cache, and attends over the whole cache — roughly constant compute per token, with cache memory growing linearly. This splits inference into a big parallel **prefill** over the prompt and a long, sequential, memory-bound **decode**.

**Key terms**

- **autoregressive** — each token is predicted from all previous tokens; generation is inherently sequential.
- **generation loop** — forward -> take last logits -> sample -> append token -> repeat until stop.
- **stop condition** — EOS token emitted, max_tokens hit, or a stop string matched.
- **KV cache** — stored Keys and Values for every past token, per layer, reused each step.
- **prefill** — the first forward pass over the entire prompt in parallel; fills the cache; compute-bound.
- **decode** — the per-token steps after prefill; one token in, one out; memory-bandwidth-bound.
- **TTFT (time to first token)** — latency of prefill; grows with prompt length.
- **TPOT / ITL (time per output token)** — latency of each decode step; roughly flat per token.
- **memory-bandwidth-bound** — decode is limited by moving weights + cache from HBM, not by FLOPs.
- **cache size** — `2 * n_layers * seq_len * n_kv_heads * d_head * bytes`; the linear-in-length memory cost.
- **causal mask** — ensures each token attends only to earlier tokens; what makes caching valid.

**Why interviewers ask this**

This is where "I've used an LLM API" becomes "I understand LLM inference." The generation loop tests whether you know generation is sequential and why (each token feeds the next). The KV cache is the single most important inference optimization, and being able to explain *why it's correct* (K/V depend only on past tokens, which are fixed) and *what it costs* (linear memory) is a strong senior signal. Interviewers probe the prefill-vs-decode distinction because it explains real production behavior: why TTFT scales with prompt size, why streaming feels steady, why long contexts blow up GPU memory, and why techniques like GQA/MQA and paged attention exist. Getting this right shows you could reason about serving cost, batching, and latency — not just call the endpoint.

**Common confusions**

- "The model generates the whole response at once" — no; it emits one token per forward pass, in a loop, each conditioned on the prior tokens.
- "The KV cache stores generated text" — it stores the *Key and Value tensors* per layer per past token, not tokens or logits.
- "Caching changes the output" — it doesn't; it's a pure speedup that recomputes nothing that would change. Same tokens, less work.
- "Prefill and decode are the same operation" — prefill processes many tokens in parallel (compute-bound); decode processes one token at a time (memory-bound). Their bottlenecks differ.
- "KV cache saves memory" — it *spends* memory (grows with sequence length) to save *compute*. Long contexts are memory-limited largely because of the cache.
- "Why not cache Q too?" — Q for a past token is never reused; each step only needs the new token's Q against all cached K/V.

**What follows from this topic**

The loop here calls the **forward pass** each step and hands its last-position logits to **Sampling & Decoding** to choose the next token — this topic is the outer loop those two live inside. The O(n^2) attention cost and the linear KV-cache memory pressure are exactly what modern efficiency work (FlashAttention, GQA/MQA, paged attention, sliding-window/long-context tricks) targets. And every token generated here is drawn from the distribution shaped by **Pretraining** and post-training. The applied AI Engineering primer's talk of "context window" and "streaming" is the product-facing shadow of the prefill/decode mechanics you now understand.

### Q1. Walk through the autoregressive generation loop step by step.

Generation extends a prompt one token at a time:

```text
tokens = tokenize(prompt)
loop:
    logits = model(tokens)          # forward pass, shape (seq, vocab)
    last   = logits[-1]             # only the final position matters
    next   = sample(last)           # temperature / top-p / greedy
    tokens.append(next)             # feed the new token back in
    if next == EOS or len == max: break
detokenize(tokens)
```

Each iteration: (1) run the model on the current sequence, (2) take the logits at the **last** position, (3) sample a next token, (4) append it, (5) check the stop condition (EOS token, max length, or a stop string). The appended token becomes part of the input next iteration — that feedback is what "autoregressive" means, and it's why generation is inherently **sequential**: token n+1 can't be computed until token n exists.

### Q2. Why is generation inherently sequential when the transformer processes a sequence in parallel?

During **training** and **prefill** the transformer sees the whole sequence and computes all positions in parallel — the causal mask lets each position predict its next token simultaneously because the ground-truth prefix is already known. But during **generation** the continuation doesn't exist yet: to produce token n+1 you need token n, which you only get by running the model and sampling. You can't parallelize across future tokens because each is an *input* to the next step. So the parallelism is *within* a forward pass (all layers, all heads, all current positions at once) but not *across* generation steps. This is the fundamental reason decode is slow and can't be trivially sped up by more compute — it's a data dependency, not a compute limit.

### Q3. In the naive loop, what work is wasted, and why is it O(n^2)?

Naively, each step re-runs the full forward pass over the *entire* current sequence. To generate token t you process t tokens; over a response of n tokens you do `1 + 2 + ... + n = O(n^2)` token-processings. The waste: for every already-generated token, you recompute its embedding, its Q/K/V at every layer, and its attention — even though *those tokens never change*. The Keys and Values of token 5 are identical whether the sequence is length 6 or length 600. Recomputing them every step is redundant. Attention itself is also O(n^2) per pass (each token attends to all others), so the naive loop compounds a quadratic pass over a growing sequence into brutal cost. The KV cache removes the cross-step redundancy.

### Q4. Explain the KV cache: what exactly is stored and why it's correct.

In attention, each token produces Query, Key, and Value vectors. A new token's output is `softmax(q_new @ K^T / sqrt(d)) @ V`, where K and V span *all tokens so far*. Crucially, the K and V of a **past** token depend only on that token and its position — with a causal mask, they never depend on anything that comes later. So once computed, they're frozen. The **KV cache** stores, per layer, the K and V tensors of every token seen so far. Each new step then:

```text
compute q_new, k_new, v_new  for the ONE new token
append k_new, v_new to the cache
attn = softmax(q_new @ K_cache^T / sqrt(d)) @ V_cache
```

It's correct because caching reuses values that the causal mask guarantees are unchanged — the output is bit-identical to recomputation, just without the redundant work. Q is *not* cached because a past token's query is never needed again.

### Q5. How does the KV cache change the per-token cost from O(n) to ~O(1) compute?

Without the cache, step t recomputes Q/K/V and attention for all t tokens -> O(t) work per step. With the cache, step t computes Q/K/V for only the **one** new token and attends its single query against the cached K/V — the expensive projections are done once per token for its whole lifetime. So the *compute* per decode step is roughly constant in the number of new tokens (it's O(1) in that sense, though the attention dot-product still scans the cache, an O(seq) memory read). Net: generating n tokens drops from O(n^2) to about O(n) total compute. The cost that *does* grow is **memory**: the cache size grows linearly with sequence length, and reading it each step is the new bottleneck.

### Q6. What is the memory cost of the KV cache, and how does it scale?

The cache holds, for every layer and every past token, one K and one V vector across the KV heads:

```text
cache_bytes = 2 * n_layers * seq_len * n_kv_heads * d_head * bytes_per_elem
              ^K and V     ^per layer  ^per token   ^head dim  ^e.g. 2 for bf16
```

It scales **linearly** in sequence length (prompt + generated) and in batch size (each sequence has its own cache). For a large model with many layers and heads at long context, this reaches many gigabytes — often rivaling or exceeding the weights during long-context or high-batch serving. That's why long context is *memory*-limited, why batch size is capped by cache memory, and why **GQA/MQA** (share K/V across query heads, shrinking `n_kv_heads`) and **paged attention** (allocate cache in pages to avoid fragmentation) exist — all attack this linear-in-length KV memory.

### Q7. Explain prefill vs decode and why their bottlenecks differ.

| | Prefill | Decode |
|---|---|---|
| Input | whole prompt at once | one token at a time |
| Parallelism | all prompt tokens in parallel | strictly sequential |
| Produces | first token + fills KV cache | one token per step |
| Bottleneck | compute (FLOPs) | memory bandwidth |
| Latency metric | TTFT (time to first token) | TPOT (time per output token) |

**Prefill** runs one big forward pass over the entire prompt, computing everyone's Q/K/V and attention in parallel and populating the cache — it's doing a lot of matrix multiply, so it's **compute-bound**, and its time grows with prompt length (long prompt -> slow first token). **Decode** then loops: each step is one tiny token's worth of math but must stream the *entire* model weights plus the growing KV cache from HBM to do it — the arithmetic is trivial relative to the bytes moved, so decode is **memory-bandwidth-bound**. This split explains almost all serving behavior.

### Q8. Where do time-to-first-token and per-output-token latency come from?

**TTFT (time to first token)** is dominated by **prefill** — the model must process the whole prompt before it can emit anything. It scales with prompt length (and system load / queueing), which is why a huge context or RAG dump makes the app feel slow to start. **TPOT / inter-token latency** is the cost of each **decode** step: one forward pass reading all weights + the KV cache from memory to produce a single token. It's roughly flat per token (creeping up slightly as the cache grows), which is why streaming feels like a steady drip once it starts. Total latency `~= TTFT + n_output_tokens * TPOT`. Optimizations target them separately: chunked/parallel prefill and prompt caching cut TTFT; quantization, GQA, and better batching cut TPOT.

### Q9. Why is decode memory-bandwidth-bound rather than compute-bound?

In a decode step you process a single token, so the matrix multiplies are tiny — you're multiplying a `(1, d_model)` activation against the weight matrices. But to do that you must **read every weight** of the model (and the whole KV cache) out of HBM into the compute units. The volume of *bytes moved* dwarfs the number of *FLOPs performed*, so the GPU sits idle waiting on memory rather than saturating its math units — a low arithmetic intensity regime. That's why: decode throughput tracks memory bandwidth, not TFLOPs; **batching** helps enormously (amortize one weight-read across many sequences' tokens); and **quantization** (int8/int4) speeds decode by shrinking the bytes to move. Prefill, by contrast, does big parallel matmuls over many tokens, so it *is* compute-bound.

### Q10. Show the difference between the naive and cached attention step in pseudocode.

```python
# naive: recompute everything every step
def step_naive(model, tokens):
    x = embed(tokens)                      # ALL tokens, every step
    for layer in model.layers:
        q, k, v = layer.qkv(x)             # recompute K,V for old tokens too
        x = attention(q, k, v, causal=True)
        x = layer.ffn(x)
    return lm_head(x)[-1]                   # keep only last logits

# cached: compute Q/K/V for the new token only
def step_cached(model, new_token, cache):
    x = embed(new_token)                    # ONE token
    for i, layer in enumerate(model.layers):
        q, k, v = layer.qkv(x)              # just the new token's Q,K,V
        cache[i].k = concat(cache[i].k, k)  # append
        cache[i].v = concat(cache[i].v, v)
        x = attention(q, cache[i].k, cache[i].v, causal=True)
        x = layer.ffn(x)
    return lm_head(x)                        # (1, vocab) -> next logits
```

The cached version never recomputes past K/V; it appends the new token's and attends over the accumulated cache. Same output, O(n) instead of O(n^2) total.

### Q11. Why don't we cache the Query vectors, only Keys and Values?

Because a token's Query is only used **once** — at the step where that token is the current one being generated, to attend over all prior K/V. After that step the token becomes "past," and past tokens are never re-queried: the causal mask means future tokens attend to this token's *K and V*, never its *Q*. So Q_old has no future consumer, while K_old and V_old are read at every subsequent step. Caching Q would store data nothing ever reads again. Each decode step therefore computes a fresh Q for the single new token and reuses the cached K/V of everything before it. This asymmetry falls directly out of the attention formula: the new token's query dots against all past keys, not the reverse.

### Q12. How does the causal mask make KV caching valid?

The causal (autoregressive) mask forbids any token from attending to tokens that come after it — implemented by setting the upper-triangular attention scores to `-inf` before softmax. This guarantees that token i's representation, and therefore its K and V, depend **only** on tokens 0..i. Nothing that arrives later can alter them. That invariance is precisely what licenses caching: because K_i and V_i are frozen the moment token i is processed, storing and reusing them yields identical results to recomputation. If attention were **bidirectional** (like BERT, where a token sees the whole sequence), adding a new token would change *every* token's K/V, and the cache would be invalid — you'd have to recompute the lot. Causal masking is what makes incremental decoding — and thus practical LLM generation — possible.

### Q13. Trace the shapes through prefill and the first decode step.

```text
PREFILL  (prompt of length P, one layer shown)
  input ids        (B, P)
  embeddings       (B, P, d_model)
  Q,K,V            (B, n_heads, P, d_head)   # all P tokens at once
  cache K,V   <-   (B, n_kv_heads, P, d_head)
  attn out         (B, P, d_model)
  logits           (B, P, vocab)  -> take [:, -1, :] = first next token

DECODE  (step t, one new token)
  input id         (B, 1)
  embedding        (B, 1, d_model)
  q,k,v            (B, n_heads, 1, d_head)    # ONE token
  cache K,V   <-   grows to (B, n_kv_heads, P+t, d_head)
  attn: q(1) vs K(P+t)  ->  (B, 1, d_model)
  logits           (B, 1, vocab)  -> next token
```

Prefill does the whole prompt in parallel (the `P` dimension), producing the first token and filling the cache. Each decode step feeds a single token (`seq=1`), appends its K/V so the cache length grows by one, and attends that one query against the full cache. The cache is the only thing that grows.

### Q14. What is prompt caching / prefix caching and how does it relate to the KV cache?

If many requests share a common prefix — a long system prompt, a few-shot preamble, a document you keep asking about — you can compute that prefix's KV cache **once** and reuse it across requests instead of re-running prefill every time. Since the KV of those prefix tokens is fixed (causal mask), it's safe to store and prepend. Benefits: prefill only runs over the *new* suffix, so **TTFT drops** dramatically and cost falls for repeated long contexts. This is exactly the "prompt caching" some APIs bill at a discount. It's the same KV-cache mechanism, just persisted and shared beyond a single generation rather than rebuilt per request. The catch: the cache is only reusable while the prefix is byte-identical and the model/version is unchanged.

### Q15. How do GQA/MQA and paged attention relate to the KV cache's cost?

Both attack the KV cache's linear memory footprint, which limits context length and batch size. **MQA (multi-query attention)** uses a single shared K/V head for all query heads; **GQA (grouped-query attention)** shares each K/V head across a *group* of query heads — a middle ground. Either way `n_kv_heads` shrinks, so the cache (`2 * layers * seq * n_kv_heads * d_head`) gets much smaller, cutting the memory read per decode step (which is why they also *speed up* the bandwidth-bound decode) at a small quality cost. **Paged attention** (from vLLM) tackles *fragmentation*: instead of reserving one contiguous max-length cache per sequence, it allocates the cache in fixed-size **pages** on demand, like OS virtual memory, so memory isn't wasted on unused slots and more sequences fit — raising batch size and throughput. All three exist because the KV cache, not the weights, is often what caps serving at long context.

## Pretraining

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

How a base LLM is created: the self-supervised **next-token-prediction** objective, the **cross-entropy loss** that drives it, **teacher forcing** during training, and what the resulting **base model** does and does not know. This is the training-time counterpart to everything the earlier topics described at inference time — the same forward pass, but now with a loss and a backward pass that update the weights. The 15 questions here cover why predicting the next token is enough to learn language/facts/skills, why it's called self-supervised (the data is its own label), how teacher forcing lets you train all positions of a sequence in parallel, the scale of data (trillions of tokens), the role of data quality, and the crucial gap this leaves — a base model is a *text completer*, not an assistant, which is exactly what post-training (SFT, RLHF/DPO) fixes. Understanding pretraining is understanding where the model's knowledge comes from and why it behaves like an autocomplete until aligned.

**Mental model**

Pretraining is one idea applied at massive scale: **guess the next token, check the answer, nudge the weights, repeat — over trillions of tokens.** Take a chunk of text, and at every position ask the model to predict the actual next token given everything before it. Because the true next token is right there in the data, you need no human labels — the text *is* the supervision (self-supervised). Compare the model's predicted distribution to the truth with cross-entropy loss (`-log` of the probability it assigned to the correct token), backpropagate to get gradients, and let the optimizer shift every weight slightly to make the correct token more likely next time. Do this across the internet, code, and books, and to get good at the seemingly simple game of prediction the model is *forced* to learn grammar, facts, reasoning patterns, translation, and code — because all of those help it predict better. The output is a **base model**: a fluent, knowledgeable text-completer that will happily continue "The capital of France is" but doesn't yet know it's supposed to be a helpful assistant.

**Key terms**

- **pretraining** — the initial, expensive self-supervised training on a huge unlabeled corpus.
- **next-token prediction** — the objective: model `p(token_t | token_0 ... token_{t-1})` for every position.
- **self-supervised** — labels come from the data itself (the next token), not human annotation.
- **cross-entropy loss** — `-log(p_model(true next token))`, averaged over all positions; what training minimizes.
- **teacher forcing** — feed the ground-truth prefix during training so all positions can be predicted in parallel.
- **base / foundation model** — the pretrained model before any instruction tuning; a raw text completer.
- **token budget / corpus** — trillions of tokens of web text, code, books, scraped and filtered.
- **perplexity** — `exp(cross-entropy)`; the average "branching factor," a common training metric.
- **epoch / step** — a step is one batch's forward+backward+update; big runs make few passes over the data.
- **shifted labels** — the target at position t is the input token at position t+1.
- **data quality** — deduplication, filtering, and mixture; often matters as much as raw quantity.

**Why interviewers ask this**

Pretraining is where the model's capabilities *come from*, so understanding it separates people who think LLMs are "trained on Q&A pairs" from those who know they're trained to predict text. The headline question — "how does predicting the next token teach it facts and reasoning?" — reveals whether you grasp that a simple objective at scale induces rich internal structure. Interviewers probe the loss (can you write cross-entropy in ASCII and say what `p` is?), teacher forcing (why can training parallelize across positions but generation can't?), and the base-vs-chat distinction (why does a raw base model ramble instead of answering?). Getting the last one right shows you understand *why post-training exists* — that helpfulness is bolted on after pretraining, not learned from the web. Strong answers connect pretraining to inference: the same next-token distribution you sample at generation time is the one this objective shaped.

**Common confusions**

- "It's trained on question-answer pairs" — no; pretraining is unsupervised next-token prediction over raw text. Instruction/answer behavior comes later, in SFT.
- "Self-supervised means unsupervised/no objective" — there *is* a crisp supervised objective (predict the next token); it's just that the labels are free, extracted from the data.
- "The base model is a chatbot" — it's a text completer. Give it "The capital of France is" and it continues; give it a question and it may write *more questions*. Assistant behavior is post-training.
- "More data always beats better data" — quality (dedup, filtering, mixture) often matters as much; garbage tokens waste compute and can hurt.
- "It memorizes the training set" — it mostly *generalizes* (learns patterns); it can memorize rare/duplicated strings, which is why dedup and privacy filtering matter.
- "Loss is accuracy" — the loss is cross-entropy over the full distribution, not whether the top-1 guess was exactly right; it rewards putting probability mass on the true token.

**What follows from this topic**

Pretraining produces the weights that every earlier topic runs at inference — the **forward pass** computes exactly the `p(next token)` this objective trained, and **Sampling & Decoding** draws from that distribution. The base model this yields is deliberately *not* an assistant; that gap is the entire motivation for post-training (SFT to teach instruction-following and the chat template, then RLHF/DPO to align it), which the following topics cover. The trillions-of-tokens scale here is what scaling laws (compute-optimal token/parameter ratios) quantify, and the memorization concerns connect to the privacy and data-quality themes throughout. In short: this topic explains *where the knowledge comes from*; the rest of the primer explains how it's shaped and served.

### Q1. What is the pretraining objective, in one sentence, and why is it enough?

The objective is **next-token prediction**: given a prefix of tokens, predict the probability distribution of the very next token, for every position in the corpus. It's enough because becoming good at this one game *forces* the model to learn everything that helps predict text — grammar and syntax (to form valid continuations), facts (to complete "The capital of France is ___"), reasoning and arithmetic (to continue a worked solution), translation, code structure, style, and more. There's no separate module for "facts" or "grammar"; they emerge as *useful internal machinery* for lowering prediction error across a vast, varied corpus. A deceptively simple objective, applied to trillions of tokens with a large enough model, yields broad competence — which is the central surprise of LLMs.

### Q2. Write the cross-entropy loss for language modeling in ASCII and explain each part.

For a sequence of tokens `x_0, x_1, ..., x_{T-1}`, the loss is the average negative log-probability the model assigns to each true next token:

```text
loss = -(1/T) * sum over t of log( p_model( x_{t+1} | x_0 ... x_t ) )
```

For a single position it's `-log(p_correct)`, where `p_correct` is the softmax probability the model put on the *actual* next token. Reading it: `p_model(...)` is the model's predicted distribution over the vocab at position t; you pick out the probability of the true next token `x_{t+1}`; take `-log` (so assigning high probability -> low loss, and assigning near-zero probability to the truth -> huge loss); average over all positions. Minimizing this pushes the model to concentrate probability mass on the tokens that actually follow. `exp(loss)` is the **perplexity** — an interpretable "how many tokens is it effectively choosing between."

### Q3. Why is next-token prediction called self-supervised rather than unsupervised or supervised?

It has a genuine **supervised** signal — a target label at every position (the next token) that the loss compares against — so it's not "unsupervised" in the sense of clustering with no target. But unlike classic supervised learning, **no human labeled anything**: the labels are extracted for free from the raw text itself. The input is `tokens[0:t]` and the label is `tokens[t]`; the data is its own answer key. That's the defining trait of **self-supervised** learning — a supervised objective whose labels are constructed automatically from the unlabeled data. This is what makes pretraining scalable: you can use essentially all the text on the internet without paying anyone to annotate it, which is why corpora reach trillions of tokens.

### Q4. Explain teacher forcing and why it lets you train all positions in parallel.

**Teacher forcing** means that during training you feed the model the **ground-truth** prefix at every position, rather than its own previous predictions. Because the entire correct sequence is known ahead of time, you can compute the prediction (and loss) for *all* positions in a single forward pass — position t is trained to predict token t+1 given the real tokens 0..t, and the causal mask ensures each position only sees its true past. All T targets are evaluated simultaneously:

```text
input:   [BOS]  the    cat    sat
targets:  the   cat    sat    [EOS]     # inputs shifted left by one
```

One forward pass over the sequence yields a prediction at every position, all supervised at once — hugely parallel and efficient. Contrast **generation**, which has no ground truth to feed, so it must run sequentially, feeding its *own* sampled tokens back in. Teacher forcing is why training parallelizes but inference doesn't.

### Q5. During pretraining, what exactly gets updated, and how?

The **weights** get updated — every learnable parameter: the token embedding matrix, the per-layer attention projections `W_q, W_k, W_v, W_o`, the feed-forward weights, the layer-norm gains, and the output/unembedding head. One training **step** is: (1) **forward** — run a batch of sequences through the model and compute the cross-entropy loss; (2) **backward** — backpropagation computes the gradient of the loss with respect to every weight; (3) **update** — the optimizer (typically AdamW) nudges each weight a small step in the direction that lowers the loss. Repeat for hundreds of thousands of steps over trillions of tokens.

```text
for batch in data:
    logits = model(batch.inputs)              # forward
    loss   = cross_entropy(logits, batch.targets)
    grads  = backprop(loss)                    # d loss / d weights
    weights = optimizer.step(weights, grads)   # AdamW update
```

What is *not* updated at inference: nothing — the weights are frozen once training ends. "Learning" is exactly this weight-updating loop.

### Q6. How can predicting the next token teach a model facts and reasoning?

Because correctly predicting text often *requires* the fact or the reasoning. To complete "The chemical symbol for gold is ___" with high probability, the model must have encoded that fact; to continue "17 * 3 = ___" or a step-by-step proof, it must have internalized the procedure; to finish a function body, it must model program semantics. The loss relentlessly rewards whatever internal representations lower prediction error, so over a huge, diverse corpus the model is *pressured* into building world knowledge, arithmetic circuits, syntactic structure, and reasoning patterns — not because it was told to, but because these are the most compact way to predict such varied text well. Prediction is a proxy for understanding: to model the data-generating process (humans writing about the world) accurately, you end up modeling a lot of the world. That's the deep reason a "mere autocomplete" objective produces broad capability.

### Q7. What is a base model, and how does it behave differently from a chat model?

A **base (foundation) model** is the raw output of pretraining: a fluent text-completer that predicts likely continuations, nothing more. It has language, facts, and skills but no notion that it should *answer* you. Behaviors:

```text
prompt: "What is the capital of France?"
base model  -> might continue: "What is the capital of Italy? What is the ..."
               (it completes the pattern of a quiz, not the answer)
chat model  -> "The capital of France is Paris."
```

A base model given "The capital of France is" will correctly continue "Paris" — because that's a text completion — but given an instruction it may ramble, echo, or continue the document rather than help. The **chat model** is the base model after **post-training** (SFT on instruction/response pairs, then RLHF/DPO), which teaches it to follow instructions, adopt an assistant persona, use the chat template, and refuse unsafe requests. Same pretrained knowledge; different learned *behavior*. This gap is the whole reason post-training exists.

### Q8. Why does a base model complete text rather than answer questions?

Because that's *literally the only thing it was trained to do*: predict the next token of naturally occurring text. The internet is full of documents where a question is followed by *more* questions (quizzes, forms, FAQs lists) as often as by answers, so the highest-probability continuation of a lone question isn't necessarily an answer — it's whatever the training distribution says usually follows. The base model has no concept of a "user" it should help or a "turn" it should take; it has no assistant persona and no instruction to be helpful. It's an extremely capable autocomplete with no goal beyond likelihood. Making it reliably answer requires showing it many (instruction -> helpful response) demonstrations in **SFT** and then reinforcing helpful behavior with **RLHF/DPO** — post-training that redirects the same capabilities toward being an assistant.

### Q9. Roughly how much data and compute does pretraining use, and how is that framed?

Modern LLMs pretrain on the order of **trillions of tokens** — filtered web crawl, code, books, and curated sources — over weeks to months on thousands of GPUs/TPUs. The rough training-compute rule is:

```text
FLOPs  ~=  6 * N * D          # N = parameters, D = training tokens
```

The factor ~6 accounts for the forward and backward passes per token. **Scaling laws** (Kaplan; Chinchilla) frame the trade: for a fixed compute budget there's a *compute-optimal* balance of parameters vs tokens — Chinchilla found roughly **20 tokens per parameter**, meaning many early models were too big and *under-trained* on too little data. So pretraining is fundamentally a compute-budget allocation problem: given your FLOPs, pick N and D to minimize loss. Data scale (D) and model scale (N) both matter, and pushing either without the other wastes compute.

### Q10. What role does data quality play versus raw quantity?

Enormous, but it's not "more is always better." Raw web crawl is full of duplicates, spam, boilerplate, and low-value text; training on it wastes compute and can *degrade* the model. Key levers: **deduplication** (repeated documents cause memorization and skew the distribution), **filtering** (remove junk, keep high-signal text — quality classifiers, heuristics), and **mixture / weighting** (up-weight valuable sources like code, curated books, or high-quality web; balance domains and languages). The mixture strongly shapes capabilities — more code improves reasoning; more of a language improves that language. Empirically, a smaller, well-curated corpus can beat a larger dirty one at equal compute. So the frontier is as much *data engineering* (dedup, filter, mix, decontaminate against test sets) as it is scale. Quantity buys breadth; quality buys efficiency and reliability.

### Q11. How do the training labels line up with the inputs? Show it concretely.

The target at each position is simply the input token at the **next** position — the labels are the inputs shifted left by one:

```text
tokens:  [BOS]  the    cat    sat    on
inputs:  [BOS]  the    cat    sat          # positions 0..3
targets:  the   cat    sat    on           # what each position must predict
```

So position 0 (seeing `[BOS]`) is trained to predict `the`; position 1 (seeing `[BOS] the`) predicts `cat`; and so on. In code you take the sequence, use `x = tokens[:-1]` as inputs and `y = tokens[1:]` as targets, run one forward pass, and compute cross-entropy between the logits at each position and `y`. The causal mask guarantees position t only attended to tokens 0..t, so it can't cheat by peeking at the answer it's predicting. This "shifted-by-one" setup is the entire supervision signal.

### Q12. What is perplexity and how does it relate to the loss?

Perplexity is the exponential of the average cross-entropy loss (in nats):

```text
perplexity = exp(cross_entropy_loss) = exp( -(1/T) * sum_t log p(x_{t+1}) )
```

Intuitively it's the model's **effective branching factor** — the average number of equally-likely tokens it's "choosing between" at each step. Perplexity 1 means perfect prediction (always certain of the next token); perplexity 50 means it's as uncertain as if picking uniformly among 50 options. Lower is better. It's a monotonic transform of the loss, so minimizing loss minimizes perplexity; people report perplexity because it's more interpretable than raw nats and comparable across models on the same tokenization. Caveat: perplexity depends on the tokenizer and the eval corpus, so it's only comparable under matched conditions — which is why held-out benchmark accuracy is often reported alongside it.

### Q13. Does the model memorize its training data or generalize? Why does the distinction matter?

Mostly it **generalizes** — it learns reusable patterns (grammar, facts as compressed associations, procedures) rather than storing the corpus verbatim, which is why it can handle inputs it never saw. But it *can* **memorize**, especially strings that are rare-yet-duplicated or highly distinctive (a repeated license text, a leaked secret, a famous quote). The likelihood of memorization rises with duplication in the data and with model capacity. Why it matters: (1) **privacy** — a model can regurgitate PII or copyrighted passages it memorized, a real risk that motivates dedup and filtering; (2) **eval integrity** — if benchmark test data leaked into training ("contamination"), high scores are meaningless, so labs decontaminate; (3) **generalization health** — heavy memorization can signal overfitting. This is a big reason **deduplication** is a core data-pipeline step: it both improves generalization and reduces memorization/leakage.

### Q14. Contrast pretraining with fine-tuning / post-training.

| | Pretraining | Post-training (SFT, RLHF/DPO) |
|---|---|---|
| Data | trillions of tokens, raw web/code/books | thousands-millions of curated examples |
| Objective | next-token prediction on raw text | next-token on demonstrations; then preference optimization |
| Supervision | self-supervised (free labels) | human-written/curated, human preferences |
| Cost | massive (weeks, thousands of GPUs) | small relative to pretraining |
| Result | base model — a text completer | chat/assistant model — follows instructions, aligned |
| Updates | all weights, from scratch | all or a subset of weights, starting from the base |

Pretraining builds the raw capability and knowledge; post-training *redirects* that capability toward being a helpful, safe assistant. SFT is still next-token prediction, just on curated (instruction, response) demonstrations, and RLHF/DPO then optimizes for human-preferred outputs. Crucially, post-training rarely teaches *new facts* — it shapes *behavior* on top of what pretraining already learned. Same engine; pretraining forges it, post-training aims it.
