### Q15. If pretraining already learned facts and language, why is post-training necessary at all?

Because knowledge and capability are not the same as **helpful, safe behavior**. Pretraining produces a model that *can* do enormous things but has no idea it's supposed to answer your question, follow your instructions, format a response, refuse harmful requests, or maintain a consistent assistant persona — it just predicts likely text, which often means continuing a document or echoing the prompt's pattern. Post-training supplies exactly those missing pieces: **SFT** on (instruction, response) pairs teaches instruction-following and the chat template (special tokens for system/user/assistant turns); **RLHF or DPO** then optimizes toward responses humans actually prefer — more helpful, honest, and harmless — using preference data the base objective never saw. In short, pretraining gives the model *what it knows*; post-training gives it *how to behave*. Without post-training you have a brilliant autocomplete, not an assistant.
## Training Mechanics: Backprop & Optimization

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

This topic explains what "learning" actually is inside an LLM — the mechanical loop that turns a randomly-initialised pile of weight matrices into a language model. It covers **backpropagation** (how the gradient of the loss with respect to every weight is computed via the chain rule), **gradient descent** (how those gradients nudge the weights), **exactly what gets updated** (the weight matrices — embeddings, W_q/W_k/W_v/W_o, FFN weights — and NOT the activations or the training data), the **optimizer** (Adam / AdamW, and why plain SGD isn't used), **learning-rate schedules** (warmup then decay), how a huge **effective batch size** is assembled via **gradient accumulation** and **data parallelism**, and **mixed precision** (bf16). The through-line is the single training step: forward pass to compute the loss, backward pass to compute gradients, optimizer update to change the weights. The 16 questions here answer the seeded interview classic "what actually gets updated during training". This topic assumes you already know the forward pass and cross-entropy next-token loss; it explains the reverse direction.

**Mental model**

Think of the network as one enormous differentiable function y = f(inputs; W) with billions of tunable knobs W. Training asks: which way should I turn every knob, by a tiny amount, to make the loss on this batch of text a little smaller? The forward pass computes the loss. The backward pass (backprop) is just the chain rule applied mechanically from the loss back to every weight, reusing the intermediate activations that the forward pass already computed — it produces one gradient number per weight: dL/dW. Gradient descent then does W = W - lr * gradient. Repeat this on trillions of tokens and the weights drift into a configuration that predicts text well. Nothing "understands" during a step; it is billions of tiny nudges down a loss surface. The optimizer (AdamW) is a smarter rule than raw subtraction: it keeps a running memory of each weight's gradients so it can take an adaptive, well-scaled step per parameter.

**Key terms**

- **Loss** — a single scalar measuring how wrong the model is on this batch; for LLMs, average cross-entropy `-log(p_model(true next token))` over positions.
- **Gradient** — the vector of partial derivatives dL/dW; points in the direction of steepest loss increase, so we step the opposite way.
- **Backpropagation** — reverse-mode automatic differentiation; one backward sweep computes every dL/dW using the chain rule and cached activations.
- **Gradient descent** — the update rule `W = W - lr * dL/dW`; SGD does this on random minibatches.
- **Learning rate (lr)** — step size; the single most important hyperparameter.
- **Adam / AdamW** — adaptive optimizer tracking per-parameter running mean and variance of gradients; AdamW applies weight decay correctly (decoupled).
- **Weight decay** — pulls weights toward zero each step; a regulariser.
- **Warmup + decay** — lr ramps up from ~0 over the first steps, then decays (cosine/linear) to near zero.
- **Batch size** — number of tokens/sequences per update; larger is a less noisy gradient estimate.
- **Gradient accumulation** — sum gradients over several micro-batches before one optimizer step, to fake a huge batch on limited memory.
- **Mixed precision (bf16)** — store/compute in 16-bit brain-float for speed and memory, with care around numerically sensitive parts.
- **Epoch vs step** — one step = one optimizer update; LLM pretraining often sees each token roughly once (well under one epoch of "repeat everything").

**Why interviewers ask this**

This is the fastest way to separate someone who has only used an LLM API from someone who understands the machine. A junior answer is vague — "it trains on data and gets better". A senior answer names the exact loop (forward, loss, backward, step), states precisely that gradient descent updates the weight matrices and not the activations or data, explains why AdamW rather than SGD (adaptive per-parameter steps, decoupled weight decay, robustness to the ill-conditioned loss surfaces of transformers), and can talk about lr warmup, gradient accumulation, and bf16 as the practical machinery that makes billion-parameter training stable. Interviewers also probe misconceptions: that backprop "sends data backward", that the data changes, or that Adam replaces backprop (it consumes backprop's output). Getting the object of the update right — "the weights, computed by backprop, applied by the optimizer" — is the whole signal.

**Common confusions**

- "Training updates the data / the embeddings of the input text." No — it updates the model's weight matrices; the embedding *matrix* is a weight, but the input tokens and their activations are transient.
- "Backprop and gradient descent are the same thing." Backprop *computes* gradients; gradient descent/Adam *uses* them to update weights. Two separate steps.
- "Adam replaces backpropagation." Adam is the update rule; it still needs the gradients that backprop produces.
- "Bigger learning rate = faster learning, always." Too big diverges; warmup exists precisely because a large lr on step 1 blows up.
- "Mixed precision means the weights are int8." bf16 is floating point; quantization to int8/int4 is a separate inference-time technique.

**What follows from this topic**

Once you know one training step and its cost, **Scaling Laws & Compute** quantifies how many such steps (tokens) and parameters you need, via FLOPs ~ 6 * N * D and Chinchilla's ~20 tokens/param. And once a base model is trained by this loop, **Post-Training: SFT & Instruction Tuning** reuses the exact same backprop-and-AdamW machinery on curated demonstration data to turn a text-completer into an assistant. This topic is the engine; those two are the fuel budget and the finishing school.

### Q1. What actually gets updated during training — the weights, the activations, or the data?

The **weights**. Specifically the learnable parameter matrices: the token **embedding matrix** `(vocab, d_model)`, the attention projections **W_q, W_k, W_v, W_o** per layer, the **FFN weights** W_1/W_2 (and biases, norms' gain/bias), and the **unembedding / LM head** (often tied to the embedding). Backprop computes a gradient dL/dW for each of these, and the optimizer nudges them.

What does **not** get updated:

- **Activations** — the intermediate vectors like the `(batch, seq, d_model)` hidden states are recomputed fresh every forward pass. They are used *during* backprop (cached to compute gradients) but they are outputs, not parameters.
- **The training data** — the text is a fixed input and its own label (self-supervised). It never changes.
- **The token IDs / inputs** — fixed.

```text
per step:  inputs (fixed) --forward--> activations (transient) --> loss
                                                      |
weights (UPDATED) <--optimizer-- gradients <--backward-- loss
```

Common trap: "it updates the embeddings of my prompt". The embedding *matrix* is a weight and is trained; but at inference your prompt just looks up rows — nothing is learned from it.

### Q2. Walk through one full training step end to end.

Four phases, in order:

```text
1. FORWARD:   run the batch of token IDs through the network,
              producing logits (batch, seq, vocab).
2. LOSS:      cross-entropy of logits vs the true next tokens,
              averaged: L = mean over positions of -log p_model(true next).
3. BACKWARD:  backprop computes dL/dW for every weight W
              (one reverse sweep, chain rule, reusing cached activations).
4. UPDATE:    optimizer (AdamW) turns gradients into a step:
              W <- W - lr * adam_step(dL/dW).
              then zero the gradients for the next step.
```

```python
for batch in data:               # batch = (input_ids, targets)
    logits = model(batch.input_ids)          # forward
    loss = cross_entropy(logits, batch.targets)
    loss.backward()                          # backprop fills w.grad
    optimizer.step()                         # AdamW update of weights
    optimizer.zero_grad()                    # clear grads for next step
```

That loop, run for hundreds of thousands of steps over trillions of tokens, *is* training. Everything else (schedules, accumulation, bf16, parallelism) is machinery to make this loop fast, stable, and big.

### Q3. Explain backpropagation. What is it actually computing?

Backprop is **reverse-mode automatic differentiation**: it computes the gradient of the scalar loss with respect to every weight, dL/dW, by applying the **chain rule** from the loss backward through the network.

The network is a composition of functions L = loss(f_N(...f_2(f_1(x)))). The chain rule says the derivative of a composition is the product of local derivatives. Backprop evaluates that product efficiently:

```text
forward:   x -> h1 -> h2 -> ... -> logits -> L   (cache each h_i)
backward:  start with dL/dL = 1, then repeatedly
           dL/dh_{i-1} = dL/dh_i * (dh_i/dh_{i-1})   <- local Jacobian
           and at each weight:  dL/dW_i = dL/dh_i * (dh_i/dW_i)
```

Key properties:

- **One backward pass** gets the gradient for *all* weights at once — cost comparable to one forward pass. This is why training billions of parameters is even feasible; you don't perturb each weight separately.
- It **reuses the cached activations** from the forward pass (that's why training needs much more memory than inference).
- It **does not send data backward** through the model in any meaningful sense; it propagates *derivatives* (error signals) backward.

The output of backprop is just numbers: one gradient per weight. What you *do* with them is gradient descent / Adam.

### Q4. What is gradient descent, and how does the gradient tell us which way to move?

The gradient dL/dW points in the direction of **steepest increase** of the loss. To *reduce* loss, step in the opposite direction:

```text
W_new = W_old - lr * dL/dW
```

`lr` (learning rate) scales the step. Intuition: you are on a hilly loss surface in billion-dimensional weight space; the gradient is the local uphill direction; you take a small downhill step. Repeat.

- Compute the true gradient over the *entire* dataset = **batch gradient descent** (too expensive).
- Compute it on one random **minibatch** = **stochastic gradient descent (SGD)**. The minibatch gradient is a noisy but unbiased estimate of the true gradient; the noise is actually helpful for escaping bad regions.

```python
grad = compute_gradient(loss, W)   # from backprop
W = W - lr * grad                  # the entire idea, one line
```

Everything fancier (momentum, Adam, schedules) is about choosing the step *direction and size* more cleverly than this raw rule, because the transformer loss surface is highly non-uniform across parameters.

### Q5. Why AdamW instead of plain SGD?

Plain SGD uses one global learning rate and the raw gradient. Transformer training is **ill-conditioned** — different parameters have gradients of wildly different scales, and the loss surface is sharp in some directions and flat in others. SGD either diverges (lr too big for the sharp directions) or crawls (lr too small for the flat ones).

**Adam** fixes this by keeping, per parameter, a running estimate of:

- **m** — the mean of recent gradients (momentum, smooths noise and accelerates consistent directions),
- **v** — the mean of recent *squared* gradients (the scale of that parameter's gradients).

The update normalises each parameter's step by its own gradient magnitude:

```text
m = b1*m + (1-b1)*g              # first moment  (mean)
v = b2*v + (1-b2)*g*g            # second moment (variance)
W = W - lr * m_hat / (sqrt(v_hat) + eps)
```

So every parameter effectively gets an **adaptive, well-scaled step** — big steps where gradients are small/consistent, small steps where they're large/noisy. That is why Adam-family optimizers dominate LLM training.

**AdamW** is the variant that applies **weight decay** correctly — decoupled from the gradient-based step (`W = W - lr*update - lr*wd*W`) rather than folded into the gradient as classic Adam did. This gives cleaner regularisation and is the standard for LLMs. Cost: Adam stores m and v per weight, so the optimizer state is ~2x the model size in memory (a real training-cost factor).

### Q6. Why do we warm up the learning rate and then decay it?

The learning-rate **schedule** is: start near 0, ramp up (warmup) for the first few thousand steps, then decay (cosine or linear) toward ~0 over the rest of training.

```text
lr
 |        ___________
 |       /           \____
 |      /                 \____
 |     /                       \___
 |____/___________________________\___ steps
     warmup        decay
```

- **Warmup**: at initialisation the weights are random and Adam's running variance estimate v is unreliable (built from almost no history). A full-size lr on step 1 produces huge, badly-scaled updates that can destabilise or diverge training — especially with the large batches and bf16 used in LLMs. Ramping up lets Adam's statistics settle and the model find a stable region first.
- **Decay**: early on you want big steps to make fast progress; late in training you want small steps to settle into a good minimum without bouncing around it. Cosine decay to a small floor is the common choice.

Net effect: warmup buys stability at the start, decay buys precision at the end. The peak lr and the schedule shape are among the most tuned knobs in a training run.

### Q7. What is a batch, and why do we want large batches? How do we get them without enough memory?

A **batch** is the set of sequences/tokens processed together to compute one gradient before one optimizer update. A larger batch gives a **less noisy** gradient estimate (averaging over more examples), which allows a higher stable learning rate and better hardware utilisation. LLM pretraining uses *huge* effective batches (millions of tokens per step).

The problem: a big batch's activations don't fit in one GPU's memory. Two standard fixes, usually combined:

- **Gradient accumulation** — process several small **micro-batches** sequentially, *sum* their gradients, and only call the optimizer once after N of them. This simulates a batch N times larger with the memory of one micro-batch.

```python
optimizer.zero_grad()
for micro in range(N):                # accumulate
    loss = cross_entropy(model(x[micro]), y[micro]) / N
    loss.backward()                   # grads ADD up across micro-batches
optimizer.step()                      # one update for the big effective batch
```

- **Data parallelism** — replicate the model across many GPUs, each processes a different slice of the batch, then **all-reduce** (average) the gradients across GPUs before the step. This multiplies the effective batch by the number of GPUs.

Effective batch size = micro_batch * accumulation_steps * num_data_parallel_gpus.

### Q8. What is mixed precision (bf16) training and why use it?

Mixed precision does the heavy compute (matmuls) in a **16-bit** floating-point format instead of 32-bit, cutting memory roughly in half and roughly doubling throughput on modern GPUs (which have fast 16-bit matrix units).

The preferred format for LLMs is **bf16 (brain float16)**:

```text
             exponent bits   mantissa bits   dynamic range   precision
fp32              8               23           huge            high
fp16              5               10           small           medium
bf16              8                7           huge (=fp32)    low
```

bf16 keeps fp32's **exponent range** (so it rarely overflows/underflows) at the cost of mantissa precision. That range is what matters for training stability, which is why bf16 largely replaced fp16 (fp16 needs fiddly "loss scaling" to avoid gradient underflow; bf16 usually doesn't).

"Mixed" because some parts stay in fp32 for numerical safety — typically a **master copy of the weights** and the optimizer state (m, v), and often the loss/softmax accumulation. So: compute forward/backward matmuls in bf16, accumulate and update in fp32.

Note this is a *training* precision choice, distinct from **quantization** (int8/int4), which is an *inference* technique to shrink and speed up a finished model.

### Q9. Why does training use so much more memory than inference?

Because backprop needs the **cached activations** from the forward pass to compute gradients, plus **optimizer state** and **gradients**, none of which inference needs.

For a model with N parameters, the rough memory ledger during training (bf16 compute, fp32 master/Adam):

```text
weights (bf16)             ~2N bytes
gradients                  ~2N bytes
Adam state m + v (fp32)    ~8N bytes
fp32 master weights        ~4N bytes
------------------------------------
optimizer+weights          ~16N bytes   (before any activations)
plus: activations for every layer, scaling with batch*seq*depth
```

So a model whose weights are, say, 14 GB can need well over 100 GB just for state, before activations. Inference by contrast needs only the weights plus a KV cache. This is why:

- Training needs many more/bigger GPUs than serving the same model.
- **Activation checkpointing** (recompute activations in the backward pass instead of storing them) is a common memory-for-compute trade.
- Optimizer state (the 8N for Adam's m and v) is a major reason people sometimes use memory-lighter optimizers or shard state across GPUs (ZeRO).

### Q10. What is the loss function for pretraining, and how does it connect to backprop?

The loss is **average cross-entropy on next-token prediction**. For each position t, the model outputs a probability distribution p_model over the vocab; the target is the actual next token. The per-token loss is `-log(p_model(true next token))`, averaged over all positions:

```text
L = (1/T) * sum over t of  -log( p_model(token_{t+1} | tokens_1..t) )
where p_model = softmax(logits_t)
```

Intuition: loss is low when the model put high probability on the token that actually came next, high when it was surprised. This is exactly the negative log-likelihood; minimising it maximises the likelihood of the training text.

Connection to backprop: this scalar L is the *starting point* of the backward pass. Backprop begins with dL/dL = 1 and propagates derivatives backward, and the very first useful gradient — of cross-entropy through softmax — has a famously clean form:

```text
dL/dlogit_i = p_i - y_i     # predicted prob minus one-hot target
```

That tidy `(prediction - target)` signal at the logits is what flows back through the LM head, the blocks, and into every weight. So the loss defines *what* we optimise; backprop turns it into per-weight gradients; AdamW applies them.

### Q11. What is teacher forcing, and why can pretraining compute the loss on a whole sequence in parallel?

**Teacher forcing** means that during training you feed the model the **ground-truth** previous tokens as context, not its own predictions. At every position the model predicts the next token given the *real* prefix, regardless of what it would have generated.

Because of teacher forcing plus **causal masking**, a single forward pass over a length-T sequence computes the prediction (and hence the loss) at *all T positions at once*, in parallel:

```text
input:   the  cat  sat  on
targets: cat  sat  on   the      # each position predicts its right neighbour
```

The causal mask guarantees position t only sees tokens 1..t, so position 3's prediction is a legitimate "predict token 4 from the cat sat" even though the whole sequence is in the batch. This parallelism over positions is a huge efficiency win of transformers over RNNs (which must step sequentially) and is why one forward pass yields T training signals.

Contrast with **generation**, which is autoregressive and *not* parallel — the model must feed its own output back in one token at a time. The mismatch between teacher-forced training and self-generated inference is called **exposure bias**.

### Q12. If backprop computes gradients for all weights at once, why is training still so expensive?

Because you must run the forward-plus-backward loop an astronomical number of times, and each iteration touches every parameter.

The cost drivers:

- **Tokens**: pretraining processes *trillions* of tokens; each token's forward+backward costs roughly `6 * N` FLOPs (N = parameter count) — forward ~2N, backward ~4N (backward is about twice the forward, since it computes gradients w.r.t. both inputs and weights).

```text
total training FLOPs ~ 6 * N * D      (N params, D tokens)
```

- **Parameters**: every one of the billions of weights gets a gradient and an update every step, and Adam stores two extra numbers per weight.
- **Memory bandwidth**: moving weights, activations, and gradients around dominates as much as raw math.
- **Distribution overhead**: gradients must be all-reduced across hundreds/thousands of GPUs each step.

So backprop being efficient *per step* (one backward pass, not billions of perturbations) is exactly what makes training merely very-expensive rather than impossible — but D (tokens) and N (params) are both huge, and the 6ND product is what turns into weeks of thousands of GPUs. The next topic, **Scaling Laws & Compute**, is entirely about budgeting this 6ND.

### Q13. What is gradient clipping and why is it needed?

**Gradient clipping** caps the size of the gradient before the optimizer step, to prevent a single unusually large gradient (a "spike") from producing a giant, destabilising weight update.

The common form is **global norm clipping**: compute the norm of the whole gradient vector; if it exceeds a threshold, scale the entire gradient down to that threshold.

```text
g_norm = sqrt(sum over all weights of grad^2)
if g_norm > max_norm:
    grad = grad * (max_norm / g_norm)   # rescale, preserve direction
```

Why LLMs need it: on rare bad batches (or in the unstable early steps) gradients can momentarily blow up; without clipping, one such step can send the loss to NaN and wreck a run that's been going for days. Clipping bounds the worst case while preserving the gradient's *direction*. It pairs naturally with warmup — both are about surviving the numerically fragile parts of training. A sudden loss spike in a training curve is often a sign clipping (or the lr) needs attention.

### Q14. Spot the misconception: "Adam replaces backpropagation, so you don't need the chain rule anymore."

False — they do different jobs and both are required.

- **Backpropagation** is how you *get* the gradients: it applies the chain rule to compute dL/dW for every weight. Without it you have no gradients at all.
- **Adam / AdamW** is an *update rule*: given the gradients, it decides how to turn them into a weight change (using per-parameter running mean m and variance v).

The pipeline is strictly sequential and Adam sits *downstream* of backprop:

```text
loss --backprop (chain rule)--> gradients --AdamW--> weight update
```

If you removed backprop, Adam would have nothing to consume. If you removed Adam, backprop's gradients would still be valid — you'd just fall back to plain SGD (`W -= lr*grad`) to apply them. So Adam is an alternative to *SGD* (the update step), not to *backprop* (the gradient computation). Anyone conflating the two is missing the fundamental split: **compute the gradient, then apply the gradient.**

### Q15. What is an optimizer's state, and why does it roughly triple training memory versus the weights alone?

The **optimizer state** is the extra per-parameter bookkeeping the optimizer maintains between steps. For AdamW that's two numbers per weight:

- **m** — the running mean of gradients (first moment),
- **v** — the running mean of squared gradients (second moment).

Both are typically stored in fp32 for numerical stability, and mixed-precision training also keeps an fp32 **master copy** of the weights. So per parameter you are holding, roughly:

```text
bf16 weight          2 bytes
bf16 gradient        2 bytes
fp32 master weight   4 bytes
fp32 Adam m          4 bytes
fp32 Adam v          4 bytes
--------------------------------
total               16 bytes per parameter
```

versus just 2 bytes/param to *serve* the model. That ~8x blow-up (and the fact that m, v, and master weights alone are 12 of those 16 bytes) is why:

- training a model needs vastly more GPU memory than inference,
- optimizer-state **sharding** (e.g. ZeRO stage 1) — splitting m and v across data-parallel GPUs so no single GPU holds all of it — is a standard large-scale trick.

The takeaway for an interview: "the weights are only a fraction of training memory; Adam's m and v plus the fp32 master copy dominate."

### Q16. How is the model initialised before any training, and why does initialisation matter?

Before step 1 the weights are set to small **random** values (not zeros). Zeros would be fatal: every neuron in a layer would compute the identical output and receive the identical gradient, so they'd stay identical forever — you need to **break symmetry**, which randomness does.

The *scale* of that randomness is carefully chosen so that signals and gradients neither explode nor vanish as they pass through many layers. Schemes like **Xavier/Glorot** and **Kaiming/He** set the variance based on layer width (`~1/d`) to keep activation magnitudes stable across depth. Transformers often add residual-aware scaling (e.g. shrinking certain projections by `1/sqrt(2N)` for N layers) so the residual stream doesn't grow uncontrollably as blocks stack.

Why it matters:

- Good init lets a very deep network train from the first step; bad init produces immediate NaNs or a loss that won't move.
- It interacts with the lr warmup (Q6) and clipping (Q13): all three are about surviving the fragile early phase.

Only the *embeddings and other weights* start random; the architecture (shapes, number of layers) is fixed by design and does not change during training — training only moves the numbers inside those fixed matrices.

## Scaling Laws & Compute

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

This topic explains why making language models bigger keeps making them better, and how to budget the resources to do it. It covers the **scaling laws** (Kaplan et al.: loss falls as a smooth power law in **parameters N**, **data D**, and **compute C**), the **Chinchilla** correction (for a fixed compute budget the *optimal* split is ~**20 training tokens per parameter**, and most early models were badly **under-trained** — too many parameters, too little data), the **compute accounting** identity **FLOPs ~ 6 * N * D** (worked with a concrete example), **emergent abilities** at scale and the debate over whether they are real capabilities or a metric artifact, why scaling has kept working, **compute budget as the true constraint**, and the distinction between **training compute** (one-time, enormous) and **inference compute** (per-request, recurring). The 15 questions here turn "bigger is better" into something you can quantify on a whiteboard. It builds directly on the previous topic — one training step costs ~6N FLOPs per token, and scaling laws budget how many tokens and parameters to buy.

**Mental model**

Picture three dials — model size N, dataset size D, and compute C — and a single output, the test loss. Scaling laws say the loss traces a smooth, predictable **power law** as you turn any dial: doubling compute reduces loss by a fixed fraction, straight-line on a log-log plot, across many orders of magnitude. That predictability is the point — you can train small models, fit the curve, and *extrapolate* the loss of a model 100x bigger before spending the money. But N and D aren't independent: they're chained by compute, C ~ 6*N*D. So for a fixed budget C you must *split* it — bigger model or more data? Chinchilla answered: scale them *together*, roughly 20 tokens per parameter. GPT-3-era models spent almost everything on N and starved D, leaving performance on the table. The mental image: compute is a fixed pot of money; scaling laws are the exchange rate; Chinchilla is the optimal way to spend it.

**Key terms**

- **Scaling law** — an empirical power-law relationship: test loss decreases predictably as a function of N, D, or C.
- **N** — number of (non-embedding) model parameters.
- **D** — number of training tokens.
- **C** — total training compute, measured in FLOPs.
- **Power law** — loss ~ (quantity)^(-a); a straight line on log-log axes; diminishing but non-vanishing returns.
- **Kaplan scaling laws** — the 2020 OpenAI result that loss scales smoothly in N, D, C.
- **Chinchilla** — the 2022 DeepMind correction: compute-optimal training uses N and D in roughly equal measure, ~20 tokens/param.
- **Compute-optimal** — the (N, D) split that minimises loss for a *fixed* compute budget C.
- **Under-trained** — a model given too few tokens for its size (too big for its data); early LLMs were this.
- **FLOPs ~ 6ND** — the compute-accounting identity: total training FLOPs ~ 6 * params * tokens.
- **Emergent ability** — a capability that appears (or appears to appear) sharply only above some scale.
- **Irreducible loss** — the floor of the power law; the entropy of language itself, which no model can beat.

**Why interviewers ask this**

Scaling is the single biggest reason modern LLMs exist, so an interviewer wants to know you understand *why* the field poured billions into scale rather than cleverness — and that you can reason about it numerically. A junior answer says "bigger models are better". A senior answer states the power-law form, can write and *use* FLOPs ~ 6ND to estimate a training run's cost, knows the Chinchilla result and can explain *why* GPT-3 was under-trained (and what that implies: for a fixed budget, buy more data not just more params), and can discuss emergent abilities with appropriate skepticism (real capability jump vs. a discontinuous *metric* masking smooth underlying improvement). It also reveals whether you think about compute as the actual constraint — that model design is really budget allocation. This topic rewards someone who can do back-of-envelope math and hold a nuanced view of a debated result.

**Common confusions**

- "Scaling laws promise loss goes to zero." No — power laws have an **irreducible** floor (the entropy of text); returns diminish and you approach, never reach, zero.
- "Bigger is always better." Only if you feed it enough data; an over-sized, under-trained model is *worse* than a right-sized one at the same compute (the Chinchilla point).
- "FLOPs ~ 6ND counts inference too." It's the *training* cost; inference is a separate, recurring cost of ~2N FLOPs per generated token.
- "Emergent abilities prove models suddenly gain understanding." Contested — many 'emergences' vanish under smoother metrics, suggesting a measurement artifact rather than a phase change.
- "More parameters = more compute, data doesn't matter." Compute is N *times* D; doubling data doubles compute just as doubling params does.

**What follows from this topic**

Scaling laws explain why you'd build a giant **base model** at all — and the next topic, **Post-Training: SFT & Instruction Tuning**, explains why that giant base model still isn't useful out of the box and how cheap fine-tuning turns raw scale into a helpful assistant. Scaling also motivates much of the efficiency work (MoE, quantization, FlashAttention): once you've paid the 6ND to train, **inference** compute becomes the recurring bill, and the whole game shifts to serving that scale affordably. Scale buys capability; post-training and efficiency make it usable and cheap.

### Q1. What are the scaling laws, and what is their basic shape?

Scaling laws are **empirical power-law relationships** showing that a language model's test loss falls smoothly and predictably as you increase any of three quantities: parameters **N**, training tokens **D**, or compute **C**. Kaplan et al. (2020) found each follows the form:

```text
loss(N) ~ (N_c / N)^a          # more params -> lower loss
loss(D) ~ (D_c / D)^b          # more data   -> lower loss
loss(C) ~ (C_c / C)^g          # more compute-> lower loss
```

On **log-log axes** these are straight lines — that's the signature of a power law. The exponents (a, b, g) are small positive numbers, so returns diminish (each doubling gives a fixed *fractional* loss reduction, not a fixed absolute one) but never stop.

Why this is a big deal: it makes model performance **predictable and extrapolable**. Train a handful of small models, fit the line, and you can forecast the loss of a model 100x larger *before* building it — which is exactly how labs justify nine-figure training runs. The laws held across many orders of magnitude, which is why "just scale it" became a viable strategy rather than a gamble.

The catch (Q4, Q5): N and D are coupled through compute, so you can't push one arbitrarily; and there's an **irreducible loss** floor you asymptote toward but never cross.

### Q2. Derive and explain the FLOPs ~ 6 * N * D compute rule.

This identity estimates the total **training compute** (in floating-point operations) for a model with **N** parameters trained on **D** tokens:

```text
C ~ 6 * N * D
```

Where the 6 comes from — count the work each parameter does per token:

- **Forward pass**: each parameter participates in roughly one multiply and one add per token = **2 FLOPs/param/token**.
- **Backward pass**: computing gradients costs about twice the forward (you propagate error to both the inputs and the weights) = **4 FLOPs/param/token**.
- **Total**: 2 + 4 = **6 FLOPs per parameter per token**.

Multiply by N parameters and D tokens:

```text
FLOPs ~ (6 FLOPs / param / token) * N params * D tokens = 6 * N * D
```

It's an approximation — it ignores attention's sequence-length term, layernorms, activations — but it's dominated by the big matmuls and is astonishingly accurate for real runs. It's the workhorse for budgeting: given a compute budget C, you immediately get the constraint N * D ~ C / 6, which is exactly what Chinchilla optimises over.

### Q3. Work a concrete example: how many FLOPs to train a 70B model on 1.4T tokens?

Use FLOPs ~ 6 * N * D directly.

```text
N = 70e9   parameters
D = 1.4e12 tokens
C ~ 6 * N * D
  = 6 * 70e9 * 1.4e12
  = 6 * 9.8e22
  ~ 5.9e23 FLOPs
```

So about **6e23 FLOPs** — roughly 0.6 zettaFLOPs. Note 70B / 1.4T is ~20 tokens/param, i.e. this is a **Chinchilla-optimal** run.

Sanity-check against wall-clock. Say you have 1000 GPUs each delivering 400 teraFLOP/s sustained (400e12 FLOP/s) after real-world utilisation:

```text
aggregate = 1000 * 400e12 = 4e17 FLOP/s
time      = C / aggregate = 5.9e23 / 4e17 ~ 1.5e6 seconds
          ~ 17 days
```

That's the shape of a frontier pretraining run: ~10^23-10^24 FLOPs, thousands of GPUs, weeks of wall-clock. The value of the 6ND rule is exactly this: a one-line estimate turns "train a 70B model" into a concrete GPU-weeks budget you can plan and cost.

### Q4. What is the Chinchilla result, and why were early models under-trained?

Kaplan's original laws suggested that, given more compute, you should spend most of it making the model **bigger**. Following that, GPT-3 (175B) and its contemporaries were huge but trained on relatively few tokens (~300B).

DeepMind's **Chinchilla** (2022) re-ran the analysis holding **compute fixed** and asking: for a budget C, what split of N and D minimises loss? The answer: **scale N and D together, roughly equally** — about **20 training tokens per parameter** at the compute-optimal point.

```text
compute-optimal:  D ~ 20 * N       (very roughly)
```

Implication — early models were **under-trained**: too many parameters for too little data. Chinchilla demonstrated this by training a **70B** model (Chinchilla) on **1.4T tokens** (~20 tokens/param) using the *same* compute as the **280B** Gopher (trained on far fewer tokens) — and the *smaller, more-data* model **won** across benchmarks.

```text
              params    tokens    tokens/param   compute   result
Gopher        280B      300B      ~1             X         worse
Chinchilla     70B      1.4T      ~20            X         better
```

The lesson reshaped the field: for a fixed budget, don't just inflate N — buy proportionally more data. It also explains the later obsession with data quality and quantity, and why "small" models trained on trillions of tokens now outperform older giants.

### Q5. If a smaller model can beat a bigger one, when would you deliberately over-train (go past 20 tokens/param)?

Chinchilla optimises **training** compute for a target loss. But that's the wrong objective if you're going to **serve** the model to millions of users, because inference cost scales with N, not D.

Key asymmetry:

- **Training** is a one-time cost ~ 6ND.
- **Inference** is a recurring cost ~ 2N per generated token, paid on *every* request forever.

So if you'll do a lot of inference, you want a **smaller N** (cheaper, faster to serve) even if that means spending *more* training compute per parameter — i.e. deliberately going **past** 20 tokens/param ("over-training" a small model on far more data). You pay more up front to save on every future request.

```text
Chinchilla-optimal:  minimise TRAINING cost for a given quality.
Inference-optimal:   accept higher training cost for a SMALLER model,
                     to minimise LIFETIME serving cost.
```

This is exactly why modern "small" open models (e.g. 7B-8B) are trained on 10-15T tokens — hundreds of tokens per parameter, far beyond Chinchilla — to squeeze maximum quality into a cheap-to-serve size. So the honest answer to "isn't Chinchilla the rule?" is: it's the *training-compute-optimal* rule; product economics with heavy inference push you the other way.

### Q6. Do scaling laws mean loss eventually reaches zero? What is irreducible loss?

No. Power laws have a **floor**. The Chinchilla loss model makes this explicit:

```text
loss(N, D) = E + A / N^a + B / D^b
             ^        ^          ^
       irreducible  finite-N  finite-D
         entropy      term       term
```

- The A/N^a and B/D^b terms shrink as you scale N and D — that's the improvement.
- **E** is the **irreducible loss**: the term that *remains even with infinite parameters and infinite data*. It represents the intrinsic **entropy of language** — the genuine unpredictability of text (you cannot predict a random name or a coin flip described in text no matter how good your model is).

So scaling gives **diminishing returns approaching E**, never below it. On a log-log plot the line eventually bends toward this asymptote. Practically:

- Each order of magnitude of compute buys a smaller and smaller absolute loss reduction.
- You never reach zero loss / perfect prediction, because language has irreducible randomness.

This reframes "will scaling solve everything?" — scaling reliably closes the *reducible* gap, but there's a hard floor set by the data distribution itself. The interesting research question is how much *useful capability* still lives in that shrinking gap between current loss and E.

### Q7. What are emergent abilities, and why are they debated?

**Emergent abilities** are capabilities that appear to be **absent in small models and present in large ones**, seemingly switching on sharply above some scale rather than improving smoothly — e.g. multi-step arithmetic, or following certain complex instructions, jumping from near-zero to strong once a model crosses some size.

```text
accuracy
 |                     ____
 |                    /
 |                   /
 |________________ _/            <- looks like a sudden "emergence"
 +---------------------------- model scale
```

The debate: are these **real phase transitions** in capability, or a **metric artifact**?

- The skeptical view (Schaeffer et al.) argues many "emergences" are created by **discontinuous or all-or-nothing metrics**. Exact-match accuracy on a multi-step task is 0 until *every* step is right, so it stays flat then jumps — even though the *per-step* probability the model assigns is improving **smoothly** all along. Switch to a smooth metric (per-token likelihood, partial credit) and the sharp jump often disappears into a gentle curve.
- The other view holds that *some* emergences are genuine and that unpredictable capability jumps are a real and important (and safety-relevant) feature of scale.

Why it matters for an interview: it tests nuance. The right stance is neither "scale magically grants understanding" nor total dismissal — it's "many apparent emergences are measurement effects on top of smooth underlying improvement, but the phenomenon and its unpredictability are real research concerns." Being able to explain the *metric* mechanism is the senior signal.

### Q8. Why has scaling kept working when other AI approaches plateaued?

A few reinforcing reasons, and it's fair to say it's partly still an open question:

- **A simple, general objective**: next-token prediction is a task with effectively unlimited training signal — all of human text — and it implicitly requires modeling grammar, facts, reasoning, and style to do well. There's no labeling bottleneck, so data scales with the internet.
- **An architecture that scales cleanly**: the transformer is highly **parallelisable** (no sequential recurrence to train) and its blocks are identical and stackable, so it maps efficiently onto GPU/TPU clusters. Adding parameters and depth "just works" without architectural redesign.
- **Predictability**: because loss follows smooth power laws (Q1), each scale-up is a *forecastable* bet, not a gamble — so labs kept investing, and the returns kept materialising.
- **Co-scaling of data and compute**: hardware (FLOPs), data availability, and Chinchilla-informed recipes improved together, so no single factor bottlenecked for long.

Contrast with earlier eras: hand-engineered features, task-specific architectures, and supervised learning all hit **data-labeling** and **generality** ceilings. Self-supervised next-token prediction on a scalable architecture removed those ceilings.

Caveats worth stating: returns are power-law (diminishing), high-quality **data** is becoming a real constraint (you can run out of good text), and it's genuinely uncertain how far pure scaling goes before something else (data quality, new objectives, reasoning training) becomes the dominant lever.

### Q9. What are the three quantities in scaling, and how does compute couple them?

The three quantities are:

- **N** — model parameters (size).
- **D** — training tokens (data).
- **C** — training compute (FLOPs).

They are **not independent** — compute chains N and D together:

```text
C ~ 6 * N * D
```

This coupling is the crux. You do not get to freely maximise both N and D; you have a **compute budget** C, and 6ND ~ C constrains you to a curve of (N, D) trade-offs. Turning up N forces D down and vice versa for a fixed budget.

```text
fixed C  =>  N * D ~ C / 6  =>  a hyperbola of choices:
   big N, small D   (GPT-3 style, under-trained)
   balanced N, D    (Chinchilla-optimal, ~20 tokens/param)
   small N, big D    (inference-optimal over-training)
```

So the real design question is never "how big?" alone — it's "given my compute budget C, **where on the N-vs-D trade-off curve** do I sit?" Kaplan told us loss falls with each dial; Chinchilla told us the *optimal point on the curve* (~20 tokens/param). Understanding that C is the binding constraint and N, D are how you spend it is the whole mental model of this topic.

### Q10. Contrast training compute vs inference compute.

They are fundamentally different bills:

```text
                 TRAINING                     INFERENCE
cost formula     ~ 6 * N * D  FLOPs (total)   ~ 2 * N  FLOPs / generated token
when paid        once, up front               every request, forever
scales with      params AND tokens            params (and tokens generated)
magnitude        huge one-time (10^23-10^24)  tiny per-call, but astronomical in aggregate
who cares        the lab building the model    everyone serving/using it
optimised by     Chinchilla (N,D split)        smaller N, quantization, MoE, KV cache
```

Key points:

- **Training** is dominated by the 6ND product and is a **capital expense** — thousands of GPUs for weeks, then done.
- **Inference** is ~**2N FLOPs per token** (just the forward pass, no backward, so ~2 not ~6). Per request it's cheap, but multiplied by billions of requests it becomes the **dominant lifetime cost** of a deployed model — an **operating expense**.

This asymmetry drives real decisions: it's why labs **over-train small models** past Chinchilla (Q5) to cut inference cost, and why nearly all the efficiency machinery (KV cache, GQA/MQA, quantization, MoE, FlashAttention) targets **inference**. In short: training cost is a function of how you *build* the model; inference cost is a function of how big it *is* and how much you *use* it.

### Q11. Estimate the inference cost per token, and why is it ~2N not ~6N?

Inference runs only the **forward pass** — you generate a token, you don't compute gradients or update weights. From the 6ND breakdown (Q2), the forward pass is the "2" part:

```text
forward  = 2 FLOPs / param / token
backward = 4 FLOPs / param / token   <- NOT done at inference
--------------------------------------
inference cost ~ 2 * N FLOPs per generated token
```

So a 70B model costs roughly:

```text
2 * 70e9 = 1.4e11 FLOPs per generated token (~140 GFLOPs/token)
```

Multiply by tokens generated to get per-request cost. Two practical wrinkles:

- **Prefill vs decode**: processing the prompt (prefill) does ~2N per prompt token but in parallel; generating (decode) does ~2N per new token one at a time and is usually **memory-bandwidth-bound**, not FLOP-bound — you spend more time *moving* the N weights than multiplying.
- Because decode reloads all N weights for each token, the real bottleneck is often memory bandwidth, which is exactly why **KV caching**, **quantization** (fewer bytes per weight), and **MoE** (only a subset of params active per token) matter so much.

The interview point: inference is ~2N/token (forward only), and at scale that per-token cost, times billions of requests, is what dominates the economics of a deployed LLM.

### Q12. What is the compute-optimal frontier, and how do you use it to plan a run?

The **compute-optimal frontier** is the set of best-achievable losses for each compute budget C — the lower envelope you get by choosing the *ideal* (N, D) split at every budget. Chinchilla's contribution was to map it: at the optimum, N and D grow together (~20 tokens/param), and the optimal N and D each scale as roughly a **power of the compute budget** (approximately N ~ C^0.5 and D ~ C^0.5).

How you use it to plan:

```text
1. Fix your compute budget C (GPU-hours you can afford -> FLOPs).
2. Chinchilla-optimal split:  N* ~ sqrt(C / 6 / 20),  D* ~ 20 * N*.
   (equivalently N* * D* ~ C/6 with D* ~ 20 N*)
3. That gives target model size and token count.
4. Adjust for goal: heavy inference? shrink N, raise D past 20x (Q5).
```

The value is that it turns an abstract budget into concrete design targets *before* you spend a cent, and it lets you **extrapolate** — fit the frontier from small runs, read off the loss you'll get at 100x the compute. It's the practical payoff of scaling laws: model design becomes **budget allocation along a known curve** rather than guesswork. Note the frontier is *training-optimal*; product economics (inference) can justify sitting off it deliberately.

### Q13. Is data or compute the real bottleneck going forward?

Historically **compute** was the binding constraint — the whole field organised around FLOPs budgets, and scaling laws are written with C as the master variable. But Chinchilla shifted attention: to train compute-optimally you need ~20 tokens per parameter, and pushing to inference-optimal small models needs *hundreds* of tokens per parameter. That makes **high-quality data** an increasingly real constraint:

- Frontier models already train on **10-15T tokens**, approaching estimates of the total stock of high-quality public text. This is the "**running out of data**" concern — the internet is large but finite, and quality matters more than raw quantity.
- Responses include: **data quality/curation** and filtering, **multiple epochs** (repeating data, with diminishing and eventually negative returns), **multimodal** data (images, audio, video), **code** as a high-value source, and **synthetic data** generated by existing models (promising but with risks of compounding errors / model collapse).

So the honest interview answer: compute is still expensive and the classical constraint, but at the frontier the **data** term in the Chinchilla loss (B/D^b) is getting hard to keep shrinking because good tokens are finite — which is why data curation and synthetic data have become central research areas. Both constraints are live; the *binding* one is shifting from compute toward data at the very largest scales.

### Q14. Spot the misconception: "GPT-3 proved bigger is always better, so we should just keep adding parameters."

This conflates two things and is exactly what **Chinchilla refuted**.

GPT-3 (175B) was impressive, but it was **under-trained** — it had far more parameters than its ~300B training tokens could properly teach (~1.7 tokens/param, versus the ~20 that's compute-optimal). Adding parameters *without proportionally adding data* wastes compute: those extra weights never get enough signal to be worth their cost.

The corrected view:

```text
WRONG:  more params -> better (hold data fixed)
RIGHT:  for a fixed compute budget, scale params AND data TOGETHER (~20:1);
        an over-sized, data-starved model is WORSE than a right-sized one
        at the same compute.
```

Chinchilla's 70B model beating the 280B Gopher **at equal compute** is the direct counterexample: the *smaller* model, given more data, won. So "bigger is always better" is false as stated — it's only true if you also feed the bigger model proportionally more tokens, which costs compute you might have spent better elsewhere. The precise statement is: **for a fixed compute budget, there is an optimal size; overshooting it hurts.** A candidate who just says "scale the params" is repeating the pre-Chinchilla mistake.

### Q15. How do scaling laws let you predict a large model's performance from small experiments?

Because the loss follows a **power law** in compute, it's a **straight line on log-log axes** — and a straight line is trivially **extrapolable**. The workflow:

```text
1. Train a LADDER of small models at increasing compute
   (e.g. C = 1e18, 1e19, 1e20 FLOPs), each Chinchilla-optimal.
2. Measure each one's test loss.
3. Plot loss vs compute on log-log axes -> points fall on a line.
4. Fit the line (the power law: loss ~ (C_c / C)^g + E).
5. EXTRAPOLATE the line out to your target budget (e.g. 1e24)
   to PREDICT the big model's loss before building it.
```

```text
log loss
  |  * 
  |    *  
  |       *      <- fitted line
  |          *
  |             ? <- predicted loss at target compute
  +---------------------- log compute
```

Why this is powerful and load-bearing:

- It de-risks nine-figure runs: you **forecast** the outcome from cheap experiments and only commit if the predicted loss justifies the spend.
- It informs the **(N, D) split**: fit the frontier, read off the compute-optimal size for your budget.
- It's remarkably reliable *for loss* — the metric that scales smoothly.

The important caveat: scaling laws predict **loss** cleanly, but **downstream task performance** is noisier and sometimes non-smooth (the emergent-abilities debate, Q7). So you can forecast next-token loss with confidence; forecasting exactly *which capabilities* that loss unlocks is much harder. Still, being able to predict loss at all is what makes frontier-scale investment a calculated bet rather than a leap of faith.

## Post-Training: SFT & Instruction Tuning

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

This topic explains the crucial gap between a **base model** and a **chat model**, and the first stage that bridges it: **supervised fine-tuning (SFT)**, also called **instruction tuning**. The central fact: a base model, freshly pretrained by next-token prediction on web text, is *not* an assistant — it just **completes text**. Ask it a question and it might continue with more questions, because that's what the internet looks like. SFT fixes this by continuing training on curated **(instruction, response) demonstration pairs** written or vetted by humans, teaching the model to (a) recognise an instruction and produce a helpful answer, and (b) speak in a structured **chat template** with special tokens marking system/user/assistant turns. Crucially, **SFT is still next-token prediction** — the same backprop-and-AdamW machinery from the training-mechanics topic — just on demonstration data instead of raw web text. The 16 questions cover what SFT can and can't fix, why **data quality and diversity** are the dominant lever, the chat template mechanics, and how SFT sets up **RLHF/DPO** (the preference-optimization stage that follows). It's the "finishing school" that turns raw scale into a usable assistant.

**Mental model**

A base model is a hyper-competent **improv text-completer** that has read the internet. It has all the knowledge and language ability, but no notion that it should *answer* you — its instinct is to continue the document in the most likely way, which for a question is often *more questions* or a tangent. SFT is a short, cheap phase of "here's how we'd like you to behave": you show it thousands of examples of a human asking something and an ideal assistant answering, formatted in a fixed turn-taking template, and you train on exactly those. Because it's the same next-token objective, the model simply learns that "after a user turn wrapped in these special tokens, the high-probability continuation is a helpful, well-structured assistant turn". You're not adding knowledge; you're **eliciting and formatting** knowledge the base model already has, and installing the assistant persona and the conversational protocol. Think of it as teaching an erudite person *the format of an interview*, not teaching them the facts.

**Key terms**

- **Base model** — the raw pretrained model; a next-token completer, not an assistant. Also called a foundation or pretrained model.
- **Chat / instruct model** — a base model after post-training (SFT, then usually RLHF/DPO) that follows instructions and converses.
- **Supervised fine-tuning (SFT)** — continued next-token training on curated (instruction, response) demonstration pairs.
- **Instruction tuning** — SFT specifically aimed at teaching instruction-following across many task types.
- **Demonstration data** — human-written (or human-vetted) examples of ideal assistant responses.
- **Chat template** — the fixed format with **special tokens** delimiting system/user/assistant turns.
- **Special tokens** — reserved vocabulary items (e.g. turn markers) added to structure conversations.
- **System prompt** — a leading instruction, in its own turn, that sets persona/rules for the conversation.
- **Loss masking** — training loss is computed only on the assistant's tokens, not the user's, so the model learns to *produce* answers, not questions.
- **Elicitation vs teaching** — SFT mostly surfaces latent base-model ability; it rarely installs genuinely new knowledge.
- **Alignment tax** — post-training can slightly degrade some raw capabilities as a cost of helpfulness/safety.
- **RLHF / DPO** — the preference-optimization stage after SFT (next-topic material) that refines *which* good answer to prefer.

**Why interviewers ask this**

Because a huge fraction of practitioners use ChatGPT-style models without knowing that the assistant behavior is a **thin, separate layer** on top of pretraining — and that confusion leads to wrong intuitions about what fine-tuning can do. A junior answer treats "the model" as one monolithic thing. A senior answer distinguishes base from chat, knows that SFT is *still next-token prediction* (not some new algorithm), can explain the chat template and loss masking, and — critically — knows the **limits**: SFT is great at teaching *format, style, and instruction-following*, but poor at reliably injecting *new factual knowledge* (that mostly lives in pretraining) and can induce hallucination if you fine-tune on facts the base model doesn't know. This directly informs real decisions ("should I fine-tune or use RAG?") and reveals whether a candidate understands the pretraining/post-training split that defines modern LLMs.

**Common confusions**

- "Fine-tuning is how you add knowledge to a model." Mostly false — knowledge comes from **pretraining**; SFT elicits and formats it. For new facts, prefer retrieval (RAG). Fine-tuning on unknown facts can *increase* hallucination.
- "SFT uses a different training algorithm than pretraining." No — same next-token cross-entropy, backprop, AdamW; only the *data* (curated demonstrations) and usually **loss masking** differ.
- "The base model is just a worse chat model." It's a *different kind of thing* — a completer, not a responder; it can be better at raw completion and creative continuation.
- "More SFT data is always better." Diversity and *quality* dominate; a few thousand excellent, varied examples often beat millions of mediocre ones (the "quality over quantity" finding).
- "RLHF replaces SFT." No — SFT comes first and is the foundation; RLHF/DPO *refines* it.

**What follows from this topic**

SFT produces a model that follows instructions but is only as good as its demonstrations — it can't easily learn *which* of two acceptable answers humans prefer, or be pushed on subtle helpfulness/harmlessness. That's the job of the next stage, **RLHF and DPO** (preference optimization from human comparisons), which sits directly on top of the SFT model. SFT also reuses everything from **Training Mechanics** (same backprop/AdamW loop) and only exists because **Scaling Laws** gave us a knowledgeable base model worth aligning. This topic is the hinge between "a trained brain" and "a usable assistant".

### Q1. Why is a base model not the same as a chat model?

Because they're trained on different objectives at different stages and behave differently:

- A **base model** is pretrained purely on **next-token prediction over web text**. Its only instinct is to produce the **most likely continuation** of whatever text it's given. It has enormous knowledge and language skill but no concept of "I should answer the user".
- A **chat model** is a base model that has undergone **post-training** — at minimum SFT, usually SFT + RLHF/DPO — which teaches it to interpret input as an instruction and respond as a helpful assistant in a structured format.

The classic demonstration — give both the prompt "What is the capital of France?":

```text
BASE model continuation (completes the document):
  "What is the capital of France? What is the capital of Germany?
   What is the largest city in Europe? Answer these questions..."
   -> it thinks it's seeing a quiz worksheet and continues the pattern.

CHAT model:
  "The capital of France is Paris."
   -> it recognises an instruction and answers.
```

The base model isn't broken — it's doing exactly what it was trained to do (continue text), and on the internet a question is often followed by *more questions*. SFT is what converts "continue the document" into "respond to the human". Understanding this split is the foundation of the whole topic.

### Q2. What is supervised fine-tuning (SFT), mechanically?

SFT is **continued training of the base model on curated (instruction, response) pairs**, using the **exact same next-token-prediction objective** as pretraining. The difference is entirely in the *data* and a small change in *what the loss is computed on*.

```text
pretraining data:   raw web text (undifferentiated)
SFT data:           thousands of (prompt, ideal_response) demonstrations,
                    formatted in the chat template, written/vetted by humans
```

Mechanically, one SFT step is identical to a pretraining step:

```python
for prompt, response in demonstrations:
    text = chat_template(prompt, response)   # wrap in special tokens
    logits = model(text.input_ids)
    loss = cross_entropy(logits, targets)    # SAME next-token loss
    loss = mask_to_response_tokens(loss)     # only score assistant tokens
    loss.backward()                          # SAME backprop
    optimizer.step()                         # SAME AdamW
```

The model just learns that, after a user turn wrapped in the template's special tokens, the high-probability continuation is a helpful assistant answer. It's **cheap** relative to pretraining — thousands to hundreds of thousands of examples, hours-to-days on far less hardware, not trillions of tokens on thousands of GPUs. No new architecture, no new algorithm: SFT is "pretraining, but on a small curated dataset of how to behave".

### Q3. If SFT is still next-token prediction, what actually changes in the model's behavior?

The **objective and mechanism are unchanged** — what changes is the **distribution the model has learned to imitate**. During SFT the model repeatedly sees "instruction, then ideal helpful response", so it shifts its probability mass so that, in the assistant slot, helpful and well-structured continuations become the most likely ones.

Concretely, SFT installs three things:

- **Instruction-following**: after a user turn, "answer the request" becomes higher-probability than "continue with more questions" or "change the subject". The base model's completer instinct is redirected.
- **The chat protocol**: the model learns the **special tokens** and turn structure (system/user/assistant), so it knows when its turn starts and — crucially — when to **stop** (emit the end-of-turn token) rather than rambling on and hallucinating the user's next message.
- **A persona and style**: tone, formatting (lists, code blocks), refusal behavior, helpfulness — whatever the demonstrations exemplify.

What does *not* change much: the underlying **knowledge and reasoning ability** — those are largely fixed in pretraining. SFT is **elicitation and formatting**, not new-knowledge installation. That's why the same weights can be great at completion (base) or great at chatting (SFT) — you've re-weighted *which* of its abilities surfaces by default, not taught it new facts.

### Q4. What is a chat template and why is it needed?

A **chat template** is the fixed textual format, using **special tokens**, that structures a multi-turn conversation into a single token stream the model can consume. It's needed because the model only ever sees a flat sequence of tokens — there's no separate "channel" for who's speaking, so speaker turns must be encoded *in the token stream itself*.

A schematic template (formats vary by model):

```text
<|system|>
You are a helpful assistant.
<|user|>
What is the capital of France?
<|assistant|>
The capital of France is Paris.<|end|>
```

The `<|system|>`, `<|user|>`, `<|assistant|>`, and `<|end|>` markers are **special tokens** — reserved single vocabulary items (not literal text) added to the tokenizer. Their jobs:

- **Delimit turns** so the model knows whose text is whose and where *its* turn begins.
- Let SFT apply **loss masking** — score only the assistant's tokens (Q5).
- Provide a **stop signal**: generation halts when the model emits the end-of-turn token, so it doesn't hallucinate the user's next message.
- Carry the **system prompt** in its own privileged slot for persona/rules.

Getting the template exactly right at inference matters enormously: using the *wrong* template (or none) on a chat model degrades it badly, because it was trained to expect precisely those special tokens as cues. The template is the *protocol* both training and inference must agree on.

### Q5. What is loss masking in SFT and why does it matter?

**Loss masking** means computing the training loss **only on the assistant's response tokens**, not on the system or user tokens. The user's message is fed in as *context* (the model attends to it) but the model is **not** scored on predicting it.

```text
<|user|> What is the capital of France? <|assistant|> The capital is Paris.
[------------- context, loss MASKED -------------][----- loss COUNTED -----]
```

Why it matters: the goal of SFT is to teach the model to **produce good answers**, not to get better at **generating user questions**. If you computed loss over the whole sequence, you'd be training the model to imitate *users* too — reinforcing exactly the "continue with more questions" behavior you're trying to remove, and wasting capacity modeling inputs it will always be *given* rather than generate.

By masking, the gradient signal flows only from "how well did the assistant tokens match the ideal response given this context", so the model concentrates entirely on the mapping instruction -> good answer. Mechanically it's a one-line change (zero out the loss on non-assistant positions before `backward()`), but conceptually it's what makes SFT teach *responding* rather than *conversing-as-anyone*.

### Q6. What can SFT fix, and what can't it fix?

A clean split, and one of the most useful things to know for real work:

```text
SFT is GOOD at (format & behavior):        SFT is BAD at (knowledge):
- instruction-following                    - injecting NEW facts reliably
- output format/style (JSON, tone, lists)  - fixing knowledge the base model lacks
- adopting a persona                        - deep new reasoning skills
- learning when to refuse / be safe        - anything absent from pretraining
- speaking the chat template               
- knowing when to stop                     
```

The principle: **knowledge lives in pretraining; SFT elicits and formats it.** SFT re-weights *which* latent abilities surface and *how* they're presented — it does not efficiently teach genuinely new information, because a few thousand examples can't overwrite what trillions of pretraining tokens established.

The dangerous corollary: **fine-tuning on facts the base model doesn't know can *increase* hallucination.** You're training the model to *confidently produce* a style of answer for topics it has no real knowledge of, teaching the *form* of an authoritative answer without the substance — so it learns to bluff. This is why, when the goal is new/proprietary knowledge, the right tool is usually **retrieval (RAG)** or long-context injection, not SFT. Fine-tune to change *behavior and format*; retrieve to change *knowledge*.

### Q7. Why is data quality and diversity the main lever in SFT, more than quantity?

Because SFT is **teaching behavior by imitation**, and the model imitates *exactly* what it sees — including the flaws. A body of research (e.g. the "LIMA / less-is-more" finding) showed that **a few thousand carefully curated, diverse, high-quality demonstrations** can produce a strong instruction-follower, often beating models tuned on **orders of magnitude more mediocre data**.

Why quality dominates quantity:

- **Behavior is copied faithfully**: sloppy, verbose, or subtly wrong demonstrations teach sloppy, verbose, wrong behavior. Bad examples are actively harmful, not just neutral.
- **Elicitation, not learning**: since the base model already *has* the capabilities, you don't need millions of examples to *teach* — you need enough good ones to reliably *surface* the right behavior. It's a light touch on an already-capable system.
- **Diversity drives generalisation**: coverage across *task types* (writing, coding, extraction, reasoning, refusal, multi-turn) matters more than depth in any one. The model generalises the *pattern* "follow the instruction" across tasks, so breadth teaches the meta-skill; a narrow dataset yields a narrow assistant.

Practical implication: SFT effort goes into **curation, filtering, deduplication, and human vetting**, not raw scraping. "Garbage in, garbage out" is unusually literal here because you're directly demonstrating the target behavior. A small, clean, varied dataset is the lever.

### Q8. How does SFT set up RLHF/DPO — why isn't SFT enough on its own?

SFT gets you a model that *follows instructions*, but it has a ceiling, and RLHF/DPO exists to break through it.

Where SFT falls short:

- **It can only imitate demonstrations.** It learns "produce an answer like this good example", but for most prompts there are *many* acceptable answers of varying quality, and SFT has no way to express *which is better* — it just maximises likelihood of the one demonstration it was given.
- **Writing ideal demonstrations is hard and inconsistent.** Humans are far better at **comparing** two answers ("A is better than B") than at authoring the perfect answer from scratch. Preference data is cheaper and more reliable to collect than gold demonstrations.
- **Subtle qualities are hard to demonstrate**: calibrated helpfulness, harmlessness, honesty, not-being-sycophantic — these are easier to *rank* than to *write*.

The pipeline that follows:

```text
1. Pretraining  -> base model (knowledge, language)
2. SFT          -> follows instructions, speaks chat template  <-- this topic
3. RLHF / DPO   -> optimises for HUMAN PREFERENCES over outputs <-- next topic
```

RLHF trains a **reward model** on human A-vs-B comparisons, then pushes the SFT model toward higher-reward outputs (with a KL leash to the SFT model so it doesn't drift). **DPO** achieves the same preference optimization directly from the comparison pairs, skipping the separate reward model and RL loop. Either way, SFT is the **required foundation**: you need an instruction-follower before you can refine *which* instructions-following answers humans prefer. SFT teaches the format; RLHF/DPO teaches the taste.

### Q9. Walk through the full journey from raw text to a deployed chat assistant.

Three stages, each building on the last:

```text
STAGE 1 - PRETRAINING (expensive, weeks, thousands of GPUs)
  data:      trillions of tokens of web/code/books
  objective: next-token prediction (cross-entropy)
  output:    BASE MODEL -> knows language & facts, but only completes text

STAGE 2 - SFT / INSTRUCTION TUNING (cheap, hours-days)      <-- this topic
  data:      thousands of curated (instruction, response) demonstrations
  objective: next-token prediction WITH loss masking, in chat template
  output:    INSTRUCT MODEL -> follows instructions, speaks in turns

STAGE 3 - PREFERENCE OPTIMIZATION: RLHF or DPO (moderate)
  data:      human A-vs-B preference comparisons
  objective: maximise a learned reward (PPO) or optimise preferences directly (DPO)
  output:    ALIGNED CHAT MODEL -> helpful, honest, harmless; the thing you ship
```

Key observations for an interview:

- **Cost is front-loaded**: pretraining is ~99% of the compute; post-training is a small, high-leverage finish.
- **Same core objective for stages 1-2**: both are next-token prediction; only data and masking differ. Stage 3 changes the objective to preference/reward.
- **Capabilities come from stage 1; behavior/alignment from stages 2-3.** Knowledge is baked early; helpfulness is polished late.

This staged view is *the* mental model of how a modern assistant is built, and being able to recite it — with what each stage adds — is a strong senior signal.

### Q10. Spot the misconception: "To make the model know my company's internal docs, I should fine-tune (SFT) it on them."

This is one of the most common and costly misconceptions. SFT is usually the **wrong tool for injecting knowledge**.

Why it fails:

- **Knowledge lives in pretraining, not SFT.** A few thousand fine-tuning examples can't reliably implant a large body of new facts against the weight of trillions of pretraining tokens.
- **It teaches the *form* of confident answers, not the *facts*.** Fine-tuning on Q&A about docs the base model has never seen trains it to *sound* authoritative on those topics — which **increases hallucination** when it's asked something adjacent it still doesn't actually know.
- **Docs change.** Re-fine-tuning every time a document updates is slow and expensive; the knowledge is frozen at training time.

The right tools:

```text
GOAL: inject/update KNOWLEDGE  -> RAG (retrieval-augmented generation)
                                  or long-context: put the docs IN the prompt.
GOAL: change BEHAVIOR/FORMAT/   -> SFT (fine-tuning) is appropriate:
      style/persona/refusals       teaching HOW to respond, not WHAT facts.
```

RAG keeps knowledge **external, fresh, and citable** — retrieve the relevant doc chunks at query time and let the model read them in-context. Reserve SFT for when you need to change *how* the model behaves (format, tone, domain-specific response style), not *what it knows*. "Fine-tune for behavior, retrieve for knowledge" is the rule. (The applied mechanics of RAG belong to the AI Engineering primer; the internal reason SFT can't cheaply add facts is the point here.)

### Q11. What are special tokens, and how do they get into the vocabulary?

**Special tokens** are reserved vocabulary items with a structural role rather than natural-language meaning — turn markers like `<|user|>`, `<|assistant|>`, `<|system|>`, end-of-turn/end-of-sequence markers, and sometimes tool-call or padding markers.

How they enter the model:

- They're **added to the tokenizer's vocabulary** as atomic entries (each is one token ID), so they can never be produced by ordinary text being tokenized — `<|user|>` typed literally by a user would tokenize into *ordinary* sub-tokens, not the special one. This separation is a **security/robustness** property: users can't inject fake role markers just by typing the string.
- Their **embedding rows** are added to the embedding matrix (shape grows from `(vocab, d_model)` to `(vocab + k, d_model)`), initialised fresh, and **learned during SFT** like any other weight.

```text
before SFT:  embedding matrix (V, d_model)
add k special tokens:
after:       embedding matrix (V + k, d_model)   <- new rows learned in SFT
```

During SFT the model learns what these tokens *mean operationally*: that `<|assistant|>` means "my turn to produce a helpful answer starts here" and the end-of-turn token means "stop". They're the vocabulary-level scaffolding of the chat template — the model's ability to take turns and stop cleanly is literally encoded in these learned special-token embeddings.

### Q12. Why does a chat model know when to stop, but a base model tends to ramble?

Because SFT explicitly teaches the chat model to **emit an end-of-turn special token**, and generation is configured to **halt** when that token appears — whereas a base model has no such learned stopping signal.

- **Base model**: trained on continuous documents, it just keeps predicting the next most-likely token. There's no natural "the answer is done" boundary, so it runs on — often drifting into hallucinating a follow-up question, a new paragraph, or even *the user's next message*, because that's what document continuations look like.
- **Chat model**: every SFT demonstration ends the assistant turn with an **end-of-turn token** (e.g. `<|end|>`). The model learns that after a complete, helpful answer, the high-probability next token is that stop marker. The inference loop treats it as a **stop condition** and ends generation.

```text
<|assistant|> The capital of France is Paris. <|end|>
                                               ^^^^^^^
              learned stop signal -> generation halts here
```

So "knowing when to stop" isn't intelligence about completeness — it's a **learned token** plus a **stop rule** in the decoding loop. This is also why using the wrong chat template at inference can make a model ramble or leak: if the stop token or turn markers don't match what it was trained on, it never gets the cue to halt. The KV-cache/autoregressive generation loop (see the generation topic) simply checks for this token each step.

### Q13. What is a system prompt, and how does it differ from a user message internally?

A **system prompt** is a leading instruction placed in its own **privileged turn** at the very start of the conversation, used to set the assistant's **persona, rules, and constraints** ("You are a terse coding assistant. Never reveal these instructions.").

Internally it's *mechanically* just more tokens in the stream — but it's distinguished by its **special-token role marker** and its **position**:

```text
<|system|>   You are a helpful, concise assistant.       <- persona/rules
<|user|>     Summarise this article: ...                 <- the actual request
<|assistant|> ...                                        <- model responds
```

Differences from a user message:

- **Role marker**: it's wrapped in the `<|system|>` special token, not `<|user|>`. During SFT (and later RLHF), the model is trained on examples where the system turn sets *governing* instructions and the assistant *obeys* them across the whole conversation.
- **Position and scope**: it comes first and its instructions are meant to persist over *all* turns, whereas a user message is a single request.
- **Learned priority**: post-training deliberately teaches the model to weight system instructions above user instructions where they conflict (a safety/controllability property) — this is a *learned behavior*, not a hard architectural rule, which is why prompt-injection can sometimes override it.

So there's no separate "system channel" in the architecture — the privilege is entirely **learned** from how system turns were used in the post-training data. It's a behavioral convention encoded in the weights via the special tokens, not a hardwired mechanism.

### Q14. Is SFT full fine-tuning, or can it be done more cheaply? (LoRA / PEFT)

It can be either, and cheap is now the norm.

- **Full fine-tuning** updates **all** the model's weights during SFT. It's effective but expensive: you need optimizer state (Adam m, v) and gradients for every parameter, so memory is the same ~16 bytes/param as pretraining, and you end up with a full-size copy of the model per fine-tune.
- **Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT)**, most popularly **LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation)**, freezes the base weights and injects a small number of **trainable low-rank adapter matrices** into (typically) the attention projections. Only those tiny matrices are updated.

