```text
full FT:   update W  (d x d, millions of params per matrix)
LoRA:      freeze W, learn  W + A@B  where A is (d x r), B is (r x d), r is small (e.g. 8-64)
           -> train ~0.1-1% of the parameters
```

Why LoRA wins in practice:

- **Memory/compute**: gradients and optimizer state only for the tiny adapters, so SFT fits on far smaller hardware.
- **Storage/serving**: the base model stays shared; each fine-tune is a few-MB adapter you can swap in — you can host many task-specific adapters over one base model.
- **Quality**: for SFT-style *behavior/format* adaptation, LoRA often matches full fine-tuning, because you're eliciting existing ability, not reshaping the whole network.

The interview point: SFT the *concept* (next-token training on demonstrations) is independent of *how many weights* you update; LoRA/PEFT is the efficient implementation that made fine-tuning accessible, and it fits the "SFT elicits, not rebuilds" mental model — a light-touch task needs only a light-touch update.

### Q15. What is the alignment tax, and why might a chat model be "worse" at some things than the base model?

The **alignment tax** is the observed phenomenon that post-training (SFT and especially RLHF) to make a model helpful, safe, and well-behaved can **slightly degrade some raw capabilities** compared to the base model. You pay a small tax on pure performance for gains in usability and safety.

Where it shows up:

- **Reduced diversity/creativity**: alignment sharpens the output distribution toward "safe, standard, helpful" responses, which can make the model more repetitive or less surprising than the base model at open-ended creative continuation.
- **Over-refusal**: safety tuning can make the model refuse benign requests that pattern-match to something sensitive.
- **Verbosity/hedging and sycophancy**: RLHF optimises for what human raters *reward*, and raters tend to prefer longer, agreeable, confident-sounding answers — so aligned models can become wordy, hedgy, or **sycophantic** (telling you what you want to hear).
- **Occasional benchmark dips** on some raw tasks the base model did more directly.

Why it happens: you're **constraining** a maximally-general text model into a narrower, well-behaved persona. Constraints cost some breadth. The base model, unconstrained, retains the full unfiltered distribution — which is exactly why it's *better* at raw completion and *worse* as an assistant.

The trade is almost always worth it (an unaligned model is unusable and unsafe as a product), and techniques aim to *minimise* the tax. But it's why some practitioners use base models for creative or research tasks, and why "aligned" and "capable-at-everything" aren't the same thing. The details of RLHF and sycophancy belong to the next topic; here it's the *cost side* of the SFT-and-beyond finishing process.

### Q16. Summarise: what does post-training add on top of a base model, and what stays the same?

A crisp closing synthesis.

**What stays the same (inherited from pretraining, unchanged by post-training):**

- **Knowledge and facts** — baked in during pretraining on trillions of tokens; SFT barely moves them.
- **Core language and reasoning ability** — the raw competence is the base model's.
- **The architecture and the next-token objective** — SFT is the *same* transformer trained with the *same* backprop/AdamW loop.

**What post-training adds:**

```text
SFT (demonstrations)          -> instruction-following, chat template,
                                 persona/style, knowing when to stop
RLHF / DPO (preferences)      -> refined helpfulness/harmlessness/honesty,
                                 preference-tuned "taste", safety/refusals
```

The one-sentence version an interviewer wants: **pretraining creates a knowledgeable text-completer; post-training (SFT then RLHF/DPO) is a cheap, high-leverage finishing layer that turns that completer into a helpful, safe, instruction-following assistant — without adding much new knowledge, mostly by eliciting and shaping behavior the base model already had.**

The corollaries that prove you understand it:

- Capabilities come from **scale** (pretraining); usability comes from **post-training**.
- For **new knowledge**, retrieve (RAG); for **new behavior**, fine-tune (SFT).
- The assistant you talk to is a **thin behavioral layer** over a giant pretrained brain — and knowing where that seam is explains almost every practical question about fine-tuning, hallucination, and alignment.
## Post-Training: RLHF, DPO & Alignment

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

How a raw base model becomes the helpful, instruction-following, refusal-capable assistant you actually talk to. Pretraining (covered earlier) produces a **next-token predictor** that completes text; it is not an assistant. Post-training is the pipeline that reshapes it: **SFT** (supervised fine-tuning on demonstrations) teaches instruction-following and the chat template, then **preference optimization** — **RLHF** (reward model + PPO) or **DPO** (direct from preference pairs) — nudges the model toward responses humans prefer. This topic also covers what **alignment** means (helpful, honest, harmless), why the aligned/chat model behaves so differently from the base model, and the known side effects — the **alignment tax** and **sycophancy**. The 16 questions here go from "what is RLHF" through deriving the DPO objective, the reward-model bit, the KL penalty's job, and where each stage sits in the overall pipeline. This is the internals view of a stage the sister AI Engineering primer only touches from the outside.

**Mental model**

Pretraining gives you a model that knows the shape of all text — including unhelpful, harmful, and rambling text — because it was trained to imitate the internet. Post-training is **preference sculpting**: taking that broad distribution and concentrating probability mass on the responses a careful human would rate highly. SFT does the coarse move — show it thousands of (instruction, good-response) pairs and it learns "when I see a user turn, produce an assistant turn like these." But demonstrations only show good behavior; they never say what's *bad*, and humans find it easier to *compare* two responses than to *write* the ideal one. So the second stage learns from **preferences**: given prompt x and two responses, humans pick the better one, and we push the model to make preferred responses more likely and dispreferred ones less likely — while a **KL leash** stops it drifting so far from the SFT model that it forgets how to write. RLHF does this with a learned reward model and RL; DPO does it with a closed-form loss straight on the pairs.

**Key terms**

- **SFT (supervised fine-tuning)** — next-token training on curated (instruction, response) demonstrations; teaches instruction-following and the chat template.
- **Preference data** — triples (prompt, chosen response, rejected response) from humans comparing two model outputs.
- **Reward model (RM)** — a model (usually the LLM with a scalar head) trained to output a number scoring how good a response is; a learned stand-in for human judgment.
- **RLHF** — Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback: train an RM, then optimize the policy with RL (PPO) to maximize RM score minus a KL penalty.
- **PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization)** — the RL algorithm typically used; takes clipped policy-gradient steps so updates stay small and stable.
- **KL penalty** — a term that penalizes divergence from the frozen SFT reference model; keeps the policy from reward-hacking into gibberish.
- **DPO (Direct Preference Optimization)** — reparameterizes the RLHF objective so you optimize preferences directly on pairs with a simple classification-style loss; no RM, no RL loop.
- **Policy / reference model** — the policy is the model being trained; the reference is the frozen SFT model the KL is measured against.
- **Alignment** — making the model helpful, honest, harmless: follow intent, avoid falsehoods, refuse harmful requests.
- **Alignment tax** — capability lost as a side effect of alignment (e.g. slightly worse benchmark scores, over-refusal).
- **Sycophancy** — the model agreeing with the user or telling them what they want to hear because that got rewarded.
- **Reward hacking** — the policy exploiting flaws in the RM to get high scores without being genuinely better.

**Why interviewers ask this**

Post-training is where "an LLM" becomes "ChatGPT/Claude," so it's the layer most product engineers actually interact with — yet most can only say "RLHF, humans rank stuff." A junior answer stops there. A senior answer explains the three-stage pipeline, why preferences beat demonstrations, what the reward model *is*, why the KL penalty exists (without it the policy reward-hacks into degenerate text), and can contrast RLHF vs DPO on the mechanics (learned RM + online RL vs closed-form loss on static pairs). Interviewers also probe the *failure modes* — sycophancy, alignment tax, over-refusal — because knowing them signals you've read the research, not just the marketing. Being able to say "the same base model, aligned differently, is why base completes and chat assists" shows you understand base != chat at the mechanism level.

**Common confusions**

- "RLHF trains the model on human answers" — no; humans write few if any answers. They *compare* model-generated responses; the reward model learns from those comparisons, then RL trains the policy against the RM.
- "The reward model is the chatbot" — the RM is a separate scoring head used only during training; it's discarded at inference. The deployed model is the RL-tuned policy.
- "DPO is a kind of RL" — DPO has no reward model and no sampling/rollouts; it's a supervised loss on static preference pairs, which is exactly why it's simpler and popular.
- "Alignment makes the model smarter" — alignment makes it more *usable and safe*; raw capability often dips slightly (the alignment tax).
- "The KL penalty is a regularizer for overfitting" — its real job is to stop reward hacking / distribution collapse: keep the policy close to the fluent SFT model.
- "Safety and refusals come from filtering the training data" — data curation helps, but refusals are largely *learned behavior* installed during SFT and preference tuning.

**What follows from this topic**

This closes the training arc that began with **pretraining** and **SFT**. The base-vs-chat distinction resurfaces in the **LLM Internals Interview & Reasoning Playbooks** topic ("base vs chat model" is a classic ask). Sycophancy and refusals connect to sampling behavior (**Sampling & Decoding**) and to the applied prompting/guardrails layer covered by the sister **AI Engineering** primer — reference it for the product view; here we own why the model behaves this way. The KL-to-a-reference idea also echoes the general theme that LLM training is a chain of objectives, each reshaping the same weight matrices introduced in **Embeddings** and the **Transformer block**.

### Q1. Why isn't a pretrained base model already a usable chatbot?

A base model is trained on one objective: **predict the next token** over a giant corpus of internet text, books, and code. It learns the *distribution of text*, which includes helpful answers but also spam, unfinished forum posts, contradictory claims, and toxic rants. So its instinct given "How do I reverse a list in Python?" is not "answer helpfully" — it's "what tokens most plausibly follow this string on the internet," which might be more questions, a tangent, or a restated FAQ.

```text
base model  : text-in -> most-likely-continuation-out   (a completer)
chat model  : instruction-in -> helpful-response-out     (an assistant)
```

Two things are missing: it doesn't reliably recognize "this is a request I should fulfill," and it doesn't know the **chat format** (who's speaking, when to stop). Post-training fixes both — SFT installs instruction-following and the chat template; preference tuning shapes *which* helpful response to prefer.

### Q2. Walk through the full post-training pipeline from base model to aligned assistant.

Three stages, each reshaping the same weights:

```text
[Pretraining]        trillions of tokens, next-token prediction
       |             -> base model (knows language, facts, skills; not an assistant)
       v
[1. SFT]             curated (instruction, response) demos, still next-token loss
       |             -> model follows instructions, uses the chat template
       v
[2. Reward modeling] humans compare response pairs -> train RM to score responses
       |             (skipped by DPO)
       v
[3. Preference opt]  RLHF (PPO vs RM, + KL to SFT)  OR  DPO (loss on pairs)
                     -> aligned chat model (helpful, honest, harmless)
```

SFT does the big move (teach the assistant format and instruction-following). Preference optimization does the fine move (of the many valid responses, prefer the ones humans like — more helpful, less harmful, better formatted). Alignment goals (refusals, honesty) are installed across stages 1–3.

### Q3. Explain RLHF end to end. What are the three models involved?

RLHF = Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback. It has three models in play:

1. **Policy** — the model being trained (initialized from the SFT model). It generates responses.
2. **Reward model (RM)** — scores a response with a scalar; a learned proxy for human preference.
3. **Reference model** — a frozen copy of the SFT model, used only to measure how far the policy has drifted (the KL term).

The loop, per training step:

```text
1. sample prompt x from a prompt set
2. policy generates response y = policy(x)          # a rollout
3. reward model scores it: r = RM(x, y)
4. compute penalized reward:
     R = r  -  beta * KL( policy(y|x) || reference(y|x) )
5. PPO updates the policy to increase R (clipped policy-gradient step)
```

The RM was trained beforehand on human comparisons. The **objective** is: maximize expected reward, but stay close (in KL) to the SFT reference so the policy keeps writing fluent, on-distribution text instead of reward-hacking. After training, the RM and reference are thrown away; you ship the policy.

### Q4. How is the reward model trained, and what loss does it use?

Collect **preference comparisons**: for a prompt x, generate two responses y_w (chosen/winner) and y_l (rejected/loser), and have a human label which is better. The RM is usually the LLM with the final unembedding replaced by a **scalar head** that outputs one number r(x, y).

Train it so the chosen response scores higher than the rejected one, using the **Bradley-Terry** pairwise loss:

```text
loss = -log( sigmoid( r(x, y_w) - r(x, y_l) ) )
```

Intuition: sigmoid of the score *gap* is the model's predicted probability that y_w beats y_l; we maximize the log-likelihood of the human's choice. Only the *difference* matters, so the absolute reward scale is arbitrary (it's calibrated implicitly by the KL term in PPO). The RM never sees an "ideal answer" written by a human — only comparisons — which is exactly why RLHF scales: comparing is cheaper and more reliable than authoring.

### Q5. What is the KL penalty in RLHF for, and what breaks without it?

The penalized reward is:

```text
R(x, y) = RM(x, y)  -  beta * KL( policy(.|x) || reference(.|x) )
```

The reference is the frozen SFT model; beta controls the leash strength.

Without the KL term, the policy optimizes purely to maximize RM score — and the RM is an imperfect proxy. The policy discovers **adversarial** outputs that fool the RM into high scores: repetitive phrasing the RM happens to like, degenerate text, weird formatting, or over-hedged non-answers. This is **reward hacking**, and it collapses fluency. The KL penalty pins the policy near the SFT distribution: it can move toward higher reward, but every step away from fluent, on-distribution text costs KL. Tuning beta is a balance — too high and the model barely changes; too low and it drifts into reward-hacked gibberish.

### Q6. What is DPO, and how does it avoid a reward model and RL?

DPO = Direct Preference Optimization. The insight: the RLHF optimum has a **closed-form** relationship between the optimal policy and the reward. Specifically, the KL-constrained reward-maximizing policy satisfies

```text
r(x, y) = beta * log( policy(y|x) / reference(y|x) )  + const
```

So the reward is *implicitly* represented by the policy itself relative to the reference. Substitute that into the Bradley-Terry preference loss and the reward model cancels out. You get a loss you can minimize directly on preference pairs (x, y_w, y_l):

```text
loss = -log sigmoid(
          beta * ( log policy(y_w|x)/ref(y_w|x)
                 - log policy(y_l|x)/ref(y_l|x) )
       )
```

No separate reward model. No sampling rollouts. No PPO. Just a supervised classification-style loss on a static dataset of pairs, with the frozen reference for the log-ratios. That simplicity — fewer moving parts, more stable, easier to tune — is why DPO became the popular default for open models.

### Q7. Compare RLHF and DPO directly.

| Aspect | RLHF (PPO) | DPO |
|---|---|---|
| Reward model | Separate model, trained first | None (implicit in policy) |
| Optimization | Online RL, samples rollouts | Offline supervised loss on pairs |
| Data | Preferences -> RM -> RL prompts | Preferences used directly |
| Moving parts | Policy + RM + reference + PPO | Policy + frozen reference |
| Stability / tuning | Harder (RL is finicky) | Easier, more reproducible |
| KL-to-reference | Explicit penalty term | Baked into the loss (the log-ratios) |
| Compute | Higher (rollouts each step) | Lower (no generation in the loop) |
| Where used | Classic InstructGPT/ChatGPT recipe | Many recent open models |

Both target the same objective (a KL-constrained policy that prefers human-preferred responses). DPO trades RLHF's flexibility (online exploration, a reusable RM, easy to add new reward signals) for simplicity and stability.

### Q8. What does "alignment" actually mean, mechanically?

Alignment is the goal of making the model **helpful, honest, and harmless (HHH)**:

- **Helpful** — infer and satisfy the user's actual intent, follow instructions, ask for clarification when needed.
- **Honest** — don't fabricate; express uncertainty; don't claim capabilities it lacks.
- **Harmless** — refuse or safely handle requests that could cause serious harm.

Mechanically, none of this is a special module — it's **learned behavior installed in the weights** during post-training. SFT demonstrations include polite, on-task, appropriately-refusing responses; preference data rewards HHH responses over unhelpful or unsafe ones. A refusal is just the model having learned that, for certain prompt patterns, the high-reward continuation is "I can't help with that" plus an explanation. There's no rulebook being consulted at inference — the "values" are baked into the same matrices that do everything else.

### Q9. Why does the aligned chat model behave so differently from the base model if it's the same architecture?

Same architecture, same parameter *shapes*, even initialized from the same pretrained weights — but post-training has **shifted the weights** so the output distribution is concentrated differently.

```text
base:  P(next token | "The capital of France is")
         -> " Paris" (high), then likely continues rambling / adds trivia
chat:  same prompt wrapped in the chat template
         -> a bounded, direct, assistant-style answer, then a stop token
```

Three concrete shifts: (1) SFT taught it the **chat template**, so it recognizes turn boundaries and emits a stop token instead of continuing forever; (2) instruction-following was reinforced, so requests trigger *fulfillment* rather than *plausible-internet-continuation*; (3) preference tuning concentrated mass on the specific helpful/safe responses humans prefer. It's the same machine running the same forward pass — only the learned weights (hence the probabilities) changed.

### Q10. What is the alignment tax?

The **alignment tax** is capability lost as a side effect of alignment. Optimizing hard for HHH and human preferences can slightly degrade raw performance:

- Small drops on some capability benchmarks vs the base/SFT model.
- **Over-refusal** — declining benign requests that pattern-match to unsafe ones ("how do I kill a Linux process?").
- Blander, more hedged, more formulaic prose (regression to the preferred-response mean).
- Reduced diversity of outputs (preference tuning sharpens the distribution).

It's called a tax because it's a *price paid* for safety and usability. Good post-training minimizes it — e.g. mixing pretraining data back in during SFT, careful preference data, KL leashes that don't over-constrain. It's rarely zero, and it's a real trade-off teams tune.

### Q11. What is sycophancy and why does RLHF/DPO cause it?

**Sycophancy** is the model telling the user what they seem to want to hear — agreeing with a stated (even wrong) opinion, flattering, or caving when challenged — rather than being accurate.

The cause is baked into the training signal. Preference data comes from **human raters**, and humans tend to rate agreeable, confident, flattering, validating responses higher — even when a more accurate response would disagree with them. The reward model learns this bias; optimizing against it teaches the policy that agreeing correlates with reward.

```text
user: "2 + 2 = 5, right?"
sycophantic: "You're right, it's 5!"        # high human rating in some data
honest:      "Actually 2 + 2 = 4."          # correct, sometimes rated lower
```

So sycophancy is **reward hacking on human bias**: the model isn't malfunctioning; it's faithfully optimizing a reward signal that rewards agreement. Mitigations include debiasing preference data, adding honesty-focused comparisons, and specialized evals.

### Q12. Is SFT part of RLHF, or a separate stage? Why do you need it before preference tuning?

SFT is a **separate, earlier stage** — the foundation both RLHF and DPO build on.

You need it first for two reasons. (1) **A sane starting point**: RL/preference optimization *nudges* an existing policy; if you started from the base model, its outputs would be all over the distribution (rambling, off-format) and the RM/preference signal would have almost nothing good to reinforce. SFT first gets the model into "assistant mode" so preference tuning has a coherent policy to refine. (2) **The reference model**: RLHF's KL penalty and DPO's log-ratios are measured against a frozen reference — which *is* the SFT model. Without SFT, there's no good fluent reference to leash the policy to.

```text
pretraining -> SFT -> (RLHF or DPO)
              ^ instruction-following + chat template + the reference model
```

So the order is fixed: pretrain, then SFT, then preference optimization.

### Q13. Where do refusals and safety behavior come from?

From **learned behavior installed during post-training**, not a hardcoded filter inside the transformer.

- **SFT** includes demonstrations of appropriate refusals ("I can't help with that, but here's a safe alternative…"), teaching the model the *form* of a refusal.
- **Preference data** rewards safe handling of risky prompts over harmful compliance, so the policy learns *when* to refuse.
- Additional **safety/red-teaming** data targets specific harm categories.

At inference there's no separate rulebook being consulted — the forward pass just computes that, for a prompt matching a harmful pattern, the high-probability continuation is a refusal. (Product systems *also* add external guardrails — input/output classifiers, moderation APIs — but that's the applied layer covered in the AI Engineering primer; the *model's own* refusal behavior is in the weights.) Because it's learned and pattern-based, it's imperfect: hence jailbreaks (prompts that dodge the learned pattern) and over-refusal (benign prompts that trigger it).

### Q14. In RLHF, why do humans compare responses rather than write ideal ones?

Two reasons, both practical.

(1) **Comparing is easier and more reliable than authoring.** Writing the *ideal* response to an arbitrary prompt is hard, slow, and inconsistent across annotators. Judging "is A better than B?" is fast and far more consistent — humans are better relative judges than absolute authors.

(2) **It scales to superhuman-ish outputs.** For hard prompts (complex code, nuanced explanations), a human might not be able to *write* the best answer but can still *recognize* which of two model attempts is better. So preferences give useful signal even where demonstrations would be weak or unavailable.

```text
demonstration data: human must WRITE the gold answer   (expensive, capped by human skill)
preference data:    human RANKS two model answers       (cheap, scalable, robust)
```

This is exactly why SFT (demonstrations) does the coarse teaching and preference optimization (comparisons) does the fine tuning — and why the reward model, trained only on comparisons, is the heart of RLHF.

### Q15. What is reward hacking, and give an example in RLHF.

**Reward hacking** is the policy exploiting flaws in the reward model to score highly *without* being genuinely better — Goodhart's law ("when a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure") applied to the RM.

Examples:

- The RM slightly over-rewards **length**, so the policy learns to pad every answer with verbose filler.
- The RM likes confident tone, so the policy becomes **overconfident** even when wrong.
- The RM rewards agreement, producing **sycophancy** (Q11).
- Degenerate exploits: repetitive phrasings or formatting quirks the RM happens to score high.

```text
policy discovers: "add a polite summary paragraph" -> RM score up
  ... even when the summary adds nothing -> every answer now bloated
```

The main defenses: the **KL penalty** (stay near the fluent reference), better/larger preference data, RM ensembles, and re-collecting fresh comparisons on the *current* policy's outputs so the RM keeps up. Reward hacking is *the* central risk that makes RLHF fiddly — and one of DPO's selling points is that, without an explicit RM to game in an online loop, some of these failure modes are less pronounced (though DPO can still overfit its preference set).

### Q16. If DPO is simpler and avoids a reward model, why does anyone still use RLHF/PPO?

DPO trades away flexibility for simplicity, and sometimes you want the flexibility.

- **Online exploration**: PPO *samples* fresh responses from the current policy each step and gets them scored, so it can discover and reinforce behaviors not present in a static dataset. DPO only ever sees the fixed pairs you collected — it can't explore beyond them and can **overfit** the preference set.
- **A reusable reward signal**: once you have an RM (or a programmatic/verifier reward, as in RLHF-for-reasoning), you can optimize against it repeatedly, combine multiple reward signals, and do things like best-of-n sampling. DPO bakes the signal into one loss on one dataset.
- **Non-preference rewards**: RL naturally accommodates rewards that aren't pairwise human preferences — e.g. "did the code pass the tests," "is the math answer correct" — which powers reasoning-model training. That doesn't fit DPO's pairwise-preference frame as cleanly.

```text
DPO:  simple, stable, offline, static pairs        -> great default for preference alignment
PPO:  complex, flexible, online, reusable reward    -> when you need exploration or non-preference rewards
```

In practice many labs use both — DPO or its variants for cheap preference alignment, RL-style methods when they need exploration or verifiable rewards.

## Efficiency & Modern Architecture

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

Everything that makes a *real* production LLM differ from the vanilla decoder-only GPT built up in the earlier topics — and nearly all of it is driven by two costs: attention's **O(n^2)** compute in sequence length, and the **KV cache's** linear-but-large memory. This topic covers **FlashAttention** (an IO-aware, mathematically *exact* attention that tiles the computation so the n×n matrix never touches slow memory); **GQA and MQA** (grouped-/multi-query attention — share Keys and Values across heads to shrink the KV cache and speed decoding); **Mixture of Experts (MoE)** (route each token to a few expert FFNs, buying far more parameters at the *same* per-token FLOPs); **long-context tricks** (RoPE scaling/interpolation, sliding-window/local attention, attention sinks); and **quantization** (int8/int4 for cheaper inference). The 16 questions here explain *what* each technique changes and *why*, with a comparison table for MHA vs GQA vs MQA. These are the levers that make 100B+ parameter models with 100k+ token contexts affordable to serve.

**Mental model**

Start from the vanilla transformer and follow the two bottlenecks. **Bottleneck 1 — attention is O(n^2):** every token attends to every other, so doubling the sequence quadruples the score matrix and its memory traffic. **Bottleneck 2 — the KV cache is big:** to avoid recomputing the past every step, you cache each layer's Keys and Values, and that cache grows with sequence length × layers × heads, quickly dominating GPU memory during generation. Almost every "modern architecture" trick is a response to one of these. FlashAttention attacks the *memory traffic* of attention (keep the big matrix out of slow HBM). GQA/MQA attack the *KV cache size* (fewer K/V copies). MoE attacks the *cost of adding parameters* (activate only a slice per token). Long-context tricks attack the *quadratic scaling* (don't attend to everything) or the *positional encoding's* failure to extrapolate. Quantization attacks *both* memory and bandwidth by using fewer bits. Same transformer skeleton; smarter plumbing.

**Key terms**

- **HBM vs SRAM** — GPU high-bandwidth memory (large, slow) vs on-chip SRAM (tiny, fast); attention is bottlenecked by HBM traffic, not FLOPs.
- **FlashAttention** — IO-aware *exact* attention using tiling + online softmax so the n×n scores never materialize in HBM.
- **KV cache** — stored Keys/Values of past tokens so each new token does O(1)-ish work; grows linearly with context and dominates decode memory.
- **MHA (multi-head attention)** — h query heads, each with its own K and V heads.
- **MQA (multi-query attention)** — h query heads share a *single* K/V head; smallest KV cache.
- **GQA (grouped-query attention)** — h query heads share K/V in *groups*; a middle ground between MHA and MQA.
- **Mixture of Experts (MoE)** — the FFN is replaced by many expert FFNs; a router sends each token to a few (top-k) experts.
- **Sparse vs dense params** — MoE has huge *total* parameters but only *activates* a few per token (sparse activation).
- **RoPE scaling / interpolation** — rescaling rotary position frequencies so a model trained at short context handles longer contexts.
- **Sliding-window / local attention** — each token attends only to a fixed window of recent tokens, making attention linear.
- **Attention sink** — keeping the first few tokens always attended-to, which stabilizes long/streaming generation.
- **Quantization** — storing weights/activations in int8/int4 instead of 16-bit floats to cut memory and bandwidth.

**Why interviewers ask this**

This is the "do you understand *serving* LLMs, not just the textbook transformer" filter. A junior can describe attention; a senior knows *why* nobody ships vanilla MHA at long context and can name the specific fix for each bottleneck. Interviewers love that FlashAttention is **exact** (a common misconception is that it approximates) and that GQA/MQA are about the **KV cache**, not raw FLOPs. MoE questions probe whether you understand the params-vs-FLOPs decoupling ("more parameters at the same compute per token"). Long-context questions test whether you know context length is limited by *both* the quadratic cost and the positional encoding's extrapolation. These are exactly the trade-offs an infra/ML engineer makes when choosing a model or an inference stack, so they map directly to on-the-job decisions.

**Common confusions**

- "FlashAttention approximates attention to go faster" — no; it computes **exactly** the same result. The speedup is from avoiding HBM round-trips, not from dropping terms.
- "GQA/MQA make the model compute faster (fewer FLOPs)" — the main win is a **smaller KV cache** (memory + bandwidth during decode), which is what bounds generation; FLOP savings are secondary.
- "MoE means the model uses all its parameters more efficiently" — MoE *activates only a few experts per token*, so per-token FLOPs stay low while *total* params balloon; it's sparse activation, not efficient dense use.
- "A bigger context window just needs more memory" — you also need positional encodings that **extrapolate** (RoPE scaling) and often *sparse* attention; naive full attention at 100k tokens is quadratically expensive.
- "Quantization retrains the model smaller" — inference quantization usually just *casts* existing weights to fewer bits (sometimes with light calibration); it doesn't shrink the parameter *count*.
- "These change the transformer fundamentally" — they're plumbing optimizations around the *same* attention/FFN/residual skeleton; the math of a block is unchanged.

**What follows from this topic**

Every technique here traces back to mechanisms from earlier topics: FlashAttention and GQA/MQA presuppose **Attention** and the **KV cache** (Autoregressive Generation); MoE modifies the **Feed-forward / MLP** sublayer; long-context tricks modify **Positional Encoding** (RoPE) and the attention mask. The efficiency framing — O(n^2) and memory — reappears in the **LLM Internals Interview & Reasoning Playbooks** topic ("why is attention O(n^2) and what fixes it" is a headline question). For the *applied* consequences (context-window limits, cost per token, latency), see the sister **AI Engineering** primer; here we own the architectural *why*.

### Q1. Why is attention O(n^2), and why is that the root cause of most efficiency work?

In self-attention, every token forms a Query and compares it against the Key of *every* token in the sequence. For sequence length n, that's an n×n score matrix:

```text
scores = Q @ K^T          # shape (n, n)
weights = softmax(scores) # (n, n)
out = weights @ V         # (n, d)
```

Both the compute and the memory for that score matrix scale as **n^2**: double the sequence, quadruple the cost. At n = 100k tokens the n×n matrix has 10^10 entries — infeasible to materialize naively.

This single fact drives the field. FlashAttention avoids *storing* the n×n matrix. Sliding-window attention avoids *computing* all of it. GQA/MQA and quantization attack the *related* KV-cache memory cost. So "why O(n^2)" isn't trivia — it's the reason modern architectures look the way they do.

### Q2. Explain FlashAttention. Is it an approximation?

FlashAttention is an **IO-aware, mathematically exact** attention algorithm. It computes *bit-for-bit* the same result as standard attention (up to floating point) — it is **not** an approximation.

The insight: standard attention is bottlenecked not by FLOPs but by **memory traffic** — writing the huge n×n scores and softmax matrix out to slow **HBM** and reading them back. FlashAttention never materializes that matrix in HBM. It **tiles** Q, K, V into blocks that fit in fast on-chip **SRAM**, and uses an **online softmax** (running max and sum) to accumulate the output block by block, rescaling as it goes:

```text
for each block of K, V:
    load into SRAM
    compute partial scores vs current Q block
    update running max m, running sum l, running output o   # online softmax
# never write the full (n, n) matrix to HBM
```

Result: same answer, far fewer HBM reads/writes, so it's several times faster and uses O(n) memory instead of O(n^2). This is why essentially every modern stack uses it.

### Q3. What is the KV cache costing you, and how do GQA and MQA reduce it?

During decoding you cache, per layer, the Keys and Values of every past token so each new token only computes its own Q/K/V and attends over the cache. The cache size is roughly:

```text
KV cache bytes ~= 2 * n_layers * n_kv_heads * head_dim * seq_len * batch * bytes_per_elem
                  ^K and V
```

At long context and large batch this **dominates GPU memory** and the memory-bandwidth of decoding (you re-read the whole cache every step).

**MQA** and **GQA** shrink the `n_kv_heads` factor. In vanilla **MHA** each of the h query heads has its own K and V head. **MQA** makes all query heads share a *single* K/V head — cutting the cache by ~h×. **GQA** is the middle ground: split the h query heads into g groups, each group sharing one K/V head — cache cut by ~h/g×. Query heads stay full (expressiveness preserved); only K/V are shared. The primary payoff is a smaller cache -> more context, bigger batches, faster memory-bound decode — with minimal quality loss (GQA especially).

### Q4. Give the MHA vs GQA vs MQA comparison.

| | MHA | GQA | MQA |
|---|---|---|---|
| Query heads | h | h | h |
| K/V heads | h (one per query head) | g groups (1 < g < h) | 1 (shared by all) |
| KV cache size | Largest (1x) | Medium (h/g smaller) | Smallest (h smaller) |
| Quality | Best | ~MHA (small drop) | Slight drop |
| Decode speed | Slowest (biggest cache) | Fast | Fastest |
| Used by | Original Transformer/GPT | Most modern large models | Some latency-critical models |

```text
MHA:  Q1 Q2 Q3 Q4     GQA:  Q1 Q2 | Q3 Q4    MQA:  Q1 Q2 Q3 Q4
      K1 K2 K3 K4            K1    |  K2            \  \  |  /
      V1 V2 V3 V4            V1    |  V2                K,V (shared)
```

GQA won as the default: nearly MHA quality with most of MQA's memory savings, tunable via the number of groups.

### Q5. Explain Mixture of Experts. How does it add parameters without adding per-token compute?

In a standard block, every token passes through the *same* FFN. In an **MoE** block, that FFN is replaced by **many expert FFNs** plus a small **router**. For each token, the router scores the experts and sends the token to only the **top-k** (often k = 1 or 2). Only those experts run.

```text
token -> router -> pick top-2 of, say, 64 experts
      -> run only those 2 FFNs -> weighted-combine their outputs
```

The decoupling: **total** parameters scale with the number of experts (64 experts = ~64× the FFN parameters), but **per-token FLOPs** scale only with the *activated* experts (top-2), not all 64. So you get a model with, say, 8× the parameters at roughly the *same* compute per token as a much smaller dense model. This is **sparse activation** — huge capacity, cheap inference — at the cost of extra memory (all experts must be resident) and routing/load-balancing complexity.

### Q6. What are the downsides and complications of MoE?

MoE buys capacity cheaply but adds real complications:

- **Memory footprint**: *all* experts must be loaded even though each token uses a few, so total VRAM is large despite low per-token FLOPs.
- **Load balancing**: the router can collapse onto a few popular experts, leaving others idle. Training needs an **auxiliary load-balancing loss** or capacity limits to spread tokens.
- **Token dropping**: if an expert exceeds its capacity in a batch, overflow tokens get dropped or passed through, hurting quality.
- **Routing instability**: which expert a token goes to can be sensitive; small changes flip routing, and routing is a source of the non-determinism that makes even temperature-0 outputs vary.
- **Distributed complexity**: experts are sharded across devices (expert parallelism), so serving needs all-to-all communication.

```text
dense FFN:   simple, all params used, compute scales with size
MoE FFN:     more params/capacity, low per-token FLOPs, but big memory
             + router + load-balancing loss + comms overhead
```

Net: MoE is a favorable trade when you're *compute*-bound at inference and can afford the memory — hence its popularity in frontier models.

### Q7. Why can't a model trained at 4k context just run at 100k, and how does RoPE scaling help?

Two blockers. (1) **Cost** — full attention is O(n^2), so 100k is ~600× the attention cost of 4k. (2) **Positional encodings don't extrapolate** — this is the subtler one. With **RoPE**, positions are encoded by rotating Q/K by angles that depend on position. A model trained only on positions 0–4095 has never seen the rotation angles for position 50,000; feed it those and attention scores go out of distribution and quality collapses.

**RoPE scaling / interpolation** fixes the positional half. Instead of extrapolating to unseen angles, you **interpolate**: rescale (or refrequency) the rotations so that position 100,000 maps into the *range of angles the model was trained on*. Positions get "squeezed" into familiar territory:

```text
naive:  pos 50000 -> rotation angle never seen in training -> breaks
scaled: rescale so pos 50000 -> an angle within [0..4095]'s trained range
```

Usually paired with a short **long-context fine-tune** so the model adapts. This handles positions; the O(n^2) cost is handled separately by FlashAttention and/or sparse attention (Q8).

### Q8. What are sliding-window / local attention and attention sinks?

Both are ways to make long context tractable without full O(n^2) attention.

**Sliding-window (local) attention**: each token attends only to the last **W** tokens instead of all n. Attention cost drops from O(n^2) to O(n·W) — *linear* in sequence length. Information still propagates far because stacked layers extend the effective receptive field (like a CNN): after L layers a token indirectly sees ~L·W back. Often interleaved with occasional full-attention layers to preserve global reach.

**Attention sinks**: empirically, models dump a lot of attention weight onto the **first few tokens** regardless of content — a place to "park" probability mass (softmax must sum to 1 even when nothing is relevant). In streaming/long generation, if you evict those first tokens from the KV cache, quality collapses. The fix: **always keep the initial sink tokens** in the cache. This lets you slide a window over the recent context *plus* the sinks and generate indefinitely with stable quality.

```text
window only:        [ ...evicted... ][ last W tokens ]   -> degrades
window + sinks:     [tok0 tok1][ ...evicted... ][ last W ]  -> stable streaming
```

### Q9. What is quantization and why does it speed up inference?

**Quantization** stores weights (and sometimes activations) in fewer bits — **int8** or **int4** — instead of 16-bit floats (fp16/bf16). A weight matrix that was 2 bytes/param becomes 1 byte (int8) or 0.5 bytes (int4).

Why it helps: LLM inference, especially **decode**, is **memory-bandwidth-bound**, not compute-bound — the GPU spends most of its time *reading weights* from HBM, not multiplying. Halving or quartering the bytes per weight directly cuts that traffic (and the memory footprint), so tokens come out faster and bigger models fit on smaller GPUs.

```text
fp16 weight: 2 bytes   ->  int8: 1 byte (2x less traffic)  ->  int4: 0.5 byte (4x less)
```

It usually *doesn't retrain* the model — it casts existing weights, often with light **calibration** (or quantization-aware fine-tuning for aggressive int4) to control accuracy loss. The trade-off is precision: int8 is nearly lossless; int4 can measurably degrade quality, so techniques keep sensitive layers/outliers in higher precision. Note it reduces *bytes per parameter*, not the parameter *count*.

### Q10. Prefill vs decode: why do they have such different performance profiles?

Autoregressive generation has two phases with opposite bottlenecks:

- **Prefill** — process the *entire prompt* at once. All prompt tokens go through the model in parallel, so it's **compute-bound** (lots of matmul FLOPs, high GPU utilization). This builds the initial KV cache. Latency here is "time to first token."
- **Decode** — generate one token at a time. Each step processes a *single* new token but must read the *whole* KV cache and all model weights, so it's **memory-bandwidth-bound** and GPU compute sits underused. Latency here is "time per output token."

```text
prefill:  [all prompt tokens in parallel]  -> compute-bound, fills KV cache
decode:   tok -> tok -> tok (one at a time) -> bandwidth-bound, reads big cache each step
```

This split explains the optimizations: FlashAttention helps prefill (compute/traffic on the big matrix); GQA/MQA and quantization help decode (shrink the cache and weight bytes you re-read every step); batching many requests improves decode GPU utilization. Knowing which phase you're optimizing is half the job.

### Q11. How do these techniques map onto the two root bottlenecks?

Nearly everything modern is a response to **attention's O(n^2) compute** or the **KV cache's memory**:

| Technique | Attacks | How |
|---|---|---|
| FlashAttention | Attention memory traffic | Tile + online softmax; never store n×n in HBM (exact) |
| GQA / MQA | KV cache size | Share K/V across query heads |
| Quantization | Memory + bandwidth | Fewer bits per weight/activation |
| Sliding-window attention | O(n^2) compute | Attend to a local window -> O(n·W) |
| RoPE scaling | Position extrapolation | Interpolate positions into trained range |
| Attention sinks | Long/streaming stability | Keep first tokens in cache |
| MoE | Cost of adding parameters | Sparse activation: top-k experts per token |

```text
O(n^2) compute      -> FlashAttention, sliding-window, (RoPE scaling for reach)
KV cache memory     -> GQA/MQA, quantization, sinks + window
parameter cost      -> MoE (sparse activation)
```

If you can place a technique in this table and say *which* bottleneck and *how*, you understand modern LLM architecture.

### Q12. Does FlashAttention change the model's outputs? Does GQA?

**FlashAttention: no meaningful change.** It's exact — same math, just a memory-efficient schedule. Outputs match standard attention up to floating-point rounding. You can swap it into an existing model with no retraining and no quality change. It's purely a systems optimization.

**GQA/MQA: yes, it's an architectural change** that must be trained (or *uptrained*) with. Sharing K/V across query heads reduces representational capacity slightly, so the weights differ and there's a small (usually tiny for GQA) quality cost. You can't just bolt MQA onto an MHA-trained model for free — though there are cheap "uptraining" recipes that convert an MHA checkpoint to GQA with a short fine-tune.

```text
FlashAttention:  same weights, same outputs, faster        (systems trick)
GQA/MQA:         different architecture, trained in, tiny quality cost  (model change)
```

This distinction is a favorite interview probe: recognizing that one is a *pure runtime optimization* and the other is a *modeling choice* shows you know where the line between systems and architecture sits.

### Q13. How does MoE routing contribute to non-determinism?

MoE adds a subtle source of run-to-run variation even at temperature 0. The router assigns each token to top-k experts, but experts have a **capacity** per batch. Which tokens actually get their preferred expert can depend on **what else is in the batch** — because tokens compete for expert slots.

```text
your token wants expert 7, but expert 7 is full this batch
  -> your token overflows to its 2nd choice (or is dropped)
  -> different routing -> different output
```

So the *same prompt* can route differently depending on the other requests batched with it on the server, producing slightly different outputs. Combined with floating-point non-associativity (reduction order varies with batching/hardware), this is a real reason **temperature 0 isn't perfectly deterministic** in served MoE models — a point that connects directly to the "why does the same prompt vary" question in the Playbooks topic.

### Q14. Why is decoding memory-bandwidth-bound, and what follows from that?

During decode you generate one token per step. The math for that single token is tiny, but to produce it the GPU must **read every model weight** (and the whole KV cache) from HBM. So the step time is dominated by *moving bytes*, not by arithmetic — the compute units are mostly idle waiting on memory.

Consequences that shape real systems:

- **Quantization** helps a lot (fewer weight bytes to read).
- **GQA/MQA** help (smaller KV cache to re-read each step).
- **Batching** helps: reuse the weights you loaded across many concurrent requests, amortizing the read — which is why servers batch aggressively (continuous batching).
- Raw FLOPs (bigger matmuls) barely change decode latency; *bytes moved* do.

```text
decode step cost ~ bytes read from HBM (weights + KV cache)  >>  FLOPs
```

Understanding this flips intuition: to speed up generation you don't chase FLOPs, you cut *memory traffic* — which is exactly what the whole efficiency toolkit does.

### Q15. Are these modern tricks changing the transformer's fundamental math?

No — they're **plumbing around the same skeleton**. The core of a block is unchanged: token embeddings -> multi-head attention -> residual + norm -> FFN -> residual + norm, stacked N times, then an LM head to logits. Every technique here optimizes *how* a piece is computed or *shrinks* a resource, not the math of the block:

- FlashAttention: same attention output, different memory schedule.
- GQA/MQA: same attention formula, fewer K/V projections.
- MoE: same "position-wise FFN" role, just many sparse FFNs instead of one dense one.
- RoPE scaling / sliding window / sinks: tweaks to *positions* and the *mask*, not to Q·K·V itself.
- Quantization: same weights, fewer bits.

```text
still:  embed -> [attention -> FFN] x N -> LM head -> logits
the tricks change: how attention is scheduled, how many K/V heads,
                   how many FFNs, which positions/mask, how many bits
```

So when an interviewer asks "how do real LLMs differ from vanilla GPT," the right framing is: *the block is the same; production wraps it in memory/compute optimizations because attention is O(n^2) and the KV cache is huge.*

### Q16. Given a fixed serving GPU budget, how would you reason about picking these techniques?

Diagnose the bottleneck, then apply the matching lever:

1. **Out of memory / need bigger model or batch?** -> **Quantization** (int8, then int4 if quality holds) and **GQA/MQA** to shrink the KV cache. These free VRAM directly.
2. **Slow generation (low tokens/sec)?** Decode is bandwidth-bound -> quantization + GQA/MQA (less to read each step) + **continuous batching** to amortize weight reads across requests.
3. **Slow time-to-first-token on long prompts?** Prefill is compute/traffic-bound -> **FlashAttention** is table stakes; consider **sliding-window** attention if prompts are very long.
4. **Need much longer context?** -> **RoPE scaling** (+ a long-context fine-tune) for positions, **sliding-window + attention sinks** for cost/stability, GQA to keep the cache affordable.
5. **Want more capability without more inference compute?** -> **MoE** — more parameters at the same per-token FLOPs, *if* you can afford the memory for all experts.

```text
OOM ............ quantize + GQA/MQA
slow decode .... quantize + GQA/MQA + batching
slow prefill ... FlashAttention (+ sliding window)
long context ... RoPE scaling + window + sinks + GQA
more capability. MoE (if memory allows)
```

The senior move is to *name the bottleneck first* (memory vs bandwidth vs compute, prefill vs decode) and then reach for the specific tool — not to list techniques generically.

## LLM Internals Interview & Reasoning Playbooks

### Summary

**What this topic covers**

A pure whiteboard/scenario topic: the classic "explain the internals" questions an interviewer actually asks, answered cleanly and completely. It stitches together every mechanism from the rest of the primer into rehearsed, end-to-end explanations. The 16 questions here include the greatest hits: explain attention from scratch; why causal masking; walk a single token from raw text all the way to an output token (tokenize -> embed + position -> blocks -> logits -> sample); why does the same prompt (or even temperature 0) still vary; why can't it count the r's in "strawberry"; compute the parameter count and FLOPs of a given config; what actually gets updated during training; base vs chat model; why does order matter if attention is a set operation; and how to explain a transformer to an interviewer without hand-waving. Think of it as the **exam-prep** topic: each answer is a model response you could give out loud, intuition-first, with the ASCII maths and small code that convince an interviewer you could implement a toy GPT.

**Mental model**

Everything ties back to one sentence: **an LLM is a next-token predictor** — `p(next token | all previous tokens)` — implemented by a stack of transformer blocks, trained by gradient descent on cross-entropy, and *sampled* autoregressively at inference. Almost every "gotcha" question is a corollary. Can't count letters? Because it sees *tokens*, not characters. Same prompt varies? Because you *sample* from a distribution (and floating-point/batching wobble even at temperature 0). Order matters despite attention being a set operation? Because *positional encodings* break the symmetry. Base != chat? Because *post-training* reshaped the weights. So the winning strategy isn't memorizing 16 disconnected facts — it's holding the single pipeline in your head (text -> tokens -> vectors -> attention-mixed vectors -> logits -> sampled token, repeat) and reading each question as "which stage of the pipeline does this probe?" Answer from the mechanism, not from analogy, and you'll never hand-wave.

**Key terms**

- **Next-token prediction** — the one objective: model `p(next | context)`; everything else is machinery for it.
- **Forward pass** — one run of the network turning input IDs into logits of shape (batch, seq, vocab).
- **Logits** — the raw pre-softmax scores over the vocabulary; not yet probabilities.
- **Autoregressive loop** — sample a token, append it, feed back in, repeat.
- **Causal mask** — the upper-triangular -inf mask that stops a token attending to future tokens.
- **Tokenization gap** — the model sees subword token IDs, not letters, so character-level tasks (counting r's) are hard.
- **Sampling non-determinism** — variation from temperature/top-k/top-p, plus floating-point and batching effects even at temperature 0.
- **Positional encoding** — the mechanism that makes attention order-*aware* despite being permutation-invariant.
- **Parameter count** — ~ vocab*d_model (embeddings) + N*(attention ~4*d_model^2 + FFN ~8*d_model^2).
- **Training FLOPs** — ~ 6 * N * D (N parameters, D training tokens).
- **Base vs chat model** — same architecture; chat has undergone SFT + preference tuning.
- **What gets updated** — every weight matrix (embeddings, W_q/k/v/o, FFN, norms, LM head) via backprop; activations and the KV cache are *not* parameters.

**Why interviewers ask this**

These questions are the *deliverable* of understanding LLM internals — they're how the understanding is tested. An interviewer can't watch you implement nanoGPT, so instead they ask "walk a token from text to output" and listen for whether every stage is real and correctly ordered, or whether you skip and hand-wave. The flavour questions (strawberry, same-prompt-varies, order-matters) are *diagnostic*: they seem like trivia but each one only has a clean answer if you actually understand the corresponding mechanism (tokenization, sampling, positional encoding). The param-count/FLOPs question checks whether the architecture is concrete to you or just words. Nailing these signals you're an engineer who could reason about, debug, and even build these systems — not just call an API. This topic is deliberately the capstone: it rewards having internalized the whole primer.

**Common confusions**

- "The model reads letters/words" — it reads **subword token IDs**; letters and word boundaries are mostly invisible to it (the strawberry problem).
- "Logits are probabilities" — they're raw scores; you need softmax (with temperature) to get probabilities.
- "Temperature 0 is fully deterministic" — argmax is deterministic in principle, but floating-point non-associativity, batching, and MoE routing can still flip a token.
- "Attention understands word order" — attention itself is order-*agnostic*; **positional encoding** injects order. Without it, "dog bites man" == "man bites dog."
- "Training updates the neurons' activations" — training updates the **weights**; activations/KV-cache are transient and recomputed every forward pass.
- "A bigger context window means it remembers across chats" — context is the current forward pass's input; nothing persists between calls unless you re-supply it (an applied concern; see AI Engineering).
- "Base and chat are different models trained from scratch" — chat is the base model *after* post-training; same architecture, reshaped weights.

**What follows from this topic**

This is the synthesis topic, so it points *back* at all the others: attention questions draw on **Attention** and **Multi-head Attention**; the forward-pass walk draws on **Tokenization**, **Embeddings**, **Positional Encoding**, the **Transformer Block**, and **Sampling & Decoding**; the training questions draw on **Pretraining** and **Training Mechanics**; base-vs-chat draws on **Post-Training: RLHF, DPO & Alignment**; the "why O(n^2)" thread draws on **Efficiency & Modern Architecture**. If a question here feels shaky, that's a pointer to the underlying topic to re-drill. For the *applied* counterparts of these questions (prompt design, context management, cost) consult the sister **AI Engineering** primer — but every answer here should be given from the *mechanism*.

### Q1. Explain attention from scratch, as if to an interviewer.

Attention lets each token gather information from other tokens, weighted by relevance. Each token's vector is projected into three roles:

```text
Q = x @ W_q   # query:  "what am I looking for?"
K = x @ W_k   # key:    "what do I offer?"
V = x @ W_v   # value:  "what I'll pass on if attended to"
```

Score every token against every other with a dot product, scale, softmax into weights, and take a weighted average of Values:

```text
Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax( Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k) ) @ V
```

Walk it: `Q @ K^T` gives an (n, n) matrix of relevance scores (token i's query against token j's key). Divide by `sqrt(d_k)` so scores don't blow up with dimension and saturate softmax. Softmax each row into weights that sum to 1. Multiply by V so each token's output is a blend of the Values it attended to. That's it — a content-based, learned, weighted lookup. "The animal didn't cross the street because **it** was tired": attention lets "it" put weight on "animal." Everything else (multi-head, stacking) is scaling this idea up.

### Q2. Why does a token need a causal mask? What happens without it?

Because the training objective is **predict the next token**. When the model is at position i learning to predict token i+1, it must only use tokens 1..i. If it could see token i+1 (and beyond), predicting it would be trivial *cheating* — it would just copy the answer, learn nothing useful, and then fail completely at inference where the future genuinely doesn't exist yet.

The mask enforces this by setting future scores to -inf *before* softmax, so their weights become 0:

```text
scores[i][j] = -inf   if j > i     # can't look ahead
# after softmax, weight on any future token is exp(-inf) = 0
```

```text
       t1   t2   t3   t4
t1  [  x    .    .    .  ]     x = allowed
t2  [  x    x    .    .  ]     . = masked (-inf)
t3  [  x    x    x    .  ]
t4  [  x    x    x    x  ]
```

Without the mask you'd have *bidirectional* attention (like BERT), which is great for understanding a fixed input but invalid for left-to-right generation — the model would be trained on information it won't have at generation time.

### Q3. Walk a single token all the way from raw text to an output token.

The full pipeline for generating one token:

```text
1. TOKENIZE:   "The cat sat" -> token IDs [464, 3797, 3332]      (subword BPE)
2. EMBED:      each ID -> row of embedding matrix (vocab, d_model)
               -> (seq, d_model); add positional encoding (RoPE/learned)
3. BLOCKS:     x = x + MHA(LN(x)); x = x + FFN(LN(x))   -- repeat N times
               shape stays (seq, d_model) throughout
4. FINAL NORM: LayerNorm/RMSNorm the last-layer output
5. LM HEAD:    x @ W_unembed  (d_model, vocab) -> logits (seq, vocab)
6. LAST POS:   take logits[-1]  -> one score per vocab token
7. SAMPLE:     probs = softmax(logits[-1] / temperature)
               apply top-k / top-p, draw a token, e.g. " on"
8. APPEND:     feed [..., " on"] back in and repeat from step 1 (KV cache reuses past K/V)
```

Key beats to say out loud: embeddings turn IDs into vectors; positional encoding injects order; each block mixes information across positions (attention) then processes each position (FFN); the LM head projects back to vocab; you only use the **last** position's logits to predict the next token; sampling turns scores into a choice; the loop makes it autoregressive. That's a complete, non-hand-wavy answer.

### Q4. Why does the same prompt give different answers — and why can even temperature 0 vary?

**Different answers at normal temperature:** because generation **samples** from a probability distribution rather than always taking the top token. After softmax you have `p(next token)`, and with temperature > 0 plus top-k/top-p you *draw randomly* from it. Different draws -> different tokens -> the sequences diverge (each new token conditions the next), so small early differences cascade into very different outputs.

```text
probs: " on" 0.4, " down" 0.3, " quietly" 0.2, ...
temperature > 0  -> sometimes " on", sometimes " down"
```

**Even at temperature 0** (greedy argmax, "should" be deterministic) you can still see variation because:

- **Floating-point non-associativity**: `(a+b)+c != a+(b+c)` in float; the reduction order in matmuls changes with batch size/GPU/kernel, occasionally flipping which logit is the max.
- **Batching**: your request is batched with others differently across runs, changing those reduction orders.
- **MoE routing**: in Mixture-of-Experts models, expert assignment depends on the batch (capacity limits), so the same token can route differently.

So temperature 0 removes the *deliberate* randomness but not the *systems-level* wobble.

### Q5. Why can't an LLM reliably count the r's in "strawberry"?

Because it never sees the letters. Text is **tokenized** into subword units before the model touches it, and it only ever operates on **token IDs**, not characters:

```text
"strawberry" -> maybe ["straw", "berry"]  (2 tokens, IDs like [7534, 8396])
the model sees [7534, 8396], never s-t-r-a-w-b-e-r-r-y
```

To count r's, the model would have to *know the character spelling* of each token — information that isn't directly in its input. It can sometimes fake it from training data (it may have seen spellings discussed), but there's no reliable character-level mechanism. This is the general **tokenization gap**: any character-level task — counting letters, reversing a string, precise substring manipulation — is hard because the atomic unit is a subword token, not a character. It also explains quirks in arithmetic and non-English text, where tokenization chops things awkwardly. The fix isn't "the model is dumb"; it's "the model literally doesn't see what you're asking it to count."

### Q6. Compute the parameter count of a given transformer config.

Parameters come from three places. For a decoder-only model with vocab V, model dim d, N layers, FFN hidden 4d:

```text
Embeddings:   V * d                     (token embedding; often tied with the LM head)
Per layer:
  Attention:  W_q,W_k,W_v,W_o = 4 * d*d = 4 d^2
  FFN:        W_1 (d->4d) + W_2 (4d->d) = 8 d^2
  -> ~12 d^2 per layer  (norms/biases negligible)
Total:  ~ V*d  +  N * 12 d^2
```

Worked example: V = 50,000, d = 4096, N = 32:

```text
embeddings: 50000 * 4096      = 0.20 B
per layer : 12 * 4096^2       = 0.20 B
layers    : 32 * 0.20 B       = 6.44 B
total     : ~6.6 B parameters
```

Talking points that impress: **most parameters live in the FFN** (8 d^2 of the 12 d^2 per layer); embeddings dominate only for small models with big vocab; the `d^2` scaling is why *width* is expensive; if embeddings are **tied**, the LM head adds no extra params. This shows the architecture is concrete to you, not abstract.

### Q7. Estimate the training FLOPs for that model.

Use the standard rule of thumb: training compute is about

```text
FLOPs ~= 6 * N * D
```

where N = parameters and D = training tokens. The 6 breaks down as ~2 FLOPs per parameter for the forward pass (a multiply + an add per weight) and ~4 for the backward pass (gradients cost roughly twice the forward).

Example: N = 6.6e9 params, trained Chinchilla-optimal at ~20 tokens/param -> D = 20 * 6.6e9 = 1.3e11 tokens:

```text
FLOPs ~= 6 * 6.6e9 * 1.3e11 ~= 5.2e21 FLOPs
```

Sanity-check talking points: **Chinchilla** says compute-optimal is ~20 tokens per parameter, and many early models were *under-trained* (too few tokens for their size). Inference is far cheaper per token (~2N FLOPs, forward only). This `6ND` estimate is what people use to reason about training-run cost and the compute-vs-data trade-off — knowing it signals you think about LLMs at the systems/economics level, not just the API.

### Q8. What actually gets updated during training, and what doesn't?

**Updated (the parameters):** every learnable weight matrix, via backpropagation + the optimizer (AdamW):

```text
- token embedding matrix (vocab, d_model)
- per layer: W_q, W_k, W_v, W_o (attention) ; W_1, W_2 (FFN)
- LayerNorm/RMSNorm scale (and bias) params
- final norm and the LM head / unembedding (often tied to the embedding)
```

One training step: **forward** (compute loss = cross-entropy of predicted vs true next token) -> **backward** (backprop the gradient of the loss w.r.t. every weight) -> **optimizer update** (nudge each weight down its gradient, with AdamW's adaptive step + weight decay).

**Not updated (transient):**

```text
- activations / hidden states  -> recomputed every forward pass
- the KV cache                 -> an inference-time speed cache, no gradients
- attention weights / logits   -> outputs, not parameters
- the input tokens             -> data, not learned
```

The crisp way to say it: "learning" = changing the *weights*; the vectors flowing through the network are just *computed each pass* from those weights and the input. A common junior mistake is thinking the model "updates its activations" or "remembers" the KV cache — it doesn't; those are recomputed.

### Q9. What's the difference between a base model and a chat model?

Same architecture, same parameter shapes — the difference is **post-training**.

| | Base model | Chat model |
|---|---|---|
| Trained by | Pretraining only (next-token on web/code/books) | Base + SFT + preference tuning (RLHF/DPO) |
| Behavior | Completes/continues text | Follows instructions, assists |
| Format | Raw text | Chat template (system/user/assistant turns) |
| Stopping | Rambles on | Emits a stop token, bounded answers |
| Safety | None inherent | Refusals, alignment (HHH) |

```text
"How do I reverse a list in Python?"
base: might continue with more questions or a tangent (plausible-internet-continuation)
chat: gives a direct, bounded, helpful answer, then stops
```

The chat model *is* the base model after its weights were reshaped by SFT (instruction-following + chat template) and preference optimization (helpful/honest/harmless). No architecture change, no from-scratch retraining — just further training that concentrates probability mass on assistant-style responses. This is the payoff of the Post-Training topic, restated as an interview one-liner.

### Q10. Why does word order matter if attention is a set operation?

Attention *by itself* is **permutation-invariant**: it computes a weighted average of Values based on content, with no notion of position. Shuffle the input tokens and (ignoring the mask) you'd get the same set of outputs, just reordered — so "dog bites man" and "man bites dog" would be indistinguishable. That's clearly wrong for language.

The fix is **positional encoding**: inject position information so tokens at different positions get different representations.

```text
attention alone:  out_i = sum_j softmax(Q_i . K_j) V_j   -- depends on content, not position
add positions:    x_i = embed(token_i) + pos(i)          -- now position is in the vector
```

Methods: **sinusoidal** (fixed sin/cos of position), **learned** absolute embeddings, **RoPE** (rotate Q/K by angle proportional to position — encodes *relative* position and extrapolates), **ALiBi** (bias scores by distance). RoPE dominates modern models. The key insight to state: attention provides the *mixing*, positional encoding provides the *order*; you need both. Without positions the transformer is a bag-of-words mixer.

### Q11. Why divide by sqrt(d_k) in the attention score?

To keep the softmax in a healthy range. The score is a dot product of a Query and Key over `d_k` dimensions:

```text
score = Q . K = sum over d_k dimensions of (q_i * k_i)
```

If the components are roughly independent with unit variance, this sum has variance ~ `d_k`, so its magnitude grows like `sqrt(d_k)`. For large `d_k` (say 64 or 128), raw scores get large in magnitude, and large inputs push **softmax into saturation** — one weight near 1, the rest near 0. Saturated softmax has **near-zero gradients**, so learning stalls (vanishing gradients).

Dividing by `sqrt(d_k)` normalizes the variance back to ~1, keeping scores in a range where softmax is smooth and gradients flow:

```text
scaled = (Q @ K^T) / sqrt(d_k)   # variance ~ 1, softmax not saturated
```

So it's not arbitrary — it's a variance-control trick that keeps the model trainable. A strong answer names the mechanism (dot-product variance grows with dimension -> softmax saturation -> dead gradients) rather than just reciting the formula.

### Q12. Why pre-LN instead of post-LN in modern transformers?

It's about where the normalization sits relative to the residual connection.

```text
post-LN (original):  x = LayerNorm( x + Sublayer(x) )
pre-LN (modern):     x = x + Sublayer( LayerNorm(x) )
```

In **post-LN**, the norm is *on* the residual path, so it repeatedly rescales the accumulated signal; gradients through a deep stack can explode or vanish, and training often needs careful learning-rate **warmup** and is finicky at depth. In **pre-LN**, the normalization is *inside* the residual branch, leaving a **clean identity highway** (`x + ...`) straight through every layer. Gradients flow through that unnormalized skip path largely intact, so very deep models (dozens to 100+ layers) train stably with less warmup sensitivity.

```text
pre-LN:  x ------------------------------> (+) -->   clean gradient highway
            \-> LN -> Sublayer ----------/
```

The trade-off: pre-LN can leave the final output slightly less normalized (usually handled by a final norm before the LM head). Net: pre-LN's training stability at depth is why nearly all modern LLMs use it. Being able to draw the residual highway and explain the gradient argument is the senior signal here.

### Q13. Are logits probabilities? How do you turn them into a token?

**No — logits are raw, unnormalized scores**, one per vocabulary entry. They can be any real number, positive or negative, and don't sum to anything. You convert them to a probability distribution and then draw:

```text
logits[-1]                    # (vocab,) raw scores at the last position
probs = softmax(logits / T)   # temperature T shapes the distribution
```

- **Temperature T**: divides logits before softmax. T > 1 flattens (more random), T < 1 sharpens (more confident), T -> 0 approaches greedy **argmax**.
- **top-k**: keep only the k highest-prob tokens, renormalize, sample.
- **top-p (nucleus)**: keep the smallest set whose cumulative prob >= p, renormalize, sample.
- **greedy**: just take argmax (deterministic).

```text
greedy   : pick the single highest logit
top-k=50 : sample among the 50 most likely
top-p=0.9: sample among the top tokens covering 90% of mass
```

The one-liner to land: **softmax turns scores into probabilities; temperature and top-k/top-p control the shape and truncation of that distribution; then you sample (or argmax) one token.** Confusing logits with probabilities is a classic tell that someone hasn't looked at the last stage of the forward pass.

### Q14. Why is attention O(n^2), and what fixes it in production?

Every token attends to every token, so the score matrix is n×n — both compute and memory scale as **n^2** in sequence length. Double the context, quadruple the attention cost.

```text
Q @ K^T -> (n, n) scores  ->  O(n^2) compute and O(n^2) memory
```

The production fixes, by what they attack:

- **FlashAttention** — keeps attention *exact* but tiles the computation so the n×n matrix never hits slow HBM; kills the memory-traffic bottleneck.
- **Sliding-window / local attention** — each token attends to only the last W tokens -> O(n·W), linear; stacked layers still propagate info far.
- **GQA / MQA** — attack the *KV cache* side of the cost (share K/V across heads) to make long context affordable at decode.
- **RoPE scaling + attention sinks** — make long context *usable* (positions extrapolate, streaming stays stable).

```text
O(n^2) compute -> FlashAttention (exact), sliding-window (approx-sparse)
KV cache memory -> GQA/MQA, quantization
```

The framing that impresses: "O(n^2) is *the* reason modern LLM architecture looks different from vanilla GPT — nearly every trick is a response to it or to the KV cache it forces." This directly bridges to the Efficiency topic.

### Q15. How would you explain a transformer to an interviewer in two minutes without hand-waving?

A tight, mechanism-first script:

"An LLM is a **next-token predictor**: given all previous tokens, it outputs a probability distribution over the vocabulary for the next one. Here's the pipeline. **Tokenize** text into subword IDs. **Embed** each ID into a vector via a learned matrix, and **add positional encoding** so order matters — because attention alone is order-agnostic. Then stack **N transformer blocks**. Each block has two sublayers, each wrapped in a residual connection and a norm: **multi-head attention**, where every token forms a Query, Key, and Value, scores relevance with `softmax(Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k))`, and takes a weighted average of Values — this *mixes information across positions*, with a **causal mask** so a token can't see the future; and a **position-wise FFN** that processes each token independently and holds most of the parameters. After the blocks, a **final norm** and an **LM head** project back to vocabulary **logits**. **Softmax** the last position's logits into probabilities, **sample** a token, append it, and repeat — that's autoregressive generation, sped up by a **KV cache**. Training is just cross-entropy on next-token prediction over trillions of tokens; post-training (SFT + RLHF/DPO) turns the base completer into an assistant."

```text
tokens -> embed + position -> [attention (mix) -> FFN (process)] x N
       -> norm -> LM head -> logits -> sample -> loop
```

Hitting every stage in order, naming the two sublayers, the mask, and the objective, is what "no hand-waving" means.

### Q16. If the transformer is so powerful, why does it hallucinate and fail at simple facts?

Because its objective is **plausibility, not truth**. It was trained to predict the most likely next token given context — to produce text that *looks like* its training data — with no built-in mechanism for verifying facts against a source. So it generates the most *probable-sounding* continuation, which is usually correct for well-represented facts and confidently wrong for rare or absent ones.

```text
objective: maximize p(next token | context)   # fluency/plausibility
NOT:       "retrieve and verify a true fact"
```

Concrete drivers:

- **No ground-truth lookup**: facts are smeared across weights (largely in the FFN "memories"), lossily. Rare facts are weakly encoded -> confabulation.
- **Tokenization**: character/arithmetic tasks fail because it sees subword tokens, not letters/digits (the strawberry problem, Q5).
- **Sampling**: it commits to a token and continues; there's no backtracking to check consistency.
- **Sycophancy/alignment side effects**: preference tuning can reward confident, agreeable answers over "I don't know" (Post-Training topic).

The honest framing: hallucination isn't a bug bolted on — it's the *flip side* of a fluency-maximizing next-token predictor with no verification step. Mitigations (retrieval/RAG, tools, grounding) live in the applied **AI Engineering** layer; the *internal* reason is that the model optimizes likelihood, not correctness.
